Getting Started

Introduction to Java

A statically typed, object-oriented, garbage-collected language that compiles to bytecode and runs on any machine with a JVM — “write once, run anywhere”.

Java — a general-purpose language (Sun Microsystems, 1995; now Oracle + the OpenJDK community) whose source is compiled to platform-neutral bytecode and executed by the Java Virtual Machine, which interprets it and JIT-compiles hot code to native machine code.

Why Java?

Platform independent

The same .class files run on Windows, Linux and macOS — only the JVM is platform-specific.

Statically typed

Types are checked at compile time, so whole classes of bugs never reach production. Great IDE support and refactoring.

Automatic memory

No free/delete: the garbage collector reclaims unreachable objects. No dangling pointers, no pointer arithmetic.

Huge ecosystem

Spring, Hibernate, Android, Kafka, Hadoop/Spark, Elasticsearch, Minecraft… plus Maven Central with millions of libraries.

Fast

The JIT (HotSpot C1/C2) optimises the code that actually runs hot, often reaching C++-like throughput for long-running servers.

Backward compatible

Code written for Java 1.4 still compiles today. A new version ships every 6 months; an LTS every 2 years (8, 11, 17, 21, 25).

Key features (the classic “buzzwords”)
FeatureWhat it means in practice
Object-orientedEverything lives inside a class; encapsulation, inheritance, polymorphism, abstraction.
SimpleNo pointers, no operator overloading, no multiple class inheritance, no header files.
RobustStrong typing, exceptions, bounds-checked arrays, GC.
SecureBytecode verification, no raw memory access, sandboxable class loaders.
MultithreadedThreads, locks and concurrent collections built into the language and library.
Architecture-neutralFixed sizes for primitives: an int is 32 bits everywhere.
Hello, World
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("Hello, Java!");
        System.out.println("Running on Java " + Runtime.version().feature());
    }
}
Output
Hello, Java!
Running on Java 21
Terminal
$ javac Main.java        # compile → Main.class (bytecode)
$ java Main              # run the class on the JVM
$ java Main.java         # Java 11+: compile in memory and run in one step
$ jshell                 # Java 9+: interactive REPL
Where Java is used
Enterprise back ends (Spring Boot)Android (with Kotlin)Big data (Hadoop, Spark, Kafka)Banking & trading systemsSearch (Elasticsearch, Solr)Build tools (Maven, Gradle)Games (Minecraft)
Common confusion

Java ≠ JavaScript. The names are a 1995 marketing coincidence; the languages are unrelated.

Why is Java called platform independent?

Because javac produces bytecode, not machine code. Any OS that has a JVM can run that bytecode unchanged. The JVM itself is platform dependent — there is a different JVM build for each OS/CPU.

Is Java a purely object-oriented language?

No. It has primitive types (int, double, boolean…) that are not objects, and static members that belong to no object. Wrapper classes (Integer…) bridge primitives into the object world.

Is Java compiled or interpreted?

Both. Source is compiled to bytecode by javac; the JVM then interprets bytecode and JIT-compiles frequently executed methods to native code at run time.

Getting Started

JDK, JRE & JVM

Three nested boxes: the JDK (tools to build) contains the JRE (what's needed to run) which contains the JVM (the engine that executes bytecode).

JDK = JRE + development tools (javac, jar, javadoc, jdb, jshell…)
JRE = JVM + class libraries (java.lang, java.util, …)
What each one is

JVM — Java Virtual Machine

An abstract machine that loads, verifies and executes bytecode. Provides memory management (GC), threads and the JIT compiler. Specification + implementation (HotSpot, OpenJ9, GraalVM).

JRE — Java Runtime Environment

The JVM plus the standard library (rt.jar before Java 9, modules since). Enough to run programs, not compile them.

JDK — Java Development Kit

The JRE plus developer tools: javac, java, jar, javadoc, jdb, jshell, jcmd, jlink… What developers install.

JDKJREJVM
PurposeDevelop + runRunExecute bytecode
Contains javac?YesNoNo
Contains class library?YesYesNo
Platform dependent?YesYesYes
Modern reality

Since Java 11, Oracle no longer ships a separate JRE download. You install a JDK (e.g. Eclipse Temurin, Amazon Corretto, Oracle JDK) and, for deployment, build a trimmed runtime with jlink.

Inside the JVM
1
Class loader subsystem
Finds .class files, loads them, links (verify → prepare → resolve) and initialises them.
2
Runtime data areas
Heap (objects), Method Area / Metaspace (class metadata), one Stack per thread (frames with local variables), PC registers, native method stacks.
3
Execution engine
Interpreter + JIT compiler (C1 quick, C2 optimising) + Garbage Collector.
4
JNI / native libraries
Bridge to C/C++ code (native methods).
Checking what you have
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("feature version: " + Runtime.version().feature());
        System.out.println("spec version:    " + System.getProperty("java.specification.version"));
        System.out.println("class file ok:   " + (Main.class.getName().equals("Main")));
    }
}
Output
feature version: 21
spec version:    21
class file ok:   true
Terminal
$ java -version          # runtime version
$ javac -version         # compiler version (only in a JDK)
$ echo $JAVA_HOME        # where the JDK is installed
OpenJDK distributions

All major builds come from the same OpenJDK source; they differ in licensing, support length and extras.

Eclipse TemurinAmazon CorrettoOracle JDK / Oracle OpenJDKAzul ZuluMicrosoft Build of OpenJDKGraalVM
What is the difference between JDK, JRE and JVM?

The JVM executes bytecode. The JRE = JVM + standard libraries — enough to run Java apps. The JDK = JRE + development tools like javac, jar, javadoc — needed to write and compile Java.

Is the JVM platform independent?

No — the JVM is written for a specific OS and CPU. Bytecode is platform independent precisely because every platform has its own JVM.

What is the JIT compiler?

The Just-In-Time compiler inside the JVM. It profiles running code and compiles hot methods (called often, loops) from bytecode to optimised native machine code, with tricks like inlining and escape analysis. That's why Java programs “warm up”.

Getting Started

Compilation, Bytecode & Class Loading

javac turns each class into a .class file of bytecode; the JVM loads classes lazily, on first use, through a chain of class loaders.

Main.java
→
javac
→
Main.class (bytecode)
→
Class loader
→
Bytecode verifier
→
Interpreter / JIT
→
Native CPU
Bytecode

Bytecode — the instruction set of the JVM — a compact, stack-based code stored in .class files. Each class (including nested and anonymous classes) gets its own .class file, e.g. Outer$Inner.class.

You can read the bytecode with javap -c:

Terminal
$ cat Add.java
class Add { static int add(int a, int b) { return a + b; } }
$ javac Add.java && javap -c Add
  static int add(int, int);
    Code:
       0: iload_0        // push local 0 (a)
       1: iload_1        // push local 1 (b)
       2: iadd           // pop two ints, push their sum
       3: ireturn
Class loading: load → link → initialise
1
Loading
A class loader finds the bytes (from the module image, a JAR, the file system, the network…) and creates a Class object.
2
Linking — verify
The bytecode verifier checks the code is type-safe and can't corrupt the stack or memory.
3
Linking — prepare
Static fields are allocated and set to default values (0, null, false).
4
Linking — resolve
Symbolic references (names of other classes, methods) become direct references (may be lazy).
5
Initialisation
Static initialisers and static field assignments run, top to bottom — exactly once, on first active use.
The class loader hierarchy
LoaderLoadsNotes
BootstrapCore modules: java.base (java.lang, java.util…)Written in native code; appears as null from Java
Platform (was Extension)Other Java SE / JDK modules (java.sql…)Parent is bootstrap
Application (System)Your classes from the class path / module pathParent is platform
CustomAnything you want (plugins, hot reload, app servers)Extend ClassLoader
Key point

Parent delegation model: a loader first asks its parent to load a class, and only loads it itself if the parent can't. So nobody can replace java.lang.String with their own version.

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(String.class.getClassLoader());              // bootstrap → null
        System.out.println(java.sql.Date.class.getClassLoader().getName());
        ClassLoader app = ClassLoader.getSystemClassLoader();
        System.out.println(app.getName() + " -> parent " + app.getParent().getName());
    }
}
Output
null
platform
app -> parent platform
Lazy initialisation — classes load on first use
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("main starts");
        System.out.println(Config.NAME);     // compile-time constant: inlined, no init!
        System.out.println(Config.port);     // first active use → static block runs
        System.out.println(Config.port);     // already initialised
    }
}

class Config {
    static final String NAME = "app";       // constant — copied into the caller
    static int port = 8080;
    static { System.out.println("Config initialised"); }
}
Output
main starts
app
Config initialised
8080
8080
Watch out

static final fields initialised with a compile-time constant ("app", 42) are copied into the calling class at compile time. Reading them does not initialise the declaring class — and changing the constant requires recompiling the callers.

What triggers class initialisation?

An active use: creating an instance (new), calling a static method, reading/writing a non-constant static field, reflection like Class.forName("X"), initialising a subclass (parent first), or it being the main class. Merely declaring a variable of that type or reading a compile-time constant does not.

What is the parent delegation model and why does it exist?

Each class loader delegates a load request to its parent first. It guarantees core classes are always loaded by the bootstrap loader (security — you can't shadow java.lang.Object) and that a class is loaded only once per hierarchy.

ClassNotFoundException vs NoClassDefFoundError?

ClassNotFoundException is a checked exception when code explicitly loads a class by name (Class.forName, loadClass) and it isn't found. NoClassDefFoundError is an error when a class that was present at compile time is missing (or failed static initialisation) at run time.

Getting Started

Program Structure & main()

A Java program is a set of classes; execution starts at public static void main(String[] args) of the class you launch.

Anatomy of a source file
Java
package com.example.app;           // 1. optional package (must be first)

import java.util.List;             // 2. imports

public class Main {                // 3. at most ONE public top-level class, named like the file
    static final int LIMIT = 3;    //    fields

    public static void main(String[] args) {   // entry point
        List<String> names = List.of("Ada", "Linus", "Grace");
        for (int i = 0; i < LIMIT; i++) {
            greet(names.get(i));
        }
    }

    static void greet(String name) {            // methods
        System.out.println("Hi, " + name);
    }
}
Output
Hi, Ada
Hi, Linus
Hi, Grace
Dissecting main
PartWhy
publicThe JVM calls it from outside the class.
staticNo object exists yet — the JVM calls it on the class itself.
voidReturns nothing; use System.exit(code) to set an exit status.
mainThe name the launcher looks for.
String[] argsCommand-line arguments (also String... args). Never null; empty if none.
Java — java Main.java hello 42
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("count = " + args.length);
        for (String a : args) System.out.println("arg: " + a);
    }
}
Output
count = 2
arg: hello
arg: 42
Newer Java

Java 21 previews unnamed classes and instance main methods (void main() { … }), finalised later as “compact source files” in Java 25. For interviews and production, the classic public static void main(String[] args) is the one to know.

Rules and conventions
  • One public top-level class per file, and the file must be named after it: Main → Main.java.
  • Every statement ends with ;; blocks use { }. Java is case-sensitive (Main ≠ main).
  • Naming: PascalCase classes/interfaces, camelCase methods/variables, UPPER_SNAKE_CASE constants, lowercase.dotted packages (reversed domain: com.example.app).
  • Comments: // line, /* block */, /** Javadoc */ (turned into HTML API docs by javadoc).
  • java.lang (String, Math, System, Integer…) is imported automatically.
Console output & input
Java
import java.util.Scanner;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Scanner in = new Scanner(System.in);
        String name = in.next();
        int age = in.nextInt();
        System.out.print("No newline, ");
        System.out.println("then newline");
        System.out.printf("%s is %d years old (%.2f decades)%n", name, age, age / 10.0);
        System.out.println(String.format("[%5d] [%-5s] [%05d]", 42, "ab", 42));
    }
}
Output
No newline, then newline
Ravi is 27 years old (2.70 decades)
[   42] [ab   ] [00042]
FormatMeaning
%d / %s / %c / %binteger / string / char / boolean
%f, %.2f, %efloating point, 2 decimals, scientific
%5d, %-5s, %05dwidth 5 right-aligned, left-aligned, zero-padded
%x, %ohexadecimal, octal
%nplatform line separator (prefer over \n in printf)
Can we overload main? Can main be private or non-static?

You can overload main (e.g. main(int x)), but the JVM only calls public static void main(String[]). If it's private or non-static, the class compiles but the classic launcher reports that no valid main method was found.

Why is main static?

So the JVM can call it without creating an instance first — at start-up there is no object to call it on, and the JVM wouldn't know which constructor to use.

Can a source file have no public class?

Yes. Then the file can have any name, and all its top-level classes are package-private.

Getting Started

Primitive Types & Variables

Java has exactly 8 primitive types with fixed sizes on every platform; everything else is a reference to an object.

The 8 primitives
TypeSizeRange / valuesDefaultLiteral
byte8 bit−128 … 1270(byte) 10
short16 bit−32,768 … 32,7670(short) 10
int32 bit−2³¹ … 2³¹−1 (≈ ±2.1 billion)042, 0x2A, 0b101010, 1_000_000
long64 bit−2⁶³ … 2⁶³−1 (≈ ±9.2 × 10¹⁸)0L42L
float32 bitIEEE 754, ~7 significant digits0.0f3.14f
double64 bitIEEE 754, ~15–16 significant digits0.0d3.14, 1e-9
char16 bitUTF-16 code unit '\u0000' … '\uffff' (unsigned)'\u0000''A', '\n', '\u0041'
booleanJVM-dependenttrue / false (not 0/1!)falsetrue
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(Byte.MIN_VALUE + " " + Byte.MAX_VALUE);
        System.out.println(Integer.MAX_VALUE + " " + Long.MAX_VALUE);
        System.out.println((int) Character.MAX_VALUE + " " + Character.SIZE + " bits");
        int million = 1_000_000;                   // underscores for readability
        System.out.println(million + " " + 0xFF + " " + 0b1010 + " " + 017);
        char c = 'A';
        System.out.println(c + 1);                 // char promoted to int
        System.out.println((char) (c + 1));
    }
}
Output
-128 127
2147483647 9223372036854775807
65535 16 bits
1000000 255 10 15
66
B
Watch out

A leading 0 makes an octal literal: 017 is 15, and 09 doesn't compile.

Variables: local, instance, static
KindDeclaredLives inDefault value?
LocalInside a method/blockStack frameNo — must be assigned before use (compile error otherwise)
Instance (field)In the class, no staticInside each object on the heapYes (0, false, null)
Static (class variable)static in the classOnce per classYes
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int x;
        System.out.println(x);   // local variable never assigned
    }
}
Compiler error
Main.java:4: error: variable x might not have been initialized
        System.out.println(x);   // local variable never assigned
                           ^
1 error
var — local type inference (Java 10+)
Java
import java.util.ArrayList;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var count = 10;                       // int
        var price = 9.99;                     // double
        var names = new ArrayList<String>();  // ArrayList<String>
        names.add("Ada");
        for (var n : names) System.out.println(n + " " + count + " " + price);
    }
}
Output
Ada 10 9.99
  • Still static typing — the compiler infers the type once; it can't change later.
  • Only for local variables with an initialiser (and loop variables). Not for fields, parameters or return types.
  • var x = null; and var a = {1, 2}; don't compile — there's nothing to infer from.
Integer overflow & floating-point surprises
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int big = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
        System.out.println(big + 1);                   // wraps around silently
        long ok = big + 1L;                            // do the math in long
        System.out.println(ok);
        System.out.println(1_000_000 * 1_000_000);     // int * int overflows
        System.out.println(1_000_000L * 1_000_000);
        try {
            Math.addExact(big, 1);
        } catch (ArithmeticException e) {
            System.out.println("addExact: " + e.getMessage());
        }
        System.out.println(0.1 + 0.2);
        System.out.println(0.1f + 0.2f);
        System.out.println(1.0 / 0 + " " + -1.0 / 0 + " " + 0.0 / 0);
    }
}
Output
-2147483648
2147483648
-727379968
1000000000000
addExact: integer overflow
0.30000000000000004
0.3
Infinity -Infinity NaN
Watch out

Integer overflow is silent in Java. Use long, Math.addExact/multiplyExact (which throw), or BigInteger. For money, never use double — use BigDecimal or integer cents.

Java
import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.math.BigInteger;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(new BigDecimal("0.1").add(new BigDecimal("0.2")));
        System.out.println(new BigDecimal(0.1));    // the double's exact binary value!
        BigInteger f = BigInteger.ONE;
        for (int i = 2; i <= 25; i++) f = f.multiply(BigInteger.valueOf(i));
        System.out.println("25! = " + f);
    }
}
Output
0.3
0.1000000000000000055511151231257827021181583404541015625
25! = 15511210043330985984000000
Why is char 16 bits in Java?

Java was designed around Unicode, and in 1995 Unicode fit in 16 bits. A char is a UTF-16 code unit; characters outside the Basic Multilingual Plane (e.g. many emoji) need two chars (a surrogate pair).

What are default values, and do local variables get them?

Fields and array elements get defaults (0, 0.0, false, '\u0000', null). Local variables do not — the compiler rejects reading one before it's definitely assigned.

Does var make Java dynamically typed?

No. var is compile-time inference only; the variable has one fixed static type, exactly as if you had written it out.

Getting Started

Wrapper Classes, Autoboxing & the Integer Cache

Each primitive has an immutable wrapper class so it can live where objects are required (collections, generics, null). The compiler converts between them automatically — sometimes with surprising results.

PrimitiveWrapperCache (valueOf)
byteByteall values
shortShort−128 … 127
intInteger−128 … 127 (upper bound tunable)
longLong−128 … 127
charCharacter0 … 127
booleanBooleanTRUE / FALSE
float / doubleFloat / Doublenone

Autoboxing / unboxing — the compiler's automatic conversion int → Integer (it inserts Integer.valueOf(x)) and Integer → int (it inserts x.intValue()).

Java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>();
        list.add(5);                  // autoboxing: Integer.valueOf(5)
        int first = list.get(0);      // unboxing: list.get(0).intValue()
        Integer total = 0;
        for (int i = 1; i <= 3; i++) total += i;   // unbox, add, box again each time
        System.out.println(first + " " + total);
        System.out.println(Integer.parseInt("123") + 1);       // String → int
        System.out.println(Integer.valueOf("123").getClass().getSimpleName());
        System.out.println(Integer.toBinaryString(10) + " " + Integer.toHexString(255));
        System.out.println(Character.isDigit('7') + " " + Character.toUpperCase('q'));
    }
}
Output
5 6
124
Integer
1010 ff
true Q
The Integer cache trap

Integer.valueOf returns cached objects for −128…127. == on wrappers compares references, so it “works” for small numbers and breaks for bigger ones.

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Integer a = 127, b = 127;
        Integer c = 128, d = 128;
        System.out.println(a == b);          // same cached object
        System.out.println(c == d);          // two different objects
        System.out.println(c.equals(d));     // compare values
        System.out.println(c.intValue() == d);  // one side primitive → unboxes → value compare
    }
}
Output
true
false
true
true
Watch out

Never compare wrapper objects with ==. Use equals, or unbox first. (new Integer(5) is deprecated for removal precisely because it bypasses the cache.)

NullPointerException from unboxing
Java
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Map;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Map<String, Integer> stock = new HashMap<>();
        stock.put("apple", 3);
        try {
            int bananas = stock.get("banana");   // get → null → null.intValue()
            System.out.println(bananas);
        } catch (NullPointerException e) {
            System.out.println("NPE while unboxing");
        }
        int safe = stock.getOrDefault("banana", 0);
        System.out.println(safe);
    }
}
Output
NPE while unboxing
0
Performance cost
  • Each boxed value is a separate heap object (~16 bytes vs 4 for an int), plus the reference.
  • Summing into a Long inside a loop creates millions of garbage objects — use a primitive long.
  • Prefer int[] over List<Integer> and IntStream over Stream<Integer> for heavy numeric work.
What does Integer a = 1000, b = 1000; a == b print, and why?

false (by default). Autoboxing calls Integer.valueOf, which only caches −128…127, so 1000 creates two distinct objects and == compares references. With 127 it prints true.

Why do we need wrapper classes?

Generics and collections work only with objects (List<int> is illegal), wrappers can be null ("no value"), and they carry utility methods (parseInt, MAX_VALUE, compare, toBinaryString).

Can the Integer cache range be changed?

The upper bound can be raised with the JVM flag -XX:AutoBoxCacheMax=<n> (or java.lang.Integer.IntegerCache.high). The lower bound is fixed at −128.

Getting Started

Type Conversion & Casting

Widening conversions happen automatically; narrowing ones need an explicit cast and may silently lose information.

Widening (implicit) vs narrowing (explicit)
byte → short → int → long → float → double (char → int)
WideningNarrowing
DirectionSmaller → larger typeLarger → smaller type
SyntaxAutomatic: long l = i;Cast required: int i = (int) l;
Data loss?No (except precision: long/int → float/double)Possibly: high bits dropped, fraction truncated
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int i = 100;
        long l = i;              // widening
        double d = l;            // widening
        System.out.println(l + " " + d);

        double price = 9.99;
        int whole = (int) price;         // truncates toward zero, not rounds
        System.out.println(whole + " " + (int) -9.99 + " " + Math.round(9.5));

        int big = 300;
        byte b = (byte) big;             // keeps only the low 8 bits: 300 - 256
        System.out.println(b);
        System.out.println((int) 3_000_000_000L);   // high 32 bits dropped
        System.out.println((int) 1e20);             // double → int saturates
        System.out.println((char) 97 + " " + (int) 'a');

        long precise = 123_456_789_123L;
        float f = precise;                          // widening but loses precision
        System.out.println((long) f);
    }
}
Output
100 100.0
9 -9 10
44
-1294967296
2147483647
a 97
123456790528
Numeric promotion in expressions
  • In arithmetic, byte, short and char are first promoted to int.
  • If either operand is long → long; float → float; double → double.
  • So byte + byte is an int and must be cast back — but compound assignments (+=) cast implicitly.
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        byte a = 10, b = 20;
        // byte c = a + b;          // compile error: int cannot be converted to byte
        byte c = (byte) (a + b);
        a += 5;                     // OK: means a = (byte) (a + 5)
        byte x = 120;
        x += 10;                    // silently overflows!
        System.out.println(c + " " + a + " " + x);
        System.out.println(5 / 2 + " " + 5 / 2.0 + " " + (double) 5 / 2 + " " + (double) (5 / 2));
        System.out.println('a' + 'b' + " vs " + "" + 'a' + 'b');
    }
}
Output
30 15 -126
2 2.5 2.5 2.0
195 vs ab
Watch out

(double) (5 / 2) is 2.0 — the integer division happens before the cast. Cast an operand, not the result.

Converting to and from String
ConversionHow
String → int / doubleInteger.parseInt(s), Double.parseDouble(s) (throw NumberFormatException)
String → wrapperInteger.valueOf(s)
anything → StringString.valueOf(x), Integer.toString(n), "" + x
char ↔ digitc - '0', (char) ('0' + d), Character.getNumericValue(c)
radixInteger.parseInt("ff", 16), Integer.toString(255, 2)
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(Integer.parseInt("42") * 2);
        System.out.println(String.valueOf(3.5) + "!");
        System.out.println('7' - '0' + 1);
        System.out.println(Integer.parseInt("ff", 16) + " " + Integer.toString(10, 2));
        try {
            Integer.parseInt("4 2");
        } catch (NumberFormatException e) {
            System.out.println(e.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
Output
84
3.5!
8
255 1010
For input string: "4 2"
Reference casting (preview)

Objects are cast along the inheritance tree: upcasting (Object o = "hi";) is implicit and always safe; downcasting (String s = (String) o;) is checked at run time and throws ClassCastException if wrong. Use instanceof pattern matching — see Polymorphism.

What is the result of (byte) 200?

-56. Only the low 8 bits are kept: 200 is 11001000 in binary, which as a signed two's-complement byte is 200 − 256 = −56.

Why does short s = 1; s = s + 1; fail but s += 1; compile?

s + 1 is promoted to int, and assigning an int to a short needs an explicit cast. Compound assignment operators include an implicit cast back to the left-hand type.

Is converting long to float widening? Can it lose data?

Yes, it's a widening conversion (allowed implicitly), but float has only 24 bits of mantissa, so large long values lose precision.

Getting Started

Operators & Precedence

Java's operators are C-like, with a few Java-specific twists: >>>, string +, instanceof, and strict boolean conditions.

KindOperatorsNotes
Arithmetic+ - * / %Integer / truncates toward zero; % takes the sign of the dividend
Unary+ - ++ -- !, ~Prefix ++i increments then yields; postfix i++ yields then increments
Relational== != < <= > >=== on objects compares references
Logical&& || !Short-circuit; operands must be boolean
Bitwise& | ^ ~On boolean operands &/| evaluate both sides
Shift<< >> >>>>> keeps the sign, >>> fills with zeros
Assignment= += -= *= /= %= &= |= ^= <<= >>= >>>=Compound forms cast implicitly
Ternarycond ? a : bAn expression — yields a value
Typeinstanceofobj instanceof String s binds a variable (Java 16+)
Arithmetic details
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(7 / 2 + " " + -7 / 2 + " " + 7 % 3 + " " + -7 % 3);
        System.out.println(Math.floorDiv(-7, 2) + " " + Math.floorMod(-7, 3));
        System.out.println(5.5 % 2);
        int i = 5;
        int a = i++;          // a = 5, then i = 6
        int b = ++i;          // i = 7, then b = 7
        System.out.println(a + " " + b + " " + i);
        String s = "Sum: " + 1 + 2;        // left to right: string concat
        String t = 1 + 2 + " is the sum";  // 3 first, then concat
        System.out.println(s + " | " + t);
    }
}
Output
3 -3 1 -1
-4 2
1.5
5 7 7
Sum: 12 | 3 is the sum
Watch out

i = i++; leaves i unchanged: the old value is saved, i is incremented, then the saved old value is assigned back. A classic trick question — never write it.

Short-circuit evaluation
Java
public class Main {
    static boolean check(String label, boolean value) {
        System.out.println("evaluating " + label);
        return value;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String s = null;
        if (s != null && s.length() > 3) System.out.println("long");   // safe: right side skipped
        System.out.println(check("A", false) && check("B", true));
        System.out.println(check("C", false) & check("D", true));       // non-short-circuit
    }
}
Output
evaluating A
false
evaluating C
evaluating D
false
Bitwise & shift operators
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int a = 0b1100, b = 0b1010;
        System.out.println((a & b) + " " + (a | b) + " " + (a ^ b) + " " + ~a);
        System.out.println((1 << 10) + " " + (-16 >> 2) + " " + (-16 >>> 28));
        int n = 12;
        System.out.println("even? " + ((n & 1) == 0));
        System.out.println("power of two? " + (n > 0 && (n & (n - 1)) == 0));
        System.out.println("set bits: " + Integer.bitCount(n));
        int x = 3, y = 9;
        x ^= y; y ^= x; x ^= y;                  // XOR swap
        System.out.println(x + " " + y);
    }
}
Output
8 14 6 -13
1024 -4 15
even? true
power of two? false
set bits: 2
9 3

Assignment= += -= … (right-associative)
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(2 + 3 * 4);
        System.out.println((2 + 3) * 4);
        System.out.println(1 + 2 << 1);           // + binds tighter than <<
        System.out.println(true || false && false);   // && before ||
        int score = 72;
        String grade = score >= 90 ? "A" : score >= 70 ? "B" : "C";
        System.out.println(grade);
    }
}
Output
14
20
6
true
B
Tip

When in doubt, add parentheses. Readers shouldn't need the precedence table.

What's the difference between & and &&?

&& is logical AND with short-circuiting — the right side isn't evaluated if the left is false. & on booleans evaluates both sides; on integers it is bitwise AND.

What is >>>?

The unsigned right shift: it shifts in zeros from the left, regardless of sign. >> is the arithmetic shift, which copies the sign bit. For negative numbers they differ: -16 >> 2 is -4, -16 >>> 28 is 15.

What does System.out.println(1 + 2 + "3" + 4 + 5) print?

3345. Evaluation is left to right: 1 + 2 = 3, then 3 + "3" = "33", and from then on every + is string concatenation.

Core Language: Strings, Arrays & Methods

Strings, Immutability & the String Pool

String is an immutable object, not a primitive. Literals are interned in the string pool, so == sometimes “works” — and that is exactly the trap.

String — a final class holding an immutable sequence of UTF-16 characters (since Java 9 stored compactly as byte[] + a coder flag: Latin-1 or UTF-16). Every “modifying” method returns a new String.

Creating strings
FormWhere it livesNotes
String a = "hi";String pool (interned)Identical literals in the whole JVM share one object
String b = new String("hi");A new heap objectAlways a distinct object — almost never what you want
"h" + "i"PoolConstant expression — folded to "hi" by the compiler
s1 + s2 (non-final variables)New heap objectBuilt at runtime (via StringConcatFactory since Java 9)
b.intern()PoolReturns the pooled copy (adds it if missing)
== vs equals()
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String a = "java";
        String b = "java";
        String c = new String("java");
        String d = "ja" + "va";          // compile-time constant → pooled
        String part = "ja";
        String e = part + "va";          // runtime concatenation → new object

        System.out.println(a == b);          // same pooled object
        System.out.println(a == c);          // different objects
        System.out.println(a.equals(c));     // same characters
        System.out.println(a == d);
        System.out.println(a == e);
        System.out.println(a == e.intern());
    }
}
Output
true
false
true
true
false
true
Watch out

Always compare string contents with equals() (or equalsIgnoreCase()). == compares references and only looks right when both sides happen to be pooled.

Do

Write "yes".equals(input) — the literal on the left can never throw NullPointerException when input is null. Or use Objects.equals(a, b).

Immutability in action
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String s = "hello";
        s.toUpperCase();                 // result discarded — s is unchanged
        System.out.println(s);
        s = s.toUpperCase();             // rebind the variable to the new object
        System.out.println(s);

        String t = s;
        s = s + "!";                     // creates a new String; t still points at the old one
        System.out.println(t + " " + s);
    }
}
Output
hello
HELLO
HELLO HELLO!
Why is String immutable?

String pool

Sharing one object between many references is only safe if nobody can change it.

Security

Class names, file paths, URLs and DB credentials passed as strings can't be altered after validation.

Hash caching

hashCode() is computed once and cached — perfect for HashMap keys.

Thread safety

Immutable objects can be shared across threads with no synchronization.

Concatenation in loops

Each += on a String allocates a new object and copies all characters so far — O(n²) for a loop of n appends. Use StringBuilder (next topics) or String.join / Collectors.joining.

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String slow = "";
        for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) slow += i;      // 5 new String objects

        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
        for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) sb.append(i);   // one growing buffer
        System.out.println(slow + " " + sb);
        System.out.println(String.join("-", "a", "b", "c"));
    }
}
Output
01234 01234
a-b-c
How many objects does new String("abc") create?

Up to two: the literal "abc" is placed in the string pool when the class is loaded (if not already there), and new always creates one more String object on the heap. If the literal is already pooled, only one new object is created.

Where is the string pool located?

Since Java 7 it is part of the regular heap (it used to be in PermGen), so pooled strings can be garbage-collected when unreferenced.

Why is char[] preferred over String for passwords?

A String is immutable and may sit in memory (or the pool) until GC; you cannot wipe it. A char[] can be overwritten with zeros right after use, and it doesn't show up by accident in logs via toString().

Core Language: Strings, Arrays & Methods

The String API you use daily — searching, slicing, splitting, formatting — plus Java 15 text blocks for multi-line literals.

Searching & slicing

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String s = "Hello, World";
        System.out.println(s.length() + " " + s.charAt(7));
        System.out.println(s.substring(7) + "|" + s.substring(0, 5));
        System.out.println(s.indexOf('o') + " " + s.lastIndexOf('o') + " " + s.indexOf("xyz"));
        System.out.println(s.contains("World") + " " + s.startsWith("Hell"));
        System.out.println(s.toLowerCase().replace('l', 'L'));
        System.out.println("  padded  ".strip() + "|" + "   ".isBlank() + "|" + "".isEmpty());
        System.out.println("apple".compareTo("banana") + " " + "b".compareTo("a"));
        System.out.println("Java".equalsIgnoreCase("JAVA"));

Splitting & joining
Java
import java.util.Arrays;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString("a,b,,c,,".split(",")));      // trailing empties dropped
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString("a,b,,c,,".split(",", -1)));  // keep them
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString("1.2.3".split("\\.")));       // "." is a regex!
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString("  one   two three ".trim().split("\\s+")));
        System.out.println(String.join(" | ", Arrays.asList("x", "y", "z")));
    }
}
Output
[a, b, , c]
[a, b, , c, , ]
[1, 2, 3]
[one, two, three]
x | y | z
Watch out

split takes a regex: split(".") returns an empty array because . matches every character. Escape metacharacters: "\\.", "\\|", or use Pattern.quote(".").

Characters
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String word = "Level42";
        int letters = 0, digits = 0;
        for (char c : word.toCharArray()) {
            if (Character.isLetter(c)) letters++;
            else if (Character.isDigit(c)) digits++;
        }
        System.out.println(letters + " letters, " + digits + " digits");
        System.out.println((int) 'a' + " " + (char) ('a' + 2) + " " + ('7' - '0'));
        System.out.println(new StringBuilder(word).reverse());
        System.out.println(word.chars().filter(Character::isUpperCase).count());
    }
}
Output
5 letters, 2 digits
97 c 7
24leveL
1
Formatting
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(String.format("%-6s|%5d|%.2f|%05d", "id", 42, 3.14159, 7));
        System.out.println("%s is %d years".formatted("Asha", 30));    // Java 15
        System.out.printf("%x %o %e %b%n", 255, 8, 12345.678, true);
        System.out.println(String.format("%,d", 1234567));
    }
}
Output

ff 10 1.234568e+04 true
1,234,567
Text blocks (Java 15)

A text block starts with """ followed by a newline. Incidental indentation (the common leading whitespace) is stripped, and line endings are normalised to \n — ideal for JSON, SQL and HTML.

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String json = """
            {
              "name": "Asha",
              "age": 30
            }
            """;
        System.out.print(json);
        String oneLine = """
            SELECT * \
            FROM users""";                  // \ joins lines
        System.out.println(oneLine);
        System.out.println("""
            Hi %s!""".formatted("Ravi"));
    }
}
Output
{
  "name": "Asha",
  "age": 30
}
SELECT * FROM users
Hi Ravi!
Text blocks

The position of the closing """ controls indentation: put it on its own line to get a trailing newline; put it after the last text to avoid one.

What is the difference between trim() and strip()?

trim() removes characters with code point ≤ U+0020 (ASCII spaces and control chars). strip() (Java 11) uses Character.isWhitespace, so it also removes Unicode whitespace such as U+2000. stripLeading() / stripTrailing() do one side.

What is the difference between replace and replaceAll?

Both replace all occurrences. replace(CharSequence, CharSequence) treats the target literally; replaceAll(String regex, String replacement) interprets a regular expression (and $ / \ in the replacement).

How do you check if a string is a palindrome?

Compare s with new StringBuilder(s).reverse().toString() using equals, or use two indices moving towards the middle — the latter is O(1) extra space.

Core Language: Strings, Arrays & Methods

StringBuilder & StringBuffer

A mutable character buffer. Use StringBuilder whenever you build a string piece by piece — especially in loops.

StringStringBuilderStringBuffer
Mutable?NoYesYes
Thread-safe?Yes (immutable)NoYes (synchronized methods)
SpeedSlow for repeated editsFastestSlower (locking)
Since1.01.51.0
Use whenValues that don't changeBuilding strings in one thread (almost always)Legacy code; shared buffer across threads (rare)
Core operations
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("Java");
        sb.append(' ').append(21).append("!");     // chaining — each call returns this
        System.out.println(sb);
        sb.insert(0, ">> ");
        System.out.println(sb);
        sb.replace(3, 7, "JAVA");
        System.out.println(sb);
        sb.deleteCharAt(sb.length() - 1).delete(0, 3);
        System.out.println(sb);
        sb.setCharAt(0, 'j');
        System.out.println(sb.reverse());
        System.out.println(sb.length() + " " + sb.indexOf("AV") + " " + sb.charAt(0));
        String result = sb.toString();              // freeze into an immutable String
        System.out.println(result);
    }
}
Output
Java 21!
>> Java 21!
>> JAVA 21!
JAVA 21
12 AVAj
7 3 1
12 AVAj
Capacity

A StringBuilder has a capacity (default 16). When full, it grows to roughly (old * 2) + 2 and copies — amortised O(1) per append. Pre-size with new StringBuilder(expectedLength) when you know it.

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
        System.out.println(sb.capacity());
        sb.append("12345678901234567");            // 17 chars > 16
        System.out.println(sb.capacity());
        System.out.println(new StringBuilder("abc").capacity());   // 16 + 3
    }
}
Output
16
34
19
Pitfalls
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        StringBuilder a = new StringBuilder("x");
        StringBuilder b = new StringBuilder("x");
        System.out.println(a.equals(b));                          // identity! not overridden
        System.out.println(a.toString().equals(b.toString()));
        System.out.println(a.compareTo(b) == 0);                  // Java 11+: content compare

        StringBuilder c = new StringBuilder('A');                  // char → int capacity 65!
        System.out.println("[" + c + "] " + c.capacity());
    }
}
Output
false
true
true
[] 65
Watch out

StringBuilder does not override equals/hashCode — compare toString() values (or use compareTo, Java 11+). And new StringBuilder('A') silently calls the int capacity constructor.

When does + matter?

The compiler already turns a + b + c in a single expression into efficient code. The problem is += inside a loop: each iteration creates a new String.

Why is StringBuilder faster than String concatenation in a loop?

String is immutable, so each s += x allocates a new string and copies all previous characters: O(n²) total. StringBuilder appends into one resizable array: O(n) amortised.

StringBuilder vs StringBuffer?

Same API. StringBuffer methods are synchronized (thread-safe, slower); StringBuilder is not synchronized and is the default choice. Sharing a builder between threads is a design smell anyway.

Core Language: Strings, Arrays & Methods

Conditionals & Loops

if/else, the ternary operator and four loop forms — plus labeled break/continue for nested loops.

if / else if / else
Java
public class Main {
    static String grade(int score) {
        if (score >= 90) return "A";
        else if (score >= 75) return "B";
        else if (score >= 50) return "C";
        else return "F";
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(grade(95) + grade(80) + grade(60) + grade(10));
        int n = 7;
        String parity = (n % 2 == 0) ? "even" : "odd";     // ternary: an expression
        System.out.println(n + " is " + parity);
    }
}
Output
ABCF
7 is odd
Watch out

Conditions must be boolean — if (x = 5) or if (n) do not compile in Java (unlike C). The one sneaky exception: if (flag = true) compiles for a boolean flag and is always true.

Loops at a glance
LoopSyntaxUse when
forfor (int i = 0; i < n; i++)You need the index or a counted range
enhanced forfor (T x : arrayOrIterable)Visiting every element, read-only
whilewhile (cond) { … }Unknown number of iterations; may run 0 times
do-whiledo { … } while (cond);Body must run at least once (menus, retries)
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        for (int i = 0, j = 10; i < j; i += 3, j -= 3)       // several variables
            System.out.print(i + ":" + j + " ");
        System.out.println();

        int[] nums = {3, 1, 4};
        int sum = 0;
        for (int x : nums) sum += x;
        System.out.println("sum=" + sum);

        int n = 1234, digits = 0;
        while (n > 0) { n /= 10; digits++; }
        System.out.println("digits=" + digits);

        int k = 100;
        do { System.out.println("runs once, k=" + k); } while (k < 10);
    }
}
Output
0:10 3:7
sum=8
digits=4
runs once, k=100
break, continue & labels
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        for (int i = 1; i <= 10; i++) {
            if (i % 2 == 0) continue;      // skip evens
            if (i > 7) break;              // stop the loop
            System.out.print(i + " ");
        }
        System.out.println();

        int[][] grid = {{1, 2, 3}, {4, 5, 6}, {7, 8, 9}};
        outer:
        for (int r = 0; r < grid.length; r++) {
            for (int c = 0; c < grid[r].length; c++) {
                if (grid[r][c] == 5) {
                    System.out.println("found at " + r + "," + c);
                    break outer;           // leaves BOTH loops
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
Output
1 3 5 7
found at 1,1
Common mistakes
  • Off-by-one: i <= arr.length → ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException. Use i < arr.length.
  • Modifying the loop variable inside an enhanced for does nothing to the array — x is a copy.
  • Removing from a List inside a for-each throws ConcurrentModificationException — use an Iterator or removeIf.
  • A stray ; — for (…); or while (…); makes an empty body.
  • Floating-point loop counters (for (double d = 0; d != 1; d += 0.1)) may never terminate.
What is the difference between while and do-while?

while checks the condition first, so the body may run zero times. do-while checks after the body, so it always runs at least once.

How do you break out of nested loops?

Put a label on the outer loop (outer:) and use break outer; (or continue outer;). Alternatives: move the loops into a method and return, or use a flag variable.

Can the ternary operator replace every if/else?

No — it is an expression that must produce a value of a common type; it can't contain statements. Use it for simple value choices; nested ternaries hurt readability.

Core Language: Strings, Arrays & Methods

Switch Expressions & Pattern Matching

Modern switch (Java 14) is an expression with arrow labels and no fall-through; Java 16–21 add pattern matching for instanceof, type patterns in switch, guards and record patterns.

Classic switch statement (fall-through)
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int day = 2;
        switch (day) {
            case 1:
                System.out.println("Mon");
            case 2:
                System.out.println("Tue");      // matches here...
            case 3:
                System.out.println("Wed");      // ...and falls through (no break!)
                break;
            default:
                System.out.println("Other");
        }
    }
}
Output
Tue
Wed

Old-style switch works on int-like types (byte, short, char, int and their wrappers), String (Java 7) and enum — never on long, float, double or boolean.

Switch expressions (Java 14)
Java
public class Main {
    enum Day { MON, TUE, WED, THU, FRI, SAT, SUN }

    static int letters(Day d) {
        return switch (d) {                     // an expression — yields a value
            case MON, FRI, SUN -> 6;            // several labels, no fall-through
            case TUE -> 7;
            case THU, SAT -> 8;
            case WED -> {
                int n = "Wednesday".length();
                yield n;                        // yield returns from a block
            }
        };                                      // exhaustive over the enum: no default needed
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(letters(Day.MON) + " " + letters(Day.WED));
        String cmd = "stop";
        String msg = switch (cmd) {
            case "go" -> "moving";
            case "stop" -> "halted";
            default -> "unknown";
        };
        System.out.println(msg);
    }
}
Output
6 9
halted
case X: (colon)case X -> (arrow)
Fall-throughYes, until breakNever
As an expressionYes, with yieldYes
Multiple labelscase 1: case 2:case 1, 2 ->
ExhaustivenessOnly required for switch expressionsSame
Pattern matching for instanceof (Java 16)
Java
public class Main {
    static String describe(Object o) {
        if (o instanceof String s && !s.isEmpty())   // test + cast + bind in one step
            return "String of length " + s.length();
        if (o instanceof Integer i)
            return "Integer doubled " + (i * 2);
        return "something else";
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(describe("hello"));
        System.out.println(describe(21));
        System.out.println(describe(3.5));
    }
}
Output
String of length 5
Integer doubled 42
something else
Type patterns, guards & null in switch (Java 21)
Java
public class Main {
    static String classify(Object o) {
        return switch (o) {
            case null -> "null!";                          // otherwise NPE
            case Integer i when i < 0 -> "negative int";   // guard with `when`
            case Integer i -> "int " + i;
            case String s when s.isBlank() -> "blank string";
            case String s -> "string '" + s + "'";
            case int[] arr -> "int array of " + arr.length;
            default -> "other: " + o.getClass().getSimpleName();
        };
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Object[] inputs = {-5, 7, " ", "hi", new int[3], 2.5, null};
        for (Object o : inputs) System.out.println(classify(o));
    }
}
Output
negative int
int 7
blank string
string 'hi'
int array of 3
other: Double
null!
Watch out

Order matters: a more general pattern before a specific one (case Integer i before case Integer i when i < 0) is a dominance compile error. Without case null, a null selector throws NullPointerException.

Record patterns & sealed hierarchies (Java 21)
Java
public class Main {
    sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Rect, Square {}
    record Circle(double r) implements Shape {}
    record Rect(double w, double h) implements Shape {}
    record Square(double side) implements Shape {}

    static double area(Shape s) {
        return switch (s) {                        // exhaustive: compiler knows all subtypes
            case Circle(double r) -> Math.PI * r * r;
            case Rect(double w, double h) -> w * h;     // deconstruct the record
            case Square(var side) -> side * side;
        };
    }

    record Point(int x, int y) {}
    record Line(Point from, Point to) {}

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.printf("%.2f %.1f %.1f%n", area(new Circle(1)), area(new Rect(2, 3)), area(new Square(4)));
        Object o = new Line(new Point(0, 0), new Point(3, 4));
        if (o instanceof Line(Point(var x1, var y1), Point(var x2, var y2)))   // nested patterns
            System.out.println("dx=" + (x2 - x1) + " dy=" + (y2 - y1));
    }
}
Output
3.14 6.0 16.0
dx=3 dy=4
Why it matters

Sealed interface + records + pattern switch = algebraic data types in Java. Add a new subtype and every non-exhaustive switch fails to compile — the compiler finds the code you forgot.

What does yield do in a switch expression?

It returns a value from a block-bodied case (case X -> { …; yield v; }) or from a colon-style case in a switch expression. return would try to leave the enclosing method instead.

Is a default needed in a switch expression?

The switch expression must be exhaustive. For an enum or a sealed type covering every constant/subtype, no default is needed; for int, String or Object you need default (or an unconditional pattern).

What happens if the selector of a switch is null?

Traditionally a NullPointerException. From Java 21 you can add case null (or case null, default ->) to handle it explicitly.

Core Language: Strings, Arrays & Methods

Arrays

A fixed-size, zero-indexed, contiguous container of one element type. Arrays are objects: they live on the heap and know their length.

Array — an object holding length elements of the same type, accessed by index 0 … length-1 in O(1). Its size is fixed at creation; bounds are checked at runtime.

Declaring and creating
Java
import java.util.Arrays;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int[] a = new int[4];                  // all zeros
        int[] b = {5, 3, 9};                   // array initializer
        String[] names = new String[2];        // all null
        boolean[] flags = new boolean[2];      // all false
        double[] d = new double[]{1.5, 2.5};

        a[0] = 42;
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString(a) + " len=" + a.length);
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString(b) + " " + Arrays.toString(names) + " " + Arrays.toString(flags) + " " + Arrays.toString(d));
        System.out.println(b);                 // prints type@hash — not the contents!
    }
}

The last line prints something like [I@1b6d3586 — [I means “array of int”. Always print arrays with Arrays.toString (or Arrays.deepToString for nested arrays).

Element typeDefault value
byte, short, int, long0
float, double0.0
char'\u0000'
booleanfalse
Any reference typenull
2D and jagged arrays
Java
import java.util.Arrays;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int[][] grid = new int[2][3];          // 2 rows × 3 columns
        grid[1][2] = 7;
        System.out.println(Arrays.deepToString(grid));

        int[][] jagged = new int[3][];         // rows created separately
        for (int i = 0; i < jagged.length; i++) {
            jagged[i] = new int[i + 1];
            Arrays.fill(jagged[i], i);
        }
        System.out.println(Arrays.deepToString(jagged));

        int[][] m = {{1, 2}, {3, 4}};
        int total = 0;
        for (int[] row : m) for (int x : row) total += x;
        System.out.println("total=" + total + " rows=" + m.length + " cols=" + m[0].length);
    }
}
Output
[[0, 0, 0], [0, 0, 7]]
[[0], [1, 1], [2, 2, 2]]
total=10 rows=2 cols=2
The java.util.Arrays toolbox
Java
import java.util.Arrays;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int[] a = {5, 2, 8, 1, 9};
        Arrays.sort(a);                                        // dual-pivot quicksort for primitives
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString(a));
        System.out.println(Arrays.binarySearch(a, 8));         // array must be sorted
        int[] bigger = Arrays.copyOf(a, 7);                    // pads with zeros
        int[] mid = Arrays.copyOfRange(a, 1, 3);
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString(bigger) + " " + Arrays.toString(mid));

        int[] x = {1, 2}, y = {1, 2};
        System.out.println((x == y) + " " + x.equals(y) + " " + Arrays.equals(x, y));

        Integer[] boxed = {3, 1, 2};
        Arrays.sort(boxed, (p, q) -> q - p);                   // comparator needs objects
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString(boxed));
        System.out.println(Arrays.stream(a).sum() + " " + Arrays.stream(a).max().getAsInt());
    }
}
Output
[1, 2, 5, 8, 9]
3
[1, 2, 5, 8, 9, 0, 0] [2, 5]
false false true
[3, 2, 1]
25 9
Watch out

array.equals(other) is inherited from Object — it compares references. Use Arrays.equals (or Arrays.deepEquals for 2D).

Arrays.asList pitfalls
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String[] arr = {"a", "b", "c"};
        List<String> view = Arrays.asList(arr);     // fixed-size view backed by arr
        view.set(0, "z");
        System.out.println(arr[0]);                  // the array changed too
        try {
            view.add("d");
        } catch (UnsupportedOperationException e) {
            System.out.println("cannot add: " + e.getClass().getSimpleName());
        }

        int[] prim = {1, 2, 3};
        System.out.println(Arrays.asList(prim).size());   // List<int[]> with ONE element
        List<String> copy = new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList(arr));   // real, growable list
        copy.add("d");
        System.out.println(copy);
    }
}
Output
z
cannot add: UnsupportedOperationException
1
[z, b, c, d]
Arrays are covariant (a design flaw)
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Object[] objs = new String[2];      // compiles: String[] is an Object[]
        objs[0] = "fine";
        try {
            objs[1] = 42;                   // runtime check fails
        } catch (ArrayStoreException e) {
            System.out.println("ArrayStoreException: " + e.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
Output
ArrayStoreException: java.lang.Integer
What is the difference between length, length() and size()?

array.length is a field of arrays; string.length() is a method on String; collection.size() is a method on collections.

Can an array's size change?

No. To grow, allocate a bigger array and copy (Arrays.copyOf, System.arraycopy) — which is exactly what ArrayList does internally.

Why can't you create a generic array like new T[n]?

Arrays check element types at runtime (they are reified), but generic types are erased. The JVM wouldn't know what T is, so new T[] and new List<String>[] are compile errors. Use List<T> or (T[]) new Object[n] with care.

Core Language: Strings, Arrays & Methods

Methods & Pass-by-Value

Java is always pass-by-value. For objects, the value passed is a copy of the reference — so you can mutate the object, but never reseat the caller's variable.

Anatomy of a method
Java
public class Main {
    //  modifiers   return type  name   parameters        throws clause
    public static   int          add(   int a, int b)    /* throws … */ {
        return a + b;
    }

    static void greet(String name) {           // void: returns nothing
        if (name == null) return;              // early return
        System.out.println("Hi " + name);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(add(2, 3));
        greet("Asha");
        greet(null);
    }
}
Output
5
Hi Asha
PartMeaning
SignatureName + parameter types (e.g. add(int, int)). Return type and parameter names are not part of it.
Access modifierpublic, protected, package-private (none), private
staticBelongs to the class — call as Main.add(…) without an object
Return typeAny type or void; every path of a non-void method must return or throw
ParametersLocal variables initialized with copies of the arguments
Pass-by-value, demonstrated
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    static void bump(int x) { x++; }                          // changes the copy only

    static void mutate(List<String> list) { list.add("added"); }   // same object → visible

    static void reassign(List<String> list) {
        list = new ArrayList<>();                             // local copy now points elsewhere
        list.add("lost");
    }

    static void swap(Integer a, Integer b) { Integer t = a; a = b; b = t; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int n = 5;
        bump(n);
        System.out.println(n);

        List<String> items = new ArrayList<>(List.of("x"));
        mutate(items);
        reassign(items);
        System.out.println(items);

        Integer p = 1, q = 2;
        swap(p, q);
        System.out.println(p + " " + q);                      // swap is impossible this way
    }
}
Output
5
[x, added]
1 2
caller: items → [list object]
→
call copies the reference
→
param list → same [list object]
→
list = new … rebinds only the param
Watch out

“Objects are passed by reference” is a common but wrong interview answer. The reference is passed by value: the method gets its own copy of the pointer.

Return values & multiple results
Java
public class Main {
    record MinMax(int min, int max) {}                 // return several values via a record

    static MinMax range(int... xs) {
        int lo = Integer.MAX_VALUE, hi = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
        for (int x : xs) { lo = Math.min(lo, x); hi = Math.max(hi, x); }
        return new MinMax(lo, hi);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        MinMax r = range(4, -2, 9, 0);
        System.out.println(r + " -> " + r.min() + ".." + r.max());
    }
}
Output
MinMax[min=-2, max=9] -> -2..9
Local variables & scope
  • Local variables have no default value — using one before assignment is a compile error (fields do get defaults).
  • A variable's scope is its enclosing block { … }; you can't redeclare a name that is already in scope in the same method.
  • var (Java 10) infers a local's type: var list = new ArrayList<String>(); — locals only, must be initialized.
  • Parameters can be final to document that they're not reassigned.
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int x;
        System.out.println(x);       // error: variable x might not have been initialized
    }
}
Is Java pass-by-value or pass-by-reference?

Strictly pass-by-value. Primitives: the value is copied. Objects: the reference is copied — the method can mutate the shared object but reassigning the parameter never affects the caller's variable. That's why a swap(a, b) method can't work.

Can two methods differ only by return type?

No. The signature is name + parameter types; int f() and long f() in the same class is a compile error because a call f() would be ambiguous.

What does static mean for a method?

It belongs to the class, not an instance: no this, can't access instance fields directly, and it is resolved at compile time (not overridden — only hidden).

Core Language: Strings, Arrays & Methods

Varargs & Method Overloading

Overloading = same name, different parameter lists, chosen at compile time. Varargs (T...) lets a method accept zero or more arguments as an array.

Overloading
Java
public class Main {
    static int area(int side) { return side * side; }
    static int area(int w, int h) { return w * h; }
    static double area(double r) { return Math.PI * r * r; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(area(3));
        System.out.println(area(2, 5));
        System.out.printf("%.2f%n", area(1.0));
    }
}
Output
9
10
3.14

Overloads must differ in the number, types or order of parameters. Different return types, parameter names, or exceptions alone are not enough.

Varargs
Java
import java.util.Arrays;

public class Main {
    static int sum(int... nums) {                   // nums is an int[]
        int total = 0;
        for (int n : nums) total += n;
        return total;
    }

    static String tag(String name, Object... attrs) {   // varargs must be LAST
        return name + Arrays.toString(attrs);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(sum() + " " + sum(1) + " " + sum(1, 2, 3));
        System.out.println(sum(new int[]{4, 5}));       // can pass an array directly
        System.out.println(tag("div", "id", 7, true));
        System.out.println(String.format("%s-%s", "a", "b"));   // printf/format use varargs
    }
}
Output
0 1 6
9
div[id, 7, true]
a-b
  • Only one varargs parameter per method, and it must be the last one.
  • Inside the method it is an ordinary array (possibly empty, never null unless the caller passes null).
  • Each call allocates a new array — hot paths often add fixed-arity overloads (like List.of(e1, e2, …)).
Overload resolution: which method wins?
1
Phase 1 — exact / widening
Match without boxing or varargs; primitive widening allowed (int → long → float → double).
2
Phase 2 — boxing / unboxing
Allow int ↔ Integer, then widening of references (Integer → Number → Object).
3
Phase 3 — varargs
Only if nothing matched in phases 1–2.
4
Most specific
If several match in the same phase, the most specific wins; if none is more specific → ambiguous compile error.
Java
public class Main {
    static void f(long x)    { System.out.println("long (widening)"); }
    static void f(Integer x) { System.out.println("Integer (boxing)"); }
    static void f(int... x)  { System.out.println("int... (varargs)"); }

    static void g(Object o)  { System.out.println("g(Object)"); }
    static void g(String s)  { System.out.println("g(String)"); }

    static void h(double d)  { System.out.println("h(double)"); }
    static void h(Object o)  { System.out.println("h(Object)"); }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        f(5);              // widening beats boxing beats varargs
        f(Integer.valueOf(5));
        f();
        g(null);           // String is more specific than Object
        g((Object) "hi");  // static (compile-time) type decides
        h('A');            // char → double widening wins over boxing to Character
    }
}
Output
long (widening)
Integer (boxing)
int... (varargs)
g(String)
g(Object)
h(double)
Watch out

Overloads are chosen from the compile-time (static) types of the arguments, not the runtime objects. Object o = "hi"; g(o); calls g(Object). Contrast with overriding, which is chosen at runtime.

Java
public class Main {
    static void k(Integer a, long b) {}
    static void k(long a, Integer b) {}

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        k(1, 2);          // error: reference to k is ambiguous
    }
}
A classic trap: List.remove
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of(10, 20, 30, 1));
        list.remove(1);                       // remove(int index) → removes 20
        System.out.println(list);
        list.remove(Integer.valueOf(1));      // remove(Object) → removes the value 1
        System.out.println(list);
    }
}
Output
[10, 30, 1]
[10, 30]
Can we overload main?

Yes — you can declare other main methods with different parameters, but the JVM only starts the program with public static void main(String[] args) (or String...).

What is the priority order in overload resolution?

Exact match / primitive widening first, then boxing/unboxing, then varargs. Within a phase the most specific method is chosen; if none is most specific the call is ambiguous and won't compile.

Is void f(int[] a) vs void f(int... a) a valid overload?

No — varargs compiles to an array parameter, so both have the same signature f(int[]): a duplicate method compile error.

Core Language: Strings, Arrays & Methods

Recursion

A method that calls itself on a smaller input. Every recursion needs a base case; each call pushes a stack frame, so depth is limited.

Recursion — solving a problem by reducing it to smaller instances of the same problem, until a base case can be answered directly.

The two parts
Java
public class Main {
    static long factorial(int n) {
        if (n <= 1) return 1;                 // base case
        return n * factorial(n - 1);          // recursive case: smaller input
    }

    static int sumDigits(int n) {
        return n < 10 ? n : n % 10 + sumDigits(n / 10);
    }

    static String reverse(String s) {
        return s.isEmpty() ? "" : reverse(s.substring(1)) + s.charAt(0);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(factorial(5) + " " + factorial(20));
        System.out.println(sumDigits(9875));
        System.out.println(reverse("stack"));
    }
}
Output
120 2432902008176640000
29
kcats
The call stack
1
factorial(3) called
Frame pushed: n=3, waiting on factorial(2).
2
factorial(2)
Frame pushed: n=2, waiting on factorial(1).
3
factorial(1) — base case
Returns 1; frame popped.
4
Unwinding
2 * 1 = 2 returned, then 3 * 2 = 6. Each frame pops as it returns.
Java
public class Main {
    static int depth = 0;

    static void dive() {
        depth++;
        dive();                               // no base case
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        try {
            dive();
        } catch (StackOverflowError e) {      // an Error, not an Exception
            System.out.println("StackOverflowError after " + (depth > 1000 ? "thousands of" : "few") + " calls");
        }
    }
}
Output
StackOverflowError after thousands of calls
Watch out

The JVM does not perform tail-call optimization. Even a tail-recursive method uses one frame per call, so deep recursion (e.g. 100 000 levels) overflows the default ~512 KB–1 MB thread stack. Convert to a loop, or run in a thread with a bigger stack.

Memoization: from exponential to linear
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    static int calls = 0;

    static long fibNaive(int n) {
        calls++;
        return n < 2 ? n : fibNaive(n - 1) + fibNaive(n - 2);    // O(2^n)
    }

    static Map<Integer, Long> memo = new HashMap<>();
    static long fibMemo(int n) {
        if (n < 2) return n;
        Long cached = memo.get(n);
        if (cached != null) return cached;
        long v = fibMemo(n - 1) + fibMemo(n - 2);                // O(n)
        memo.put(n, v);
        return v;
    }

    static long fibIter(int n) {                                 // O(n) time, O(1) space
        long a = 0, b = 1;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { long t = a + b; a = b; b = t; }
        return a;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(fibNaive(25) + " in " + calls + " calls");
        System.out.println(fibMemo(90) + " " + fibIter(90));
    }
}
Output
75025 in 242785 calls
2880067194370816120 2880067194370816120
Gotcha

Don't use memo.computeIfAbsent(n, k -> fib(k-1) + fib(k-2)) for recursive memoization on a HashMap — modifying the map inside its own computeIfAbsent throws ConcurrentModificationException (Java 9+).

Classic recursive patterns
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    static int binarySearch(int[] a, int target, int lo, int hi) {
        if (lo > hi) return -1;
        int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
        if (a[mid] == target) return mid;
        return a[mid] < target ? binarySearch(a, target, mid + 1, hi)
                               : binarySearch(a, target, lo, mid - 1);
    }

    static void subsets(int[] nums, int i, Deque<Integer> cur, List<String> out) {
        if (i == nums.length) { out.add(cur.toString()); return; }
        subsets(nums, i + 1, cur, out);            // exclude nums[i]
        cur.addLast(nums[i]);
        subsets(nums, i + 1, cur, out);            // include nums[i]
        cur.removeLast();                          // backtrack
    }

    static void hanoi(int n, char from, char to, char via, List<String> moves) {
        if (n == 0) return;
        hanoi(n - 1, from, via, to, moves);
        moves.add(from + ">" + to);
        hanoi(n - 1, via, to, from, moves);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(binarySearch(new int[]{1, 3, 5, 7, 9}, 7, 0, 4));
        List<String> out = new ArrayList<>();
        subsets(new int[]{1, 2, 3}, 0, new ArrayDeque<>(), out);
        System.out.println(out);
        List<String> moves = new ArrayList<>();
        hanoi(3, 'A', 'C', 'B', moves);
        System.out.println(moves.size() + " moves: " + moves);
    }
}
Output
3
[[], [3], [2], [2, 3], [1], [1, 3], [1, 2], [1, 2, 3]]
7 moves: [A>C, A>B, C>B, A>C, B>A, B>C, A>C]
RecursionIteration
Natural for trees, graphs, divide & conquer, backtrackingNatural for linear scans
O(depth) stack spaceO(1) extra space usually
Risk of StackOverflowErrorNo stack risk
Method-call overhead per stepFaster in the JVM
What happens if a recursive method has no base case?

It recurses until the thread's stack is exhausted and the JVM throws StackOverflowError (a subclass of VirtualMachineError, i.e. an Error).

Does Java optimize tail recursion?

No. The JVM spec has no tail-call elimination (partly to keep stack traces and security checks intact), so rewrite deep tail recursion as a loop.

How do you find the time complexity of a recursive function?

Write the recurrence: e.g. naive Fibonacci T(n) = T(n-1) + T(n-2) + O(1) → O(2ⁿ) (tighter: φⁿ); binary search T(n) = T(n/2) + O(1) → O(log n); merge sort T(n) = 2T(n/2) + O(n) → O(n log n) (Master theorem).

OOP Fundamentals

Classes & Objects

A class is a blueprint that bundles state (fields) and behaviour (methods); an object is one instance of it living on the heap.

Class — a user-defined type that declares fields (what each object knows) and methods (what each object can do).

Object — a concrete instance created with new. It has its own copy of every instance field and lives on the heap until no reference points to it.

The four pillars of OOP

Encapsulation

Hide state behind methods; the class controls how its data changes.

Abstraction

Expose what an object does, not how — abstract classes and interfaces.

Inheritance

A subclass reuses and extends a superclass (extends).

Polymorphism

One reference type, many runtime behaviours — overriding + dynamic dispatch.

Declaring a class and creating objects
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        BankAccount a = new BankAccount("Asha", 500);
        BankAccount b = new BankAccount("Ravi", 100);
        a.deposit(250);
        b.withdraw(30);
        System.out.println(a.describe());
        System.out.println(b.describe());
        System.out.println("accounts opened: " + BankAccount.count);
    }
}

class BankAccount {
    String owner;          // instance field: one copy per object
    double balance;
    static int count = 0;  // static field: one copy per class

    BankAccount(String owner, double balance) {   // constructor
        this.owner = owner;
        this.balance = balance;
        count++;
    }

    void deposit(double amount) { balance += amount; }
    void withdraw(double amount) { balance -= amount; }
    String describe() { return owner + " has " + balance; }
}
Output
Asha has 750.0
Ravi has 70.0
accounts opened: 2
What new does
1
Load the class
If BankAccount isn't loaded yet, the class loader loads, links and initialises it (static fields/blocks run once).
2
Allocate
Memory for the object is reserved on the heap; every field gets its default value (0, false, null).
3
Initialise
Field initialisers and instance initializer blocks run, then the constructor body.
4
Return a reference
new evaluates to a reference (think: a safe pointer) that is stored in the variable.
References, not objects, live in variables
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Point p1 = new Point();
        p1.x = 1;
        Point p2 = p1;          // copies the reference, not the object
        p2.x = 99;
        System.out.println(p1.x);        // same object
        System.out.println(p1 == p2);    // same reference?

        Point p3 = new Point();
        p3.x = 99;
        System.out.println(p1 == p3);    // different objects

        Point nothing = null;
        System.out.println(nothing == null);
    }
}

class Point { int x, y; }
Output
99
true
false
true
Default values of fields
Field typeDefault
byte, short, int, long0
float, double0.0
char'\u0000'
booleanfalse
Any reference (String, arrays, objects)null
Watch out

Only fields get defaults. Local variables have none — using one before assigning it is a compile error (variable x might not have been initialized).

Instance vs class members
Instance memberStatic (class) member
Belongs toeach objectthe class itself
Copiesone per objectexactly one
Accessed viaobj.fieldClassName.field
Can use this?YesNo
What is the difference between a class and an object?

A class is a compile-time blueprint (a type); an object is a runtime instance of that type on the heap. One class, many objects — each with its own instance fields.

Is p2 = p1 a copy of the object?

No. It copies the reference. Both variables point to the same object, so a change through one is visible through the other. To copy, write a copy constructor or factory (new Point(p1)).

Where do objects and references live?

Objects live on the heap. A local reference variable lives in the method's stack frame; a reference stored in a field lives inside its owning object on the heap.

OOP Fundamentals

Constructors & Initialization Order

A constructor initialises a new object. Java runs static setup once per class and then a fixed sequence for every new.

Constructor — a special member with the class's name and no return type that runs when new creates an object. It is not inherited and cannot be static, final or abstract.

Kinds of constructors
KindWhat it is
DefaultAdded by the compiler only if you write no constructor at all: no args, calls super().
No-argOne you write yourself with no parameters.
ParameterizedTakes arguments to set up state.
CopyTakes another instance of the same class: Point(Point other) (Java has no built-in copy constructor).
PrivatePrevents outside new — singletons, factories, utility classes.
Overloading and chaining with this(...)
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(new Pizza());
        System.out.println(new Pizza("large"));
        System.out.println(new Pizza("small", 3));
    }
}

class Pizza {
    private final String size;
    private final int toppings;

    Pizza() { this("medium"); }                 // delegates
    Pizza(String size) { this(size, 1); }       // delegates
    Pizza(String size, int toppings) {          // the one "real" constructor
        this.size = size;
        this.toppings = toppings;
    }

    @Override public String toString() { return size + " pizza, " + toppings + " topping(s)"; }
}
Output
medium pizza, 1 topping(s)
large pizza, 1 topping(s)
small pizza, 3 topping(s)
Key point

this(...) or super(...) must be the first statement of a constructor, and you can use only one of them. If you write neither, the compiler inserts super().

Watch out

Writing any constructor removes the free default one. class A { A(int x) {} } means new A() no longer compiles — a common surprise with frameworks (JPA, Jackson) that need a no-arg constructor.

Initializer blocks

Static initializer static { … }

Runs once, when the class is initialised (first active use). For complex static setup.

Instance initializer { … }

Runs on every new, before the constructor body — the compiler copies it into each constructor after super().

The full initialization order
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("-- first new Child()");
        new Child();
        System.out.println("-- second new Child()");
        new Child();
    }
}

class Parent {
    static { System.out.println("1. Parent static block"); }
    { System.out.println("3. Parent instance block"); }
    Parent() { System.out.println("4. Parent constructor"); }
}

class Child extends Parent {
