    static { System.out.println("2. Child static block"); }
    private String name = init();
    { System.out.println("6. Child instance block"); }
    Child() { System.out.println("7. Child constructor"); }

    private String init() { System.out.println("5. Child field initializer"); return "child"; }
}
Output
-- first new Child()
1. Parent static block
2. Child static block
3. Parent instance block
4. Parent constructor
5. Child field initializer
6. Child instance block
7. Child constructor
-- second new Child()
3. Parent instance block
4. Parent constructor
5. Child field initializer
6. Child instance block
7. Child constructor
1
Static, parent first
Superclass static fields/blocks, then subclass static fields/blocks — once per class, in textual order.
2
Parent instance part
super(...) runs first: parent field initialisers + instance blocks (textual order), then parent constructor body.
3
Child instance part
Child field initialisers + instance blocks in textual order, then the child constructor body.
Pitfall: calling an overridable method from a constructor
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        new Sub();
    }
}

class Base {
    Base() { describe(); }            // dynamic dispatch — calls Sub.describe()
    void describe() { System.out.println("Base"); }
}

class Sub extends Base {
    private String label = "ready";
    @Override void describe() { System.out.println("Sub label = " + label); }
}
Output
Sub label = null
Watch out

The subclass override runs before the subclass's fields are initialised, so it sees null/0. Never call overridable methods from a constructor — make them private or final.

Can a constructor be private? Why would you do that?

Yes. It stops other classes from calling new — used for singletons, static factory methods (List.of), and utility classes like Math or Collections.

Is a constructor inherited? Can it be overridden?

Neither. Constructors are not members that are inherited, so they cannot be overridden. A subclass must call a superclass constructor (implicitly super() or explicitly super(args)).

Can a constructor return a value or be final/static?

No return type at all (writing void turns it into an ordinary method with the class's name!). It cannot be static, final, abstract or synchronized.

In what order do static blocks, instance blocks and constructors run?

Static blocks: once per class, parent before child. Then for each new: parent instance initialisers → parent constructor → child instance initialisers (fields and blocks in source order) → child constructor.

OOP Fundamentals

this & super

this refers to the current object; super refers to the superclass part of the current object.

Uses of this
UseExampleWhy
Disambiguate a field from a parameterthis.name = name;Parameter shadows the field
Call another constructorthis(name, 0);Constructor chaining — first statement only
Pass the current objectregistry.add(this);Callbacks, listeners
Return the current objectreturn this;Fluent / builder APIs
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Counter c = new Counter().add(5).add(10).add(-3);   // chaining via return this
        System.out.println(c.total);
    }
}

class Counter {
    int total;
    Counter add(int total) {
        this.total += total;   // this.total = field, total = parameter
        return this;
    }
}
Output
12
Uses of super
UseExample
Call a superclass constructorsuper(name); — first statement of the constructor
Call the overridden superclass methodsuper.describe()
Access a hidden superclass fieldsuper.name (when the subclass declares a field with the same name)
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Manager m = new Manager("Asha", 3);
        System.out.println(m.describe());
        m.showNames();
    }
}

class Employee {
    protected String name;
    String kind = "employee";
    Employee(String name) { this.name = name; }
    String describe() { return name + " (" + kind + ")"; }
}

class Manager extends Employee {
    private final int reports;
    String kind = "manager";                   // hides Employee.kind

    Manager(String name, int reports) {
        super(name);                           // must come first
        this.reports = reports;
    }

    @Override String describe() {
        return super.describe() + " manages " + reports;   // reuse parent logic
    }

    void showNames() {
        System.out.println(this.kind + " / " + super.kind);
    }
}
Output
Asha (employee) manages 3
manager / employee
Key point

Employee.describe() prints employee — fields are not polymorphic, so inside Employee the name kind always means Employee.kind. Avoid hiding fields; it is confusing.

Rules
  • this and super cannot be used in a static context — there is no current object.
  • A constructor can start with this(...) or super(...), never both.
  • super.super.method() is not allowed — you can only reach one level up.
  • If the superclass has no no-arg constructor, every subclass constructor must call super(args) explicitly.
Java
class Animal {
    Animal(String name) { }
}

class Dog extends Animal {
    Dog() { }   // implicit super() — but Animal has no no-arg constructor
}
javac
Main.java:6: error: constructor Animal in class Animal cannot be applied to given types;
    Dog() { }   // implicit super() — but Animal has no no-arg constructor
          ^
  required: String
  found:    no arguments
  reason: actual and formal argument lists differ in length
Why can't this be used inside a static method?

A static method belongs to the class and runs without any instance, so there is no current object for this (or super) to refer to.

What happens if you don't call super(...) in a constructor?

The compiler inserts super() as the first statement. If the parent has no accessible no-arg constructor, compilation fails.

OOP Fundamentals

The static Keyword

static members belong to the class, not to any object: one copy, shared by all instances, usable without new.

Where static can appear
OnMeaningTypical use
FieldOne copy per class (in the class's data, not in each object)Counters, constants (static final), caches
MethodCalled on the class; no thisUtilities (Math.max), factories (List.of)
Block static { }Runs once when the class is initialisedComplex static setup
Nested classA class inside a class that needs no outer instanceBuilders, helper types, Map.Entry
Importimport static java.lang.Math.*;Use sqrt(x) without the class name
Static field vs instance field
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        User a = new User("asha");
        User b = new User("ravi");
        User c = new User("meera");
        System.out.println(a.id + " " + b.id + " " + c.id);
        System.out.println("users created: " + User.created);
        System.out.println(User.isValidName("x"));   // static method: no object needed
    }
}

class User {
    static int created = 0;   // shared by all Users
    final int id;             // each User has its own
    final String name;

    User(String name) {
        this.name = name;
        this.id = ++created;
    }

    static boolean isValidName(String s) {
        // return name.length() > 1;   // would not compile: no 'this' in a static method
        return s.length() > 1;
    }
}
Output
1 2 3
users created: 3
false
What a static method can and cannot touch

Can

Other static fields and methods directly; instance members through an object reference (u.name).

Cannot

Instance fields/methods directly, this, super. Static methods are also not polymorphic — they are hidden, not overridden.

Static blocks and lazy initialisation
Java
import java.util.Map;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("main starts");
        System.out.println(Codes.lookup("IN"));
        System.out.println(Codes.lookup("JP"));
    }
}

class Codes {
    private static final Map<String, String> NAMES;
    static {
        System.out.println("Codes initialised");   // runs on first use, once
        NAMES = Map.of("IN", "India", "JP", "Japan");
    }
    static String lookup(String code) { return NAMES.get(code); }
}
Output
main starts
Codes initialised
India
Japan
Key point

A class is initialised lazily, on its first active use (creating an instance, calling a static method, reading a non-constant static field). That is what makes the holder idiom singleton lazy and thread-safe.

Why is main static?

The JVM must call main before any object of your class exists. Making it static lets the launcher invoke Main.main(args) directly on the class without having to choose a constructor.

Static nested class
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Order.Line line = new Order.Line("pen", 3);   // no Order instance needed
        System.out.println(line.item() + " x" + line.qty());
    }
}

class Order {
    record Line(String item, int qty) { }   // nested records/enums/interfaces are implicitly static
}
Output
pen x3
Watch out

Mutable static state is global state: it is shared across all threads and tests and never garbage-collected while the class is loaded. Prefer static final constants and stateless static helpers.

Can we override a static method?

No. A static method with the same signature in a subclass hides the parent's one. Which version runs is chosen at compile time from the reference type, not the object.

Can a static method access instance variables?

Not directly — there is no this. It can only reach them through an explicit object reference passed in or created inside it.

When is a static block executed?

Once, when the class is initialised — at its first active use — after the class is loaded and linked. Multiple static blocks run in source order.

OOP Fundamentals

The final Keyword

final means cannot be changed after it is set: a variable can't be reassigned, a method can't be overridden, a class can't be extended.

final in three places
Applied toEffectExample
Variable (local, field, parameter)Assigned exactly oncefinal int max = 10;
MethodCannot be overridden by subclassesfinal void audit()
ClassCannot be extendedString, Integer, all records
Final variables
Java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class Main {
    static final double TAX_RATE = 0.18;         // constant: static final, UPPER_CASE

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        final List<String> names = new ArrayList<>();
        names.add("Asha");                        // OK: the object is mutable
        names.add("Ravi");
        // names = new ArrayList<>();             // error: cannot assign a value to final variable names
        System.out.println(names);

        final int limit;                          // "blank final": assigned once, later
        limit = args.length > 0 ? 5 : 10;
        System.out.println(limit + " " + TAX_RATE);
    }
}
Output
[Asha, Ravi]
10 0.18
Watch out

final freezes the reference, not the object. A final List can still be modified; for a truly unchangeable list use List.of(...) or Collections.unmodifiableList(...).

Blank finals must be assigned in every constructor
Java
class Account {
    private final String id;
    Account(String id) { this.id = id; }
    Account() { }   // forgot to assign id
}
javac
Main.java:4: error: variable id might not have been initialized
    Account() { }   // forgot to assign id
                ^
Effectively final

A local variable that is never reassigned is effectively final even without the keyword. Lambdas and anonymous/local classes may only capture effectively final locals.

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int base = 100;                       // never reassigned → effectively final
        Runnable r = () -> System.out.println(base + 1);
        r.run();
    }
}
Output
101
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int count = 0;
        Runnable r = () -> System.out.println(count);
        count++;   // makes count NOT effectively final
        r.run();
    }
}
javac
Main.java:4: error: local variables referenced from a lambda expression must be final or effectively final
        Runnable r = () -> System.out.println(count);
                                              ^
Final methods and classes
  • A final method keeps its behaviour fixed in every subclass — good for security-sensitive or template-method steps.
  • A final class cannot be subclassed: String, the wrapper classes, LocalDate, every record and enum (without bodies).
  • private methods are implicitly final (they aren't inherited, so nothing can override them).
  • abstract and final together are illegal: one demands a subclass, the other forbids it.
final vs finally vs finalize
KeywordWhat it is
finalModifier: no reassignment / overriding / extending.
finallyBlock after try/catch that always runs (cleanup).
finalize()Object method once called by the GC before reclaiming an object — deprecated for removal since Java 18; use try-with-resources or Cleaner.
Does final make an object immutable?

No. It only stops the variable from being reassigned. Immutability needs a design: final class, private final fields, no setters, defensive copies of mutable parts.

Why is String final?

So no subclass can break its immutability — which the string pool, hashCode caching, security checks (file paths, class names) and thread safety all rely on.

Can a final variable be initialised later?

Yes — a blank final. A local must be assigned before use; an instance field in every constructor (or an initializer block); a static final in a static block. Exactly once.

OOP Fundamentals

Access Modifiers & Packages

Packages group related classes into namespaces; access modifiers decide who may see each class and member.

The four access levels
ModifierSame classSame packageSubclass (other package)Everywhere
privateYesNoNoNo
(default / package-private)YesYesNoNo
protectedYesYesYes (through inheritance)No
publicYesYesYesYes
Key point

Order from most to least restrictive: private → default → protected → public. Start with private and widen only when needed.

Where each modifier is allowed
  • Top-level classes: only public or default. A source file may contain at most one public top-level class, and the file must be named after it.
  • Members (fields, methods, constructors, nested types): all four.
  • Local variables: no access modifiers at all (only final).
  • Interface members: public by default; methods may also be private (Java 9+).
private is per class, not per object
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Money a = new Money(500);
        Money b = new Money(300);
        System.out.println(a.isMoreThan(b));
        // System.out.println(a.cents);   // error: cents has private access in Money
    }
}

class Money {
    private final long cents;
    Money(long cents) { this.cents = cents; }

    boolean isMoreThan(Money other) {
        return this.cents > other.cents;   // OK: other object, same class
    }
}
Output
true
Packages

Package — a namespace that groups related types and maps to a directory: package com.shop.billing; lives in com/shop/billing/. The fully qualified name is com.shop.billing.Invoice.

Terminal
src/com/shop/billing/Invoice.java     # package com.shop.billing;
src/com/shop/app/Main.java            # package com.shop.app;  import com.shop.billing.Invoice;

$ javac -d out $(find src -name '*.java')
$ java -cp out com.shop.app.Main
  • Naming: reversed domain, all lowercase — com.google.common.collect.
  • java.lang is imported automatically (String, Math, Integer, System…).
  • Sub-packages are not related for access: com.shop cannot see package-private members of com.shop.billing.
  • Classes without a package line are in the unnamed package — fine for scripts, never for real projects.
Imports
Java
import java.util.*;                        // on-demand: every public type in java.util
import java.time.LocalDate;                // single-type import (preferred)
import static java.lang.Math.max;          // static import of one member
import static java.lang.Math.PI;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> nums = new ArrayList<>(List.of(3, 9, 4));
        System.out.println(max(nums.get(0), nums.get(1)));
        System.out.printf("%.2f%n", PI);
        System.out.println(LocalDate.of(2024, 2, 29).plusDays(1));
        java.util.Date legacy = null;      // fully qualified name, no import needed
        System.out.println(legacy);
    }
}
Output
9
3.14
2024-03-01
null
Watch out

An import is only a compile-time shortcut for names — it loads nothing and has no runtime cost. import java.util.* does not import sub-packages like java.util.function.

protected in practice

protected members are visible to the whole package plus subclasses elsewhere — but a subclass in another package can only use them through its own type (this or a subclass reference), not through an arbitrary parent-class object.

Can a top-level class be private or protected?

No — only public or package-private. Nested classes can use all four modifiers.

What is the default access level?

Package-private: visible to every class in the same package and nowhere else — not even to subclasses in other packages.

Can an overriding method reduce visibility?

No. An override may keep or widen access (protected → public) but never narrow it, otherwise code written against the parent type would break.

OOP Fundamentals

Encapsulation

Keep fields private and expose behaviour through methods, so the class alone guarantees that its state is always valid.

Encapsulation — bundling data with the methods that operate on it, and hiding the data so outside code can change it only through a controlled interface.

Why it matters

Invariants

The class validates every change, so an object can never be in an illegal state (negative balance, empty name).

Freedom to change

Internal representation can change (cents instead of double) without breaking callers.

Easier debugging

Every mutation passes through a few methods — one place for a breakpoint or log.

Thread safety

Controlled access is where you add synchronisation or make the class immutable.

Getters, setters and validation
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Account acc = new Account("Asha", 1000);
        acc.withdraw(300);
        System.out.println(acc.getOwner() + ": " + acc.getBalance());
        try {
            acc.withdraw(5000);
        } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {
            System.out.println("rejected: " + e.getMessage());
        }
        System.out.println("balance still " + acc.getBalance());
    }
}

class Account {
    private final String owner;
    private long balance;                  // hidden: no one can set it to -1

    Account(String owner, long opening) {
        if (owner == null || owner.isBlank()) throw new IllegalArgumentException("owner required");
        this.owner = owner;
        this.balance = opening;
    }

    String getOwner() { return owner; }
    long getBalance() { return balance; }  // read-only: no setBalance()

    void withdraw(long amount) {
        if (amount <= 0 || amount > balance)
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("invalid amount " + amount);
        balance -= amount;
    }
}
Output
Asha: 700
rejected: invalid amount 5000
balance still 700
Key point

Don't generate a setter for every field. Expose operations (withdraw, rename) that express intent and enforce rules — a public getter + setter pair on every field is barely better than a public field.

Leaking mutable state — and defensive copies
Java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<String> input = new ArrayList<>(List.of("math", "physics"));
        Leaky leaky = new Leaky(input);
        Safe safe = new Safe(input);

        input.add("hacked");                 // caller mutates its own list
        leaky.getSubjects().clear();         // caller mutates via the getter

        System.out.println("leaky: " + leaky.getSubjects());
        System.out.println("safe:  " + safe.getSubjects());
        try {
            safe.getSubjects().add("x");
        } catch (UnsupportedOperationException e) {
            System.out.println("safe getter is read-only");
        }
    }
}

class Leaky {
    private final List<String> subjects;
    Leaky(List<String> subjects) { this.subjects = subjects; }   // stores caller's list
    List<String> getSubjects() { return subjects; }              // hands out internals
}

class Safe {
    private final List<String> subjects;
    Safe(List<String> subjects) { this.subjects = List.copyOf(subjects); }   // copy in
    List<String> getSubjects() { return subjects; }                          // already unmodifiable
}
Output
leaky: []
safe:  [math, physics]
safe getter is read-only
Do

Copy mutable arguments on the way in and return copies or unmodifiable views on the way out (List.copyOf, new Date(d.getTime())).

Avoid

Returning a reference to an internal array, list or Date — callers can then change your object's state behind its back.

Encapsulation vs abstraction
EncapsulationAbstraction
FocusHow data is protectedWhat is exposed
Mechanismprivate fields, accessors, validationInterfaces, abstract classes, simple method names
LevelImplementationDesign
How do you achieve encapsulation in Java?

Make fields private, provide methods (getters, intention-revealing operations) that validate changes, and avoid leaking references to mutable internals — use defensive copies.

Is a class with private fields and public getters/setters for all of them encapsulated?

Only nominally. If every setter accepts anything, the class can't protect its invariants. Real encapsulation exposes behaviour and validates every state change.

What is a JavaBean?

A convention: public no-arg constructor, private fields, getX/setX (isX for booleans) accessors, and usually Serializable. Frameworks use reflection on these names.

Inheritance & Polymorphism

Inheritance

A subclass extends a superclass to inherit its fields and methods, adding or overriding behaviour — an IS-A relationship.

Inheritance — the mechanism by which one class (subclass / child) acquires the non-private members of another (superclass / parent). Every class ultimately extends java.lang.Object.

Basic syntax
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Dog d = new Dog("Rex");
        d.eat();          // inherited from Animal
        d.bark();         // Dog's own
        System.out.println(d.name + " is an Animal? " + (d instanceof Animal));
        System.out.println(d.getClass().getSuperclass().getSimpleName());
        System.out.println(Animal.class.getSuperclass().getName());
    }
}

class Animal {
    protected String name;
    Animal(String name) { this.name = name; }
    void eat() { System.out.println(name + " eats"); }
}

class Dog extends Animal {
    Dog(String name) { super(name); }
    void bark() { System.out.println(name + " barks"); }
}
Output
Rex eats
Rex barks
Rex is an Animal? true
Animal
java.lang.Object
What is (and isn't) inherited
MemberInherited?
public / protected fields and methodsYes
Package-private membersOnly if the subclass is in the same package
private membersNo (they exist inside the object, but the subclass can't access them)
ConstructorsNo — they are called via super(...)
Static membersAccessible through the subclass, but hidden rather than overridden
Types of inheritance
TypeShapeIn Java?
SingleB extends AYes
MultilevelC extends B, B extends AYes
HierarchicalB extends A, C extends AYes
Multiple (classes)C extends A, BNo — ambiguity (diamond problem)
Multiple (interfaces)C implements X, YYes — interfaces carry no instance state
Hybridmix of the aboveOnly via interfaces
Constructor chaining up the hierarchy
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        new Puppy();
    }
}

class Animal { Animal() { System.out.println("Animal()"); } }
class Dog extends Animal { Dog() { System.out.println("Dog()"); } }
class Puppy extends Dog { Puppy() { System.out.println("Puppy()"); } }
Output
Animal()
Dog()
Puppy()
Key point

Each constructor implicitly starts with super(), so construction runs top-down: Object → Animal → Dog → Puppy. The parent part of the object is always ready before the child's constructor body runs.

No multiple inheritance of classes
Java
class A { }
class B { }
class C extends A, B { }
javac
Main.java:3: error: '{' expected
class C extends A, B { }
                 ^
IS-A vs HAS-A: prefer composition

IS-A (inheritance)

Car extends Vehicle. Tight coupling: the child depends on the parent's implementation details and is fixed at compile time.

HAS-A (composition)

Car has an Engine field. Loose coupling: swap the part, test it alone, reuse it anywhere.

Java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        CountingBag viaInheritance = new CountingBag();
        viaInheritance.addAll(List.of("a", "b", "c"));
        System.out.println("inheritance count = " + viaInheritance.added);

        CountingWrapper viaComposition = new CountingWrapper();
        viaComposition.addAll(List.of("a", "b", "c"));
        System.out.println("composition count = " + viaComposition.added);
    }
}

class Bag {                                   // a parent you don't control
    private final List<String> items = new ArrayList<>();
    void add(String s) { items.add(s); }
    void addAll(List<String> c) { for (String s : c) add(s); }   // self-use: calls add()
}

class CountingBag extends Bag {               // IS-A: counts every item twice
    int added;
    @Override void add(String s) { added++; super.add(s); }
    @Override void addAll(List<String> c) { added += c.size(); super.addAll(c); }
}

class CountingWrapper {                       // HAS-A: forwards to a private Bag
    private final Bag bag = new Bag();
    int added;
    void add(String s) { added++; bag.add(s); }
    void addAll(List<String> c) { added += c.size(); bag.addAll(c); }
}
Output
inheritance count = 6
composition count = 3
Watch out

Extending a class you don't control ties you to its self-use patterns (the fragile base class problem). Effective Java: “Favor composition over inheritance” — inherit only when there is a true IS-A relationship and the parent was designed for extension.

Why doesn't Java support multiple inheritance of classes?

To avoid the diamond problem: if C extends A, B and both define m() or a field x, which one does C get? Java allows multiple interface inheritance instead, where conflicts must be resolved explicitly and there is no instance state to duplicate.

Are private members inherited?

They are part of the subclass object's memory, but not inherited as members: the subclass can't name or access them. It reaches them only through inherited non-private methods.

What is the superclass of every class?

java.lang.Object. A class with no extends clause implicitly extends Object, which provides equals, hashCode, toString, getClass, wait/notify and more.

Inheritance & Polymorphism

Method Overriding vs Overloading

Overloading = same name, different parameters, chosen at compile time. Overriding = same signature in a subclass, chosen at runtime.

Side by side
OverloadingOverriding
WhereSame class (or inherited)Subclass redefines a superclass/interface method
SignatureSame name, different parameter listSame name and same parameters
Return typeAnythingSame or a subtype (covariant)
AccessAnythingSame or wider
Checked exceptionsAnythingSame, narrower or none — never new/broader
ResolvedCompile time (static binding)Runtime (dynamic dispatch)
static / private / finalCan be overloadedCannot be overridden
Polymorphism kindCompile-timeRuntime
Overloading
Java
public class Main {
    static String show(int x)          { return "int " + x; }
    static String show(long x)         { return "long " + x; }
    static String show(double x)       { return "double " + x; }
    static String show(Integer x)      { return "Integer " + x; }
    static String show(Object x)       { return "Object " + x; }
    static String show(int... xs)      { return "varargs " + xs.length; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        byte b = 5;
        System.out.println(show(7));        // exact match
        System.out.println(show(b));        // widening byte → int beats boxing
        System.out.println(show(7L));
        System.out.println(show(7.0f));     // float → double
        System.out.println(show("hi"));     // String → Object
        System.out.println(show());         // only varargs fits
    }
}
Output
int 7
int 5
long 7
double 7.0
Object hi
varargs 0
Key point

Overload resolution picks the most specific method in three phases: (1) exact/widening, (2) boxing/unboxing, (3) varargs. The return type alone never distinguishes overloads.

Overriding with @Override
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Shape s = new Circle(2);           // reference type Shape, object Circle
        System.out.println(s.name());      // runtime: Circle.name()
        Circle copy = new Circle(2).copy();   // covariant return: no cast needed
        System.out.printf("%.2f%n", copy.area());
    }
}

class Shape {
    protected String name() { return "shape"; }
    Shape copy() { return new Shape(); }
    double area() { return 0; }
}

class Circle extends Shape {
    private final double r;
    Circle(double r) { this.r = r; }

    @Override public String name() { return "circle"; }        // protected → public: widening OK
    @Override Circle copy() { return new Circle(r); }          // covariant return type
    @Override double area() { return Math.PI * r * r; }
}
Output
circle
12.57
Watch out

Always write @Override. Without it, a typo or wrong parameter type silently creates an overload instead — e.g. public boolean equals(Point p) does not override equals(Object).

Java
class Point {
    int x;
    @Override
    public boolean equals(Point other) { return other.x == x; }   // wrong parameter type
}
javac
Main.java:3: error: method does not override or implement a method from a supertype
    @Override
    ^
Rules the compiler enforces
Java
import java.io.IOException;

class Parent {
    public void run() { }
    void load() throws IOException { }
}

class Child extends Parent {
    @Override protected void run() { }   // narrowing public → protected
}
javac
Main.java:9: error: run() in Child cannot override run() in Parent
    @Override protected void run() { }   // narrowing public → protected
                             ^
  attempting to assign weaker access privileges; was public
  • An override may throw fewer or narrower checked exceptions (FileNotFoundException instead of IOException), or none; unchecked exceptions are unrestricted.
  • final methods can't be overridden; private methods aren't inherited, so a same-named subclass method is just a new method.
  • Call the parent version with super.method().
Static methods are hidden, not overridden
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Parent p = new Child();
        System.out.println(p.stat());      // static: chosen by reference type
        System.out.println(p.inst());      // instance: chosen by object type
        System.out.println(p.label);       // fields are hidden too
        System.out.println(((Child) p).label);
    }
}

class Parent {
    String label = "parent-field";
    static String stat() { return "Parent.stat"; }
    String inst() { return "Parent.inst"; }
}

class Child extends Parent {
    String label = "child-field";
    static String stat() { return "Child.stat"; }
    @Override String inst() { return "Child.inst"; }
}
Output
Parent.stat
Child.inst
parent-field
child-field
Can we overload main?

Yes — any number of main overloads may exist, but the JVM only launches public static void main(String[] args).

Can we override a static or private method?

No. A static method with the same signature hides the parent's (resolved by reference type at compile time). A private method isn't visible to the subclass, so a same-named method is a brand-new method.

Can two overloads differ only by return type?

No. The call f(1) wouldn't tell the compiler which one you want, so it's a compile error (method f(int) is already defined).

What is a covariant return type?

An overriding method may return a subtype of the parent method's return type — e.g. Circle copy() overriding Shape copy() — so callers need no cast. clone() overrides commonly use it.

Inheritance & Polymorphism

Polymorphism & Dynamic Dispatch

One reference type, many behaviours: the JVM picks the overriding method of the actual object at runtime.

Polymorphism — “many forms” — code written against a supertype works with any subtype. Java has compile-time polymorphism (overloading) and runtime polymorphism (overriding + dynamic dispatch).

Runtime polymorphism
Java
import java.util.List;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Payment> payments = List.of(new Card(100), new Upi(250), new Cash(40));
        double total = 0;
        for (Payment p : payments) {             // one loop, three behaviours
            System.out.println(p.describe());
            total += p.fee();
        }
        System.out.println("total fees = " + total);
    }
}

abstract class Payment {
    protected final double amount;
    Payment(double amount) { this.amount = amount; }
    abstract double fee();
    String describe() { return getClass().getSimpleName() + " " + amount + " fee " + fee(); }
}

class Card extends Payment { Card(double a) { super(a); } double fee() { return amount * 0.02; } }
class Upi  extends Payment { Upi(double a)  { super(a); } double fee() { return 0; } }
class Cash extends Payment { Cash(double a) { super(a); } double fee() { return 1; } }
Output
Card 100.0 fee 2.0
Upi 250.0 fee 0.0
Cash 40.0 fee 1.0
total fees = 3.0
Key point

Adding a new payment type needs no change to the loop — that is the Open/Closed Principle in action, and why polymorphism beats if (type == ...) chains.

Static type vs runtime type
QuestionDecided byWhen
Which methods may I call?Reference (static) typeCompile time
Which overload is chosen?Static types of the argumentsCompile time
Which override runs?Runtime class of the objectRuntime (dynamic dispatch)
Which field is read?Reference type — fields are not polymorphicCompile time
Which static method runs?Reference typeCompile time
How dynamic dispatch works
1
Compile
javac checks the method exists on the static type and emits invokevirtual Shape.area() (or invokeinterface).
2
Look up
At runtime the JVM reads the object's actual class and finds area in its method table (vtable) — the most specific override.
3
Optimise
The JIT often inlines the call when only one implementation has been seen (monomorphic call site), so polymorphism is usually free.
Upcasting and downcasting
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Animal a = new Dog();              // upcast: implicit, always safe
        a.speak();
        // a.fetch();                      // error: Animal has no fetch()

        if (a instanceof Dog d) {          // pattern matching (Java 16+): test + cast
            d.fetch();
        }
        Dog d2 = (Dog) a;                  // explicit downcast: checked at runtime
        d2.fetch();

        Animal c = new Cat();
        try {
            Dog wrong = (Dog) c;           // compiles, fails at runtime
            wrong.fetch();
        } catch (ClassCastException e) {
            System.out.println("ClassCastException");
        }
    }
}

class Animal { void speak() { System.out.println("..."); } }
class Dog extends Animal {
    @Override void speak() { System.out.println("Woof"); }
    void fetch() { System.out.println("fetching"); }
}
class Cat extends Animal { @Override void speak() { System.out.println("Meow"); } }
Output
Woof
fetching
fetching
ClassCastException
Watch out

A downcast is a promise to the compiler. If the object isn't really of that type you get ClassCastException at runtime — guard with instanceof (ideally the pattern form if (x instanceof Dog d)), or better, redesign so an overridden method does the work.

Overloading is decided at compile time
Java
public class Main {
    static String greet(Object o) { return "object"; }
    static String greet(String s) { return "string"; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Object o = "hello";            // runtime type String, static type Object
        System.out.println(greet(o));  // overload chosen from the STATIC type
        System.out.println(greet("hello"));
        System.out.println(o.toString().length());   // override chosen at runtime
    }
}
Output
object
string
5
What is dynamic method dispatch?

The mechanism by which a call to an overridden method through a superclass reference is resolved at runtime, using the actual object's class. It is how runtime polymorphism is implemented (via method tables).

Are fields polymorphic?

No. A field access is resolved from the reference's static type. If a subclass declares a field with the same name it hides the parent's; parentRef.x still reads the parent's field. Access state through methods instead.

Can you achieve polymorphism without inheritance?

Yes, via interfaces: unrelated classes implementing the same interface can be used interchangeably. Overloading and generics are other (compile-time) forms.

Inheritance & Polymorphism

Abstract Classes

An abstract class is a partial implementation: it can hold state, constructors and concrete methods, but leaves some methods for subclasses to fill in.

Abstract class — a class declared abstract that cannot be instantiated. It may declare abstract methods (no body) that every concrete subclass must implement.

Rules
  • A class with at least one abstract method must be declared abstract (an abstract class may also have none).
  • It can have constructors (called via super(...)), instance fields, static members, final and private methods.
  • A concrete subclass must implement all inherited abstract methods, or be abstract itself.
  • abstract can't be combined with final, static or private on a method — those can't be overridden.
Java
abstract class Shape {
    abstract double area();
}

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Shape s = new Shape();
    }
}
javac
Main.java:7: error: Shape is abstract; cannot be instantiated
        Shape s = new Shape();
                  ^
Template method pattern

The abstract class fixes the algorithm skeleton in a final method and lets subclasses supply the varying steps.

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        new CsvReport().generate();
        new JsonReport().generate();
    }
}

abstract class Report {
    private final String title;
    protected Report(String title) { this.title = title; }   // abstract classes have constructors

    // template method: fixed order, cannot be overridden
    public final void generate() {
        System.out.println("== " + title + " ==");
        System.out.println(header());
        System.out.println(body());
        footer();
    }

    protected abstract String header();       // required steps
    protected abstract String body();
    protected void footer() { System.out.println("-- end --"); }   // optional hook
}

class CsvReport extends Report {
    CsvReport() { super("CSV"); }
    @Override protected String header() { return "name,score"; }
    @Override protected String body() { return "asha,91"; }
}

class JsonReport extends Report {
    JsonReport() { super("JSON"); }
    @Override protected String header() { return "{"; }
    @Override protected String body() { return "  \"asha\": 91\n}"; }
    @Override protected void footer() { }                             // override the hook
}
Output
== CSV ==
name,score
asha,91
-- end --
== JSON ==
{
  "asha": 91
}
Anonymous subclass of an abstract class
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Greeter g = new Greeter("Asha") {          // anonymous concrete subclass
            @Override String greeting() { return "Namaste"; }
        };
        System.out.println(g.greet());
    }
}

abstract class Greeter {
    private final String name;
    Greeter(String name) { this.name = name; }
    abstract String greeting();
    String greet() { return greeting() + ", " + name + "!"; }
}
Output
Namaste, Asha!
When to choose an abstract class

Use an abstract class when…

Subclasses are closely related and share state or constructor logic; you want protected helpers or a fixed template method.

Use an interface when…

You define a capability unrelated classes can have (Comparable, Runnable), or need a type to have several such capabilities.

Can an abstract class have a constructor? Why?

Yes. It can't be called with new directly, but subclasses call it via super(...) to initialise the fields the abstract class declares.

Can an abstract class have no abstract methods?

Yes — marking it abstract simply prevents instantiation (e.g. HttpServlet had none historically). It signals extend me, don't use me directly.

Can an abstract method be private, static or final?

No. An abstract method must be overridden; private methods aren't inherited, static methods are hidden not overridden, and final forbids overriding.

Inheritance & Polymorphism

Interfaces

An interface is a contract of methods a class promises to provide. A class can implement many interfaces — Java's answer to multiple inheritance.

Interface — a reference type declaring abstract methods (plus, since Java 8, default and static methods, and since Java 9, private methods). Fields are implicitly public static final constants.

What an interface can contain
MemberImplicit modifiersSince
Abstract methodpublic abstract1.0
Constant fieldpublic static final1.0
default method (has a body, inherited)public8
static method (called on the interface only)public8
private / private static helper method—9
Nested typespublic static1.0
Implementing several interfaces
Java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Duck duck = new Duck();
        List<Swimmer> swimmers = new ArrayList<>(List.of(duck, new Fish()));
        for (Swimmer s : swimmers) s.swim();
        Flyer f = duck;                     // a Duck is also a Flyer
        f.fly();
        System.out.println(Flyer.MAX_ALTITUDE);
        System.out.println(duck instanceof Swimmer && duck instanceof Flyer);
    }
}

interface Swimmer { void swim(); }
interface Flyer {
    int MAX_ALTITUDE = 10_000;              // public static final
    void fly();
}

class Duck implements Swimmer, Flyer {
    public void swim() { System.out.println("duck paddles"); }   // must be public
    public void fly()  { System.out.println("duck flies"); }
}

class Fish implements Swimmer {
    public void swim() { System.out.println("fish swims"); }
}
Output
duck paddles
fish swims
duck flies
10000
true
Default, static and private methods
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Greeter en = name -> "Hello, " + name;    // one abstract method → lambda works
        System.out.println(en.greet("Asha"));
        System.out.println(en.greetLoudly("Ravi"));      // default method
        System.out.println(Greeter.polite().greet("Meera"));   // static factory
    }
}

interface Greeter {
    String greet(String name);                  // abstract

    default String greetLoudly(String name) {   // inherited, can be overridden
        return shout(greet(name));
    }

    static Greeter polite() {                   // Greeter.polite(), not inherited by implementers
        return name -> "Good evening, " + name;
    }

    private String shout(String s) {            // helper shared by default methods
        return s.toUpperCase() + "!";
    }
}
Output
Hello, Asha
HELLO, RAVI!
Good evening, Meera
Key point

Default methods exist so interfaces can evolve: Java 8 added forEach, stream(), removeIf to Collection/Iterable without breaking millions of existing implementations.

The diamond problem with default methods

If a class inherits the same default method from two interfaces, it must override it and may pick one with Interface.super.method(). Rules: (1) a class method always wins over an interface default; (2) a more specific sub-interface wins; (3) otherwise you must resolve it yourself.

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(new Phone().start());
    }
}

interface Camera { default String start() { return "camera on"; } }
interface Radio  { default String start() { return "radio on"; } }

class Phone implements Camera, Radio {
    @Override public String start() {           // required: otherwise compile error
        return Camera.super.start() + " + " + Radio.super.start();
    }
}
Output
camera on + radio on
Java
interface Camera { default String start() { return "camera on"; } }
interface Radio  { default String start() { return "radio on"; } }
class Phone implements Camera, Radio { }
javac
Main.java:3: error: types Camera and Radio are incompatible;
class Phone implements Camera, Radio { }
^
  class Phone inherits unrelated defaults for start() from types Camera and Radio
Abstract class vs interface
Abstract classInterface
Instance state (fields)YesNo — only public static final constants
ConstructorsYesNo
Multiple inheritanceNo — extends one classYes — implements many
Method bodiesAnydefault, static, private only
Member accessAny modifierpublic (or private helpers)
RelationshipIS-A (shared identity)CAN-DO (capability)
Lambda targetNoYes, if exactly one abstract method (functional interface)
Marker and functional interfaces

Marker interface

No methods at all; tags a class for special treatment: Serializable, Cloneable, RandomAccess. Modern code often uses annotations instead.

Functional interface

Exactly one abstract method, so a lambda or method reference can implement it: Runnable, Comparator, Function. Mark with @FunctionalInterface.

Java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.LinkedList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.RandomAccess;

public class Main {
    static String strategy(List<?> list) {
        return list instanceof RandomAccess ? "index loop" : "iterator";
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(strategy(new ArrayList<>()));
        System.out.println(strategy(new LinkedList<>()));
    }
}
Output
index loop
iterator
Why were default methods added in Java 8?

For backward-compatible interface evolution: new methods (like Collection.stream()) could be added with a body so existing implementing classes kept compiling. They also let interfaces offer convenience methods (Comparator.reversed()).

Can an interface have a constructor or instance fields?

No. Interfaces carry no per-object state; any field is implicitly public static final. That is exactly why multiple interface inheritance is safe.

Can an interface extend another interface? A class?

An interface can extend any number of interfaces (interface C extends A, B). It can never extend a class.

If a class and an interface both provide the same method, which wins?

The class. “Class wins” — a concrete (or inherited superclass) method always takes precedence over an interface default method.

Advanced Types

Inner, Nested, Local & Anonymous Classes

A class can be declared inside another class, method or expression. The four kinds differ in one key way: whether they hold a hidden reference to an enclosing instance.

Nested class — any class declared inside another class. A static nested class is just a namespaced top-level class; a non-static one is an inner class and is tied to an instance of the outer class.

The four kinds at a glance
KindDeclaredOuter instance?Typical use
Static nestedstatic class Node inside a classNoHelpers that belong with the outer class: Map.Entry, Builder, linked-list Node
Inner (member)class Itr inside a classYes — Outer.thisIterators and views that need the outer object's fields
Localclass X inside a method bodyYes, if in an instance methodA one-method helper type with a name
Anonymousnew Interface() { … } expressionYes, if in an instance contextOne-off implementation; mostly replaced by lambdas
Static nested vs inner
Java
public class Main {
    private int size = 3;
    private static String kind = "outer";

    static class StaticNested {            // no outer instance
        String describe() { return "nested sees " + kind; }   // only static members
    }

    class Inner {                          // bound to a Main instance
        int size = 10;
        String describe() {
            return "inner " + size + ", outer " + Main.this.size;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        StaticNested n = new StaticNested();          // no Main needed
        System.out.println(n.describe());

        Main outer = new Main();
        Main.Inner in = outer.new Inner();            // needs an outer instance
        System.out.println(in.describe());
    }
}
Output
nested sees outer
inner 10, outer 3
Key point

Inside an inner class, this is the inner object and Main.this is the enclosing one. Use the qualified form when a field name is shadowed.

Local and anonymous classes
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    interface Greeter { String greet(String name); }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String greeting = "Hello";                  // effectively final → can be captured

        class Polite implements Greeter {           // local class: has a name, lives in this method
            public String greet(String name) { return greeting + ", " + name + "!"; }
        }

        Greeter shout = new Greeter() {             // anonymous class: no name, one instance
            @Override
            public String greet(String name) { return greeting.toUpperCase() + " " + name; }
        };

        Greeter lambda = name -> greeting + " " + name;   // same thing, as a lambda

        System.out.println(new Polite().greet("Asha"));
        System.out.println(shout.greet("Ravi"));
        System.out.println(lambda.greet("Mei"));

        List<String> names = new ArrayList<>(List.of("bob", "Al", "carol"));
        names.sort(new Comparator<String>() {       // classic pre-Java-8 style
            public int compare(String a, String b) { return a.length() - b.length(); }
        });
        System.out.println(names);
    }
}
Output
Hello, Asha!
HELLO Ravi
Hello Mei
[Al, bob, carol]
Capturing variables: effectively final

Local, anonymous classes and lambdas can read local variables of the enclosing method only if they are final or effectively final (never reassigned). The value is copied into the object, so allowing later changes would make the copy and the variable disagree.

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int count = 0;
        Runnable r = () -> System.out.println(count);
        count++;                 // makes count not effectively final
        r.run();
    }
}
Compiler error
Main.java:4: error: local variables referenced from a lambda expression must be final or effectively final
        Runnable r = () -> System.out.println(count);
                                              ^
Do

Need a mutable counter inside a lambda? Use an AtomicInteger, a one-element array, or — better — restructure with a stream.

Anonymous class vs lambda
Anonymous classLambda
ImplementsAny interface or abstract/concrete class, many methodsOnly a functional interface (one abstract method)
this meansThe anonymous object itselfThe enclosing instance
StateCan have fieldsNo fields of its own
Compiled toA separate Main$1.class fileinvokedynamic — no extra class file
Memory leak

Every inner/anonymous class created in an instance method keeps a hidden reference to Outer.this. If it outlives the outer object (a listener, a cached task, a Runnable handed to a thread) it keeps the whole outer object alive — a classic memory leak (e.g. Android Activities). Prefer static nested classes unless you really need the outer instance.

Static nested vs inner class — when to use which?

Default to static nested: it's cheaper (no hidden outer reference), can be created without an outer instance, and can't leak the outer object. Use an inner class only when it must read or write the outer instance's state, like an iterator over the outer collection.

Why must captured local variables be effectively final?

The lambda/anonymous object gets a copy of the value (locals live on the stack and may be gone when the object runs). If the variable could change afterwards, the copy would silently be stale, so Java forbids it at compile time.

What does this refer to inside a lambda vs an anonymous class?

In an anonymous class, this is the anonymous object. A lambda has no identity of its own, so this is the enclosing class instance.

Advanced Types

The Object Class: equals, hashCode & toString

Every class extends java.lang.Object. Its equals, hashCode and toString defaults are almost never what a value class wants — and equals and hashCode must be overridden together.

What Object gives every class
MethodDefault behaviourOverride?
boolean equals(Object o)Identity: this == oYes, for value classes
int hashCode()Identity-based numberAlways together with equals
String toString()ClassName@1b6d3586 (class + hex hash)Yes — for logs and debugging
Class<?> getClass()Runtime classFinal — cannot
Object clone()Shallow field copy (needs Cloneable)Rarely — prefer copy constructors
wait / notify / notifyAllMonitor-based thread coordinationFinal — cannot
finalize()Deprecated for removal (Java 9+)Never
The equals / hashCode contract
  • Reflexive: x.equals(x) is true.
  • Symmetric: x.equals(y) ⇔ y.equals(x).
  • Transitive: x.equals(y) and y.equals(z) ⇒ x.equals(z).
  • Consistent: repeated calls give the same answer while the objects don't change.
  • x.equals(null) is false.
  • Equal objects must have equal hash codes. (Unequal objects may share a hash code — that's a collision, which is legal.)
A correct implementation
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Point a = new Point(1, 2), b = new Point(1, 2);
        System.out.println(a == b);          // different objects
        System.out.println(a.equals(b));     // same value
        System.out.println(a.hashCode() == b.hashCode());
        System.out.println(a);               // our toString

        Set<Point> set = new HashSet<>(List.of(a));
        System.out.println(set.contains(b));
    }
}

final class Point {
    private final int x, y;
    Point(int x, int y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }

    @Override
    public boolean equals(Object o) {
        if (this == o) return true;                    // fast path
        if (!(o instanceof Point p)) return false;     // also handles null
        return x == p.x && y == p.y;
    }

    @Override
    public int hashCode() { return Objects.hash(x, y); }

    @Override
    public String toString() { return "Point[x=" + x + ", y=" + y + "]"; }
}
Output
false
true
true
Point[x=1, y=2]
true
Key point

@Override makes the compiler check that you really override. A classic bug is public boolean equals(Point p) — that overloads equals(Object), so HashSet and List.contains never call it.

What breaks when hashCode is missing
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Set<Key> set = new HashSet<>();
        set.add(new Key("id-7"));
        System.out.println(new Key("id-7").equals(new Key("id-7")));
        System.out.println(set.contains(new Key("id-7")));   // looks in the wrong bucket
    }
}

class Key {
    final String id;
    Key(String id) { this.id = id; }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        return o instanceof Key k && id.equals(k.id);
    }
    // hashCode NOT overridden → identity hash, different for each object
}
Output
true
false

HashSet/HashMap first use hashCode() to pick a bucket and only then call equals on the entries in that bucket. Two "equal" keys with different hash codes land in different buckets and are never compared.

Mutable keys

Never use a mutable field in hashCode for an object stored in a hash collection. Change the field after inserting and the object is stranded in the old bucket: contains returns false and remove fails.

getClass() vs instanceof in equals
CheckSubclass instancesTrade-off
o instanceof Point pCan be equal to a PointLiskov-friendly, but symmetry breaks if a subclass adds fields and overrides equals
getClass() != o.getClass()Never equal to a PointStrictly symmetric, but a harmless subclass (or proxy) is never equal

Common guidance: make value classes final (or use a record) and use instanceof — then the question doesn't arise.

Helpers in java.util.Objects
Java
import java.util.Objects;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String a = null, b = "x";
        System.out.println(Objects.equals(a, b));          // null-safe, no NPE
        System.out.println(Objects.equals(null, null));
        System.out.println(Objects.hash(1, "a") == Objects.hash(1, "a"));
        System.out.println(Objects.hashCode(null));        // 0 for null
        System.out.println(Objects.toString(a, "n/a"));    // default for null
        System.out.println(Objects.requireNonNullElse(a, "fallback"));
        try {
            Objects.requireNonNull(a, "name must not be null");
        } catch (NullPointerException e) {
            System.out.println(e.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
Output
false
true
true
0
n/a
fallback
name must not be null
clone() — briefly

Object.clone() makes a shallow copy and only works if the class implements the marker interface Cloneable (otherwise it throws CloneNotSupportedException). It bypasses constructors and is awkward with final fields. Arrays are the one place it's pleasant: int[] copy = arr.clone();.

Do

Prefer a copy constructor new Point(other) or a static factory Point.copyOf(other) over clone().

Why must you override hashCode when you override equals?

Hash-based collections look up the bucket by hashCode() before calling equals. If two equal objects return different hash codes, HashMap.get/HashSet.contains look in the wrong bucket and fail — the contract equal ⇒ same hash is broken.

Can two unequal objects have the same hashCode?

Yes — that's a collision and it's allowed; the map then falls back to equals inside the bucket. Only the reverse is forbidden. Many collisions just make lookups slower.

What does the default toString print?

getClass().getName() + "@" + Integer.toHexString(hashCode()) — e.g. Point@1b6d3586. It is not a memory address, just the identity hash code in hex.

What is wrong with public boolean equals(Point other)?

It overloads instead of overriding equals(Object). Collections call equals(Object), which is still the identity version. Always declare the parameter as Object and add @Override.

Advanced Types

Records

A record (Java 16+) is a concise, immutable data carrier: declare the components and the compiler writes the constructor, accessors, equals, hashCode and toString.

Record — a final class whose state is exactly its components: record Point(int x, int y) {} gets private final fields, a canonical constructor, accessors x() / y(), and value-based equals/hashCode/toString.

What you get for free
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    record Point(int x, int y) {}

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Point p = new Point(3, 4);
        System.out.println(p);                     // generated toString
        System.out.println(p.x() + p.y());         // accessors: x(), not getX()
        System.out.println(p.equals(new Point(3, 4)));
        System.out.println(new HashSet<>(List.of(p)).contains(new Point(3, 4)));
    }
}
Output
Point[x=3, y=4]
7
true
true
Generated memberFor record Point(int x, int y)
Fieldsprivate final int x; private final int y;
Canonical constructorPoint(int x, int y)
Accessorsint x(), int y()
equals / hashCodeCompare / hash all components
toStringPoint[x=3, y=4]
Compact constructor: validate and normalise
Java
public class Main {
    record Range(int lo, int hi) {
        Range {                                    // compact canonical constructor: no parameter list
            if (lo > hi) throw new IllegalArgumentException("lo > hi: " + lo + " > " + hi);
        }
        int length() { return hi - lo; }           // extra methods are fine
        static Range of(int a, int b) {            // static factory
            return new Range(Math.min(a, b), Math.max(a, b));
        }
    }

    record Email(String value) {
        Email {
            value = value.trim().toLowerCase();     // reassign the parameter, not this.value
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(new Range(2, 9).length());
        System.out.println(Range.of(9, 2));
        System.out.println(new Email("  Asha@Mail.COM "));
        try {
            new Range(5, 1);
        } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {
            System.out.println("rejected: " + e.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
Output
7
Range[lo=2, hi=9]
Email[value=asha@mail.com]
rejected: lo > hi: 5 > 1
Key point

In a compact constructor the fields are assigned after your code runs, from the (possibly reassigned) parameters. You cannot write this.lo = … there.

Records and shallow immutability
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    record Team(String name, List<String> members) {
        Team {
            members = List.copyOf(members);        // defensive, unmodifiable copy
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<String> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of("Asha"));
        Team t = new Team("core", list);
        list.add("Ravi");                          // doesn't leak into the record
        System.out.println(t.members());
        try {
            t.members().add("Mei");
        } catch (UnsupportedOperationException e) {
            System.out.println("members are read-only");
        }
    }
}
Output
[Asha]
members are read-only
Watch out

A record's fields are final, but the objects they point to are not magically immutable. A List component is still mutable unless you copy it with List.copyOf in the compact constructor.

Rules and limitations
AllowedNot allowed
Static fields and methods, instance methodsExtra instance fields
Implementing interfacesExtending a class (records implicitly extend java.lang.Record)
Nested records, generic records record Pair<A, B>(A a, B b)Being extended — records are implicitly final
Overriding accessors / toString / equalsMutating components (fields are final)
Annotations on componentsNon-canonical constructors that don't delegate to this(…)

Great for

DTOs, API responses, map keys, tuples returned from methods, events, sealed-hierarchy cases with pattern matching.

Not for

JPA entities (need mutability and a no-arg constructor), classes with hidden or derived mutable state.

Record vs Lombok @Data vs a hand-written class?

A record is a language feature: immutable, transparent, no library needed, and it works with record patterns in switch. Lombok @Data generates mutable getters/setters. Write a normal class when you need mutability, inheritance or encapsulated state.

Are records immutable?

Shallowly. The component fields are private final and there are no setters, but a mutable component (a List, an array) can still be changed through its reference unless you defensively copy it.

Can a record have a no-arg constructor?

Only as a non-canonical constructor that delegates: Point() { this(0, 0); }. Every constructor must end up calling the canonical one.

Advanced Types

Enums

An enum is a class with a fixed, named set of instances. It's type-safe, can carry fields and behaviour, and works in switch, EnumMap and EnumSet.

Enum — a special class whose instances are exactly the listed constants, created once when the class loads. Each constant is a public static final instance; every enum implicitly extends java.lang.Enum.

Built-in methods
Java
public class Main {
    enum Day { MON, TUE, WED, THU, FRI, SAT, SUN }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Day d = Day.WED;
        System.out.println(d + " " + d.name() + " " + d.ordinal());
        System.out.println(Day.valueOf("FRI").compareTo(Day.MON));  // by ordinal
        System.out.println(Day.values().length);
        System.out.println(d == Day.WED);          // safe: constants are singletons
        try {
            Day.valueOf("Funday");
        } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {
            System.out.println(e.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
Output
WED WED 2
4
7
true
No enum constant Main.Day.Funday
MemberMeaning
values()Array of all constants in declaration order (a fresh copy each call)
valueOf(String)Constant by exact name, else IllegalArgumentException
name() / toString()Declared name (toString can be overridden, name cannot)
ordinal()0-based position — don't persist it; reordering constants changes it
compareToCompares by ordinal; enums are Comparable
Fields, constructors and methods
Java
public class Main {
    enum Planet {
        MERCURY(3.303e+23, 2.4397e6),
        EARTH(5.976e+24, 6.37814e6);

        private final double mass, radius;            // per-constant data
        Planet(double mass, double radius) {          // constructor is implicitly private
            this.mass = mass;
            this.radius = radius;
        }
        double surfaceGravity() {
            return 6.67300E-11 * mass / (radius * radius);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        for (Planet p : Planet.values())
            System.out.printf("%s %.2f%n", p, p.surfaceGravity());
    }
}
Output
MERCURY 3.70
EARTH 9.80
Constant-specific bodies
Java
public class Main {
    enum Op {
        ADD("+") { int apply(int a, int b) { return a + b; } },
        MUL("*") { int apply(int a, int b) { return a * b; } };

        final String symbol;
        Op(String symbol) { this.symbol = symbol; }
        abstract int apply(int a, int b);            // each constant must implement it
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        for (Op op : Op.values())
            System.out.println("6 " + op.symbol + " 7 = " + op.apply(6, 7));
    }
}
Output
6 + 7 = 13
6 * 7 = 42
Switch over an enum
Java
public class Main {
    enum Level { LOW, MEDIUM, HIGH }

    static String action(Level l) {
        return switch (l) {             // exhaustive: no default needed
            case LOW -> "ignore";
            case MEDIUM -> "log";
            case HIGH -> "page on-call";
        };
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        for (Level l : Level.values()) System.out.println(l + " -> " + action(l));
    }
}
Output
LOW -> ignore
MEDIUM -> log
HIGH -> page on-call
Key point

In a case label you write the bare constant LOW, not Level.LOW (qualified names in case labels are only allowed from Java 21). A switch expression over an enum must cover every constant or have a default.

EnumSet and EnumMap
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    enum Day { MON, TUE, WED, THU, FRI, SAT, SUN }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        EnumSet<Day> weekend = EnumSet.of(Day.SAT, Day.SUN);          // a bit vector inside
        EnumSet<Day> weekdays = EnumSet.complementOf(weekend);
        System.out.println(weekdays);
        System.out.println(EnumSet.range(Day.TUE, Day.THU));

        Map<Day, Integer> hours = new EnumMap<>(Day.class);          // array indexed by ordinal
        hours.put(Day.FRI, 6);
        hours.put(Day.MON, 8);
        System.out.println(hours);                                   // always in declaration order
    }
}
Output
[MON, TUE, WED, THU, FRI]
[TUE, WED, THU]
{MON=8, FRI=6}
Do

Use EnumSet instead of bit flags and EnumMap instead of HashMap with enum keys — both are faster, compact, and iterate in declaration order.

The enum singleton
Java
public class Main {
    enum Registry {
        INSTANCE;
        private int count;
        int next() { return ++count; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Registry.INSTANCE.next();
        System.out.println(Registry.INSTANCE.next());
    }
}
Output
2

Effective Java calls a single-element enum the best singleton: the JVM guarantees one instance, it is thread-safe to initialise, and it is safe against reflection and serialization attacks that break classic singletons.

Watch out

Don't store or persist ordinal() (e.g. in a database). Adding or reordering a constant silently changes every ordinal. Persist name() or an explicit code field instead.

Can an enum extend a class? Implement an interface?

It cannot extend anything — it already extends java.lang.Enum. It can implement interfaces, which is a common way to make several enums interchangeable.

Why is comparing enums with == safe?

Each constant is created exactly once per class loader and can't be instantiated again (constructors are private; reflection and deserialization are blocked). So identity equals equality, and == is also null-safe.

Why can't an enum constructor be public?

The set of instances is fixed at compile time. Allowing new would break that guarantee, so enum constructors are implicitly private.

Advanced Types

Sealed Classes & Interfaces

A sealed type (Java 17) lists exactly which classes may extend it. The compiler then knows the full set of subtypes — which makes switch over them exhaustive.

Sealed — sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Square — only the named types may directly implement Shape. Each permitted subtype must itself be final, sealed or non-sealed.

Why seal a hierarchy?

Closed set of cases

Model "one of these and nothing else": a Result is Ok or Err; a JSON value is one of six kinds.

Exhaustive switch

switch over a sealed type needs no default — and adding a new subtype makes every non-exhaustive switch a compile error.

Controlled extension

Public enough to use, not open for arbitrary subclassing — stronger than package-private, weaker than final.

Subtype modifiers
Permitted subtype is…Meaning
finalNo further subclasses (records and enums are implicitly final)
sealed … permits …Continues the restriction one level down
non-sealedRe-opens that branch — anyone may extend it
Sealed interface + records + pattern switch
Java
public class Main {
    sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Square, Rect {}
    record Circle(double r) implements Shape {}
    record Square(double side) implements Shape {}
    record Rect(double w, double h) implements Shape {}

    static double area(Shape s) {
        return switch (s) {                    // exhaustive: no default
            case Circle c -> Math.PI * c.r() * c.r();
            case Square sq -> sq.side() * sq.side();
            case Rect(double w, double h) -> w * h;     // record pattern (Java 21)
        };
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Shape[] shapes = { new Circle(1), new Square(2), new Rect(2, 3) };
        for (Shape s : shapes) System.out.printf("%s -> %.2f%n", s, area(s));
    }
}
Output
Circle[r=1.0] -> 3.14
Square[side=2.0] -> 4.00
Rect[w=2.0, h=3.0] -> 6.00
Key point

This is Java's version of algebraic data types: the sealed interface is the "sum", each record is a "product". Together with pattern matching it replaces many uses of the Visitor pattern.

Sealed classes with final / non-sealed
Java
public class Main {
    static abstract sealed class Vehicle permits Car, Truck {}
    static final class Car extends Vehicle {}
    static non-sealed class Truck extends Vehicle {}      // open again
    static class Pickup extends Truck {}                  // allowed: Truck is non-sealed

    static String describe(Vehicle v) {
        return switch (v) {
            case Car c -> "car";
            case Truck t -> "truck (" + t.getClass().getSimpleName() + ")";
        };
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(describe(new Car()));
        System.out.println(describe(new Pickup()));
    }
}
Output
car
truck (Pickup)
Trying to extend a sealed type
Java
public class Main {
    sealed interface Shape permits Circle {}
    record Circle(double r) implements Shape {}
    record Triangle(double b, double h) implements Shape {}   // not in permits

    public static void main(String[] args) {}
}
Compiler error
Main.java:4: error: class is not allowed to extend sealed class: Shape (as it is not listed in its 'permits' clause)
    record Triangle(double b, double h) implements Shape {}   // not in permits
    ^
  • Permitted subclasses must be in the same module (or the same package in the unnamed module).
  • If all subtypes are in the same source file, the permits clause can be omitted — the compiler infers it.
  • Every permitted subtype must directly extend/implement the sealed type.
Sealed class vs final class vs enum?

final allows no subclasses; an enum has a fixed set of instances of one type; a sealed type has a fixed set of subtypes, each of which can carry different data (e.g. Circle(r) vs Rect(w, h)).

What does sealing buy you in a switch?

Exhaustiveness checking: the compiler knows every possible subtype, so a pattern switch needs no default, and adding a new permitted subtype turns every switch that forgot it into a compile error instead of a runtime surprise.

Advanced Types

Immutable Classes

An immutable object's state can't change after construction. It is automatically thread-safe, safe to share and cache, and safe as a HashMap key — String, Integer and LocalDate are all immutable.

The recipe
1
Make the class final
(or make constructors private with static factories) so no subclass can add mutable state.
2
Make every field private final
assigned once, in the constructor.
3
No setters
no method that changes a field. "Withers" return a new object: withPrice(…).
4
Defensive copy mutable inputs
copy lists/arrays/dates passed to the constructor, so the caller can't change them later.
5
Never expose mutable internals
return copies or unmodifiable views from getters.
A broken "immutable" class
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<String> tags = new ArrayList<>(List.of("java"));
        Article a = new Article("Intro", tags);
        tags.add("hacked");                 // caller still holds the same list
        a.tags().clear();                   // getter hands out the internal list
        a.tags().add("oops");
        System.out.println(a.tags());
    }
}

final class Article {
    private final String title;
    private final List<String> tags;        // final reference, mutable list
    Article(String title, List<String> tags) { this.title = title; this.tags = tags; }
    List<String> tags() { return tags; }
}
Output
[oops]
Watch out

final makes the reference unchangeable, not the object it points to. A final List can still be cleared.

Fixed with defensive copies
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<String> tags = new ArrayList<>(List.of("java"));
        Article a = new Article("Intro", tags);
        tags.add("hacked");                           // no effect on a
        try {
            a.tags().add("oops");
        } catch (UnsupportedOperationException e) {
            System.out.println("read-only");
        }
        Article b = a.withTitle("Basics");            // "modify" = new object
        System.out.println(a.title() + " " + a.tags());
        System.out.println(b.title() + " " + b.tags());
    }
}

final class Article {
    private final String title;
    private final List<String> tags;
    Article(String title, List<String> tags) {
        this.title = Objects.requireNonNull(title);
        this.tags = List.copyOf(tags);                // copy in (and unmodifiable)
    }
    String title() { return title; }
    List<String> tags() { return tags; }              // safe: already unmodifiable
    Article withTitle(String t) { return new Article(t, tags); }
}
Output
read-only
Intro [java]
Basics [java]
Unmodifiable view vs immutable copy
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> src = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2));
        List<Integer> view = Collections.unmodifiableList(src);   // a read-only window
        List<Integer> copy = List.copyOf(src);                    // an independent snapshot
        src.add(3);
        System.out.println(view);    // sees the change!
        System.out.println(copy);    // doesn't
        try {
            List.of(1, null);
        } catch (NullPointerException e) {
            System.out.println("List.of rejects nulls");
        }
    }
}
Output
[1, 2, 3]
[1, 2]
List.of rejects nulls
APICan you modify it?Reflects source changes?Nulls
Collections.unmodifiableList(src)No (UnsupportedOperationException)Yes — it's a viewAllowed
List.copyOf(src) / Set.copyOf / Map.copyOfNoNo — snapshotRejected
List.of(…) / Set.of / Map.ofNo—Rejected
stream().toList() (16+)NoNoAllowed
Collectors.toList()Yes (currently an ArrayList)NoAllowed
Why bother?

Thread-safe for free

No state changes → no races, no locks. Can be shared across threads freely.

Safe keys & set members

Hash code never changes, so the object never gets lost in a HashMap.

Cacheable & shareable

Integer.valueOf cache, the string pool and BigInteger.ZERO all rely on immutability.

Easier reasoning

Validate once in the constructor; the invariant holds forever. No defensive copies needed by callers.

The cost is allocating a new object for every change. For heavy step-by-step construction, pair the immutable class with a mutable builder (like String + StringBuilder).

How do you make a class immutable?

final class, private final fields, no setters, validate in the constructor, defensively copy mutable arguments in, and never hand out references to mutable internals (return copies or unmodifiable collections). Records do most of this for you, except the deep copying.

Why is String immutable?

So it can be pooled and shared (string literals), cache its hash code (fast HashMap keys), be used safely for security-sensitive values (class names, file paths, URLs) and be shared across threads without synchronization.

Collections.unmodifiableList vs List.copyOf?

unmodifiableList is a read-only view: you can't modify through it, but changes to the underlying list show through. List.copyOf makes an independent immutable snapshot (and rejects nulls).

Exceptions & Generics

Exceptions: Checked vs Unchecked

An exception is an object that signals something went wrong. Java splits them into checked exceptions the compiler forces you to handle, and unchecked ones it doesn't.

Exception — an instance of Throwable that is thrown to abort normal flow. The runtime unwinds the call stack until a matching catch is found; if none is, the thread dies and the stack trace is printed.

The hierarchy
Throwable
→
Error | Exception
→
RuntimeException (under Exception)
TypeChecked?MeaningExamples
