ErrorUncheckedSerious JVM/environment problems — don't catchOutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError, NoClassDefFoundError
Exception (not Runtime)CheckedRecoverable, expected conditions outside your controlIOException, FileNotFoundException, SQLException, InterruptedException
RuntimeExceptionUncheckedProgramming bugs / violated preconditionsNullPointerException, IllegalArgumentException, IndexOutOfBoundsException, ClassCastException, ArithmeticException
Key point

Checked = Exception and its subclasses except RuntimeException subclasses. The compiler requires them to be caught or declared with throws. Everything else (RuntimeException, Error) is unchecked.

throw vs throws
throwthrows
WhatA statement that throws one exception objectPart of a method signature listing exceptions it may throw
WhereMethod body: throw new IllegalStateException("closed");Declaration: void read() throws IOException
Needed forAny exceptionChecked exceptions (optional for unchecked)
Java
import java.io.IOException;

public class Main {
    static int parseAge(String s) {                        // unchecked: no throws needed
        int age = Integer.parseInt(s);                     // may throw NumberFormatException
        if (age < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("negative age: " + age);
        return age;
    }

    static String load(String name) throws IOException {  // checked: must be declared
        if (name.isEmpty()) throw new IOException("no file name");
        return "data of " + name;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        for (String s : new String[] {"42", "-1", "abc"}) {
            try {
                System.out.println(parseAge(s));
            } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {         // NumberFormatException is a subclass
                System.out.println(e.getClass().getSimpleName() + ": " + e.getMessage());
            }
        }
        try {
            System.out.println(load("a.txt"));
            System.out.println(load(""));
        } catch (IOException e) {
            System.out.println("IO problem: " + e.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
Output
42
IllegalArgumentException: negative age: -1
NumberFormatException: For input string: "abc"
data of a.txt
IO problem: no file name
Handle or declare

Calling a method that throws a checked exception without catching or declaring it doesn't compile:

Java
import java.nio.file.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String text = Files.readString(Path.of("notes.txt"));
        System.out.println(text);
    }
}
Compiler error
Main.java:5: error: unreported exception IOException; must be caught or declared to be thrown
        String text = Files.readString(Path.of("notes.txt"));
                                      ^
Stack unwinding
Java
public class Main {
    static void c() { System.out.println("c throws"); throw new IllegalStateException("boom"); }
    static void b() { System.out.println("b calls c"); c(); System.out.println("b after c"); }  // never printed
    static void a() {
        try {
            b();
        } catch (IllegalStateException e) {
            System.out.println("a caught " + e.getMessage());
            StackTraceElement top = e.getStackTrace()[0];
            System.out.println("thrown in " + top.getMethodName() + "()");
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        a();
        System.out.println("program continues");
    }
}
Output
b calls c
c throws
a caught boom
thrown in c()
program continues

The exception travels up from c through b (whose remaining code is skipped) until a's catch matches. An uncaught exception in main terminates the thread and prints Exception in thread "main" … with the stack trace to stderr.

Common exceptions to recognise
ExceptionTypical cause
NullPointerExceptionCalling a method / accessing a field on null (Java 14+ says which reference was null)
ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsExceptionarr[arr.length], negative index
ArithmeticExceptionInteger division by zero (5 / 0); 5.0 / 0 is Infinity, no exception
ClassCastException(String) someInteger at runtime
NumberFormatExceptionInteger.parseInt("12a")
ConcurrentModificationExceptionModifying a collection while iterating it with for-each
UnsupportedOperationExceptionAdding to List.of(…) or Arrays.asList(…)
IllegalStateExceptionMethod called at the wrong time (iterator remove twice, closed resource)
StackOverflowErrorInfinite / too-deep recursion
OutOfMemoryErrorHeap (or metaspace) exhausted
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Runnable[] bugs = {
            () -> System.out.println(10 / Integer.parseInt("0")),
            () -> java.util.List.of(1).add(2),
            () -> { Object o = "text"; Integer i = (Integer) o; System.out.println(i); },
            () -> { String s = null; System.out.println(s.length()); },
        };
        for (Runnable bug : bugs) {
            try {
                bug.run();
            } catch (RuntimeException e) {
                System.out.println(e.getClass().getSimpleName());
            }
        }
        try {
            int[] a = new int[3];
            a[3] = 1;
        } catch (ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException e) {
            System.out.println(e.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
Output
ArithmeticException
UnsupportedOperationException
ClassCastException
NullPointerException
Index 3 out of bounds for length 3
Watch out

Never catch Throwable or Error just to keep going — an OutOfMemoryError or StackOverflowError leaves the JVM in a doubtful state. Catch the most specific exception you can actually handle.

Checked vs unchecked exceptions?

Checked exceptions (Exception subclasses other than RuntimeException) must be caught or declared with throws — they model recoverable conditions like I/O failures. Unchecked (RuntimeException, Error) need not be declared — they usually indicate bugs (null, bad index, bad argument) or JVM failures.

Error vs Exception?

Error signals serious problems the application generally can't recover from (OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError). Exception signals conditions a program might reasonably catch and handle.

Is NullPointerException checked?

No — it extends RuntimeException. The compiler can't force you to handle it; prevent it with null checks, Objects.requireNonNull, Optional return types and not returning null from methods.

Exceptions & Generics

try/catch/finally & try-with-resources

try marks code that may fail, catch handles specific exceptions, finally always runs — and try-with-resources closes resources automatically, in reverse order, without losing exceptions.

Anatomy
Java
public class Main {
    static void run(String input) {
        try {
            System.out.println("parsing " + input);
            int n = Integer.parseInt(input);
            System.out.println("100 / n = " + 100 / n);
        } catch (NumberFormatException | ArithmeticException e) {   // multi-catch
            System.out.println("bad input: " + e.getClass().getSimpleName());
        } finally {
            System.out.println("finally");                          // always
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        run("4");
        run("0");
        run("x");
    }
}
Output
parsing 4
100 / n = 25
finally
parsing 0
bad input: ArithmeticException
finally
parsing x
bad input: NumberFormatException
finally
  • Catch blocks are tried top to bottom; the first matching type wins.
  • A subclass catch must come before its superclass — catch (Exception e) then catch (IOException e) doesn't compile (unreachable).
  • In a multi-catch A | B, the types can't be related by inheritance and e is implicitly final.
  • try needs at least one catch or a finally (or resources).
finally always runs — even after return
Java
public class Main {
    static int value() {
        try {
            return 1;
        } finally {
            System.out.println("finally runs before the method returns");
        }
    }

    @SuppressWarnings("finally")
    static int overridden() {
        try {
            throw new RuntimeException("lost!");
        } finally {
            return 2;              // discards the exception AND any earlier return
        }
    }

    static int snapshot() {
        int x = 10;
        try {
            return x;              // return value (10) is saved here
        } finally {
            x = 99;                // changing x doesn't affect it
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(value());
        System.out.println(overridden());
        System.out.println(snapshot());
    }
}
Output
finally runs before the method returns
1
2
10
Trap

Never return (or throw) from a finally block: it silently replaces the method's result and swallows any exception in flight. The only ways finally doesn't run are System.exit(), the JVM crashing, or the thread being killed.

try-with-resources

Any object implementing AutoCloseable (or Closeable) declared in the try (…) header is closed automatically when the block ends — normally or by exception. Resources close in reverse order of declaration.

Java
public class Main {
    record Res(String name) implements AutoCloseable {
        Res { System.out.println("open " + name); }
        @Override public void close() { System.out.println("close " + name); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        try (Res db = new Res("db"); Res file = new Res("file")) {
            System.out.println("working with " + db.name() + " and " + file.name());
            throw new IllegalStateException("failure");
        } catch (IllegalStateException e) {
            System.out.println("caught " + e.getMessage() + " (resources already closed)");
        } finally {
            System.out.println("finally");
        }
    }
}
Output
open db
open file
working with db and file
close file
close db
caught failure (resources already closed)
finally
Suppressed exceptions

If the body throws and close() throws, the body's exception is the one propagated; the close exception is attached to it as suppressed instead of replacing it (which is what a hand-written finally { close(); } would do).

Java
public class Main {
    static class Conn implements AutoCloseable {
        @Override public void close() { throw new IllegalStateException("close failed"); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        try (Conn c = new Conn()) {
            throw new RuntimeException("query failed");
        } catch (RuntimeException e) {
            System.out.println("primary: " + e.getMessage());
            for (Throwable s : e.getSuppressed())
                System.out.println("suppressed: " + s.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
Output
primary: query failed
suppressed: close failed
Before and after
Do

try (var in = Files.newBufferedReader(path)) { … } — closed on every path, exceptions preserved. Java 9+ also accepts an existing effectively-final variable: try (in) { … }.

Avoid

Manual finally { if (in != null) in.close(); } — verbose, close() needs its own try/catch, and an exception from close() hides the real one.

Does finally run if there's a return in try?

Yes. The return value is computed and saved, finally runs, then the method returns. If finally itself returns, that value wins and any pending exception is discarded — so never return from finally.

When does finally NOT execute?

When the JVM stops: System.exit(), a crash / kill -9, or power loss. (Also if the try never completes, e.g. an infinite loop, or a daemon thread killed at JVM exit.)

What are suppressed exceptions?

In try-with-resources, if the body throws and then close() also throws, the close exception is added to the primary one via addSuppressed and can be read with getSuppressed(), so no failure is lost.

In what order are resources closed?

Reverse order of declaration — the last opened is closed first, like a stack — and before any catch or finally block of that statement runs.

Exceptions & Generics

Custom Exceptions & Best Practices

Define your own exception types when callers need to react to a specific failure — and always keep the original cause attached.

Defining one
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Account acc = new Account(100);
        try {
            acc.withdraw(30);
            acc.withdraw(500);
        } catch (InsufficientFundsException e) {
            System.out.println(e.getMessage());
            System.out.println("short by " + e.shortfall());
        }
    }
}

// Checked: the caller can reasonably recover (show a message, offer a smaller amount)
class InsufficientFundsException extends Exception {
    private final long shortfall;
    InsufficientFundsException(long balance, long requested) {
        super("balance " + balance + " < requested " + requested);
        this.shortfall = requested - balance;
    }
    long shortfall() { return shortfall; }
}

class Account {
    private long balance;
    Account(long balance) { this.balance = balance; }
    void withdraw(long amount) throws InsufficientFundsException {
        if (amount <= 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("amount must be positive");  // bug → unchecked
        if (amount > balance) throw new InsufficientFundsException(balance, amount);
        balance -= amount;
    }
}
Output
balance 70 < requested 500
short by 430
Checked or unchecked?
ChooseWhenExtend
CheckedThe caller can and should recover: file missing, payment declined, validation of external inputException
UncheckedProgramming errors / violated preconditions, or failures nobody nearby can handleRuntimeException

Modern code (Spring, most libraries, lambdas and streams) leans unchecked: checked exceptions don't compose with functional interfaces like Function, and they leak implementation details up the call chain.

Exception chaining: keep the cause
Java
public class Main {
    static class ConfigException extends RuntimeException {
        ConfigException(String msg, Throwable cause) { super(msg, cause); }
    }

    static int loadPort(String raw) {
        try {
            return Integer.parseInt(raw);
        } catch (NumberFormatException e) {
            throw new ConfigException("invalid port in config: " + raw, e);   // wrap, keep cause
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        try {
            loadPort("80a");
        } catch (ConfigException e) {
            System.out.println(e.getMessage());
            System.out.println("caused by " + e.getCause().getClass().getSimpleName()
                               + ": " + e.getCause().getMessage());
        }
    }
}
Output
invalid port in config: 80a
caused by NumberFormatException: For input string: "80a"

Translate low-level exceptions into ones that match your layer's abstraction (a repository throws DataAccessException, not SQLException) — but pass the original as the cause so the stack trace still shows the root problem (Caused by: …).

Anti-patterns
Anti-patternWhy it hurtsInstead
catch (Exception e) {}Swallows the failure — the bug disappears silentlyHandle it, or let it propagate
e.printStackTrace() and continueOutput goes to stderr, program runs on with bad stateLog with a logger and rethrow/handle
throw new RuntimeException(e.getMessage())Loses the original type and stack tracethrow new XException("context", e)
Catch-log-rethrow at every layerThe same error is logged five timesLog once, where it's handled
Exceptions for control flowSlow (stack trace capture) and hides intentUse if, Optional, return values
throws Exception on every methodCallers can't tell what can go wrongDeclare specific types
Catching NullPointerExceptionMasks a bugFix the null source / validate early
Best practices
  • Fail fast: validate arguments at the start of a method (Objects.requireNonNull, IllegalArgumentException).
  • Use standard exceptions when they fit: IllegalArgumentException, IllegalStateException, UnsupportedOperationException, NoSuchElementException.
  • Put useful context in the message: the bad value, the id, the expected range.
  • Name custom types …Exception and provide (String message) and (String message, Throwable cause) constructors.
  • Restore the interrupt flag if you catch InterruptedException and can't rethrow it: Thread.currentThread().interrupt();.
  • Clean up with try-with-resources, not finally blocks.
How do you create a custom exception?

Extend Exception (checked) or RuntimeException (unchecked), add constructors that call super(message) and super(message, cause), and optionally add fields carrying extra context (an error code, the invalid value).

What is exception chaining and why use it?

Wrapping a caught exception as the cause of a new, higher-level one (new ServiceException("…", e)). The API stays at the right abstraction level while the full root cause remains available via getCause() and in the Caused by: part of the stack trace.

Why are exceptions expensive?

Creating one captures the whole stack trace (fillInStackTrace), which walks the stack. That's fine for exceptional cases but wasteful as normal control flow in a hot loop.

Exceptions & Generics

Generics: Classes, Methods & Bounded Types

Generics let a class or method work with any type while the compiler still checks types — no casts, and type errors show up at compile time instead of as ClassCastException at runtime.

Type parameter — a placeholder type such as T in class Box<T>. Using the class with a concrete type argument, Box<String>, makes every T mean String for the compiler.

Why generics?
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    @SuppressWarnings({"rawtypes", "unchecked"})
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List raw = new ArrayList();            // pre-Java-5 style: a list of Object
        raw.add("hello");
        raw.add(42);                           // compiler can't stop this
        try {
            for (Object o : raw) {
                String s = (String) o;         // manual cast, fails at runtime
                System.out.println(s.toUpperCase());
            }
        } catch (ClassCastException e) {
            System.out.println("ClassCastException at runtime");
        }

        List<String> typed = new ArrayList<>();
        typed.add("hello");
        // typed.add(42);                      // compile error instead
        String s = typed.get(0);               // no cast needed
        System.out.println(s.length());
    }
}
Output
HELLO
ClassCastException at runtime
5
Generic classes
Java
public class Main {
    static class Box<T> {
        private T value;
        Box(T value) { this.value = value; }
        T get() { return value; }
        <R> Box<R> map(java.util.function.Function<T, R> f) { return new Box<>(f.apply(value)); }
    }

    record Pair<A, B>(A first, B second) {
        Pair<B, A> swap() { return new Pair<>(second, first); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Box<String> name = new Box<>("generics");      // diamond: type inferred
        Box<Integer> len = name.map(String::length);
        System.out.println(name.get() + " " + len.get());

        Pair<String, Integer> p = new Pair<>("age", 30);
        System.out.println(p.swap());
        var q = new Pair<>(1.5, true);                  // Pair<Double, Boolean>
        System.out.println(q.first() + " " + q.second());
    }
}
Output
generics 8
Pair[first=30, second=age]
1.5 true
ConventionStands for
T, U, VType (first, second, third)
EElement (collections)
K, VKey, Value (maps)
RReturn / result type
NNumber
Generic methods
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    static <T> T firstOr(List<T> list, T fallback) {       // <T> before the return type
        return list.isEmpty() ? fallback : list.get(0);
    }

    static <K, V> Map<V, K> invert(Map<K, V> map) {
        Map<V, K> out = new TreeMap<>();
        map.forEach((k, v) -> out.put(v, k));
        return out;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(firstOr(List.of("a", "b"), "none"));
        System.out.println(firstOr(List.<Integer>of(), -1));     // explicit type witness
        System.out.println(invert(Map.of("one", 1, "two", 2)));
    }
}
Output
a
-1
{1=one, 2=two}
Bounded type parameters

<T extends Bound> restricts T to Bound or its subtypes — and in return lets you call Bound's methods on a T. (extends is used for both classes and interfaces; multiple bounds: <T extends Number & Comparable<T>>, class first.)

Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    static <T extends Comparable<T>> T max(List<T> items) {
        T best = items.get(0);
        for (T item : items)
            if (item.compareTo(best) > 0) best = item;   // compareTo allowed thanks to the bound
        return best;
    }

    static <N extends Number> double sum(List<N> nums) {
        double total = 0;
        for (N n : nums) total += n.doubleValue();       // Number's method
        return total;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(max(List.of(3, 9, 4)));
        System.out.println(max(List.of("pear", "apple", "zucchini")));
        System.out.println(sum(List.of(1, 2.5, 3L)));
    }
}
Output
9
zucchini
6.5
Primitives and restrictions
  • Type arguments must be reference types: List<int> is illegal — use List<Integer> (autoboxing does the conversion).
  • A static field or static method can't use the class's T (there's one static copy for all Box<X>), though a static method can declare its own <T>.
  • You can't create new T(), new T[n], or new List<String>[n] — see type erasure in the next topic.
Raw types

Raw types (List instead of List<String>) exist only for pre-Java-5 compatibility. They switch off generic type checking for that variable and produce rawtypes/unchecked warnings. Never use them in new code; use List<?> if you really mean "a list of unknown type".

What are generics and why use them?

Parameterised types that let one class/method work with many types while keeping compile-time type safety. Benefits: errors caught at compile time, no explicit casts, and reusable, self-documenting APIs (Map<String, List<Order>>).

What does <T extends Comparable<T>> mean?

T can be any type that is comparable to itself, so the method may call a.compareTo(b) on two Ts. The fully flexible version used by the JDK is <T extends Comparable<? super T>>, which also accepts types whose compareTo is inherited from a supertype.

What is the diamond operator?

<> in new ArrayList<>() (Java 7+): the compiler infers the type arguments from the context, so you don't repeat ArrayList<String>.

Exceptions & Generics

Wildcards, PECS & Type Erasure

List<Integer> is not a List<Number>. Wildcards (?, ? extends, ? super) restore flexibility, PECS tells you which to use, and type erasure explains generics' odd limitations.

Generics are invariant

Even though Integer is a Number, List<Integer> is not a subtype of List<Number>. If it were, you could add a Double to a list of integers through the List<Number> reference.

Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> ints = new ArrayList<>();
        List<Number> nums = ints;          // not allowed
        nums.add(3.14);
    }
}
Compiler error
Main.java:6: error: incompatible types: List<Integer> cannot be converted to List<Number>
        List<Number> nums = ints;          // not allowed
                            ^

Arrays are covariant (Integer[] is an Object[]), so the same mistake compiles and only fails at runtime:

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Object[] objs = new Integer[2];      // compiles: arrays are covariant
        objs[0] = 1;
        try {
            objs[1] = "text";                // runtime type check
        } catch (ArrayStoreException e) {
            System.out.println("ArrayStoreException: " + e.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
Output
ArrayStoreException: java.lang.String
The three wildcards
WildcardAcceptsRead asCan add?
List<?>A list of any typeObjectOnly null
List<? extends Number>List<Number>, List<Integer>, List<Double>…NumberNo (except null)
List<? super Integer>List<Integer>, List<Number>, List<Object>ObjectYes, Integers
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    static double sum(List<? extends Number> nums) {    // producer: we only read
        double s = 0;
        for (Number n : nums) s += n.doubleValue();
        return s;
    }

    static void fill(List<? super Integer> sink, int n) { // consumer: we only write
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) sink.add(i);
    }

    static int count(Collection<?> anything) { return anything.size(); }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(sum(List.of(1, 2, 3)));         // List<Integer>
        System.out.println(sum(List.of(1.5, 2.5)));        // List<Double>

        List<Number> numbers = new ArrayList<>();
        List<Object> objects = new ArrayList<>();
        fill(numbers, 3);
        fill(objects, 2);
        System.out.println(numbers + " " + objects);
        System.out.println(count(Set.of("a", "b")));
    }
}
Output
6.0
4.0
[1, 2, 3] [1, 2]
2
PECS: Producer Extends, Consumer Super
If a parameter produces Ts for you → ? extends T. If it consumes Ts → ? super T. Both → plain T.
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    // src produces T (extends), dest consumes T (super) — same shape as Collections.copy
    static <T> void copy(List<? super T> dest, List<? extends T> src) {
        for (T item : src) dest.add(item);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> ints = List.of(1, 2);
        List<Double> doubles = List.of(3.5);
        List<Number> all = new ArrayList<>();
        copy(all, ints);                      // T = Integer, Number is a super of it
        copy(all, doubles);                   // T = Double
        System.out.println(all);

        List<Object> objs = new ArrayList<>(List.of("x"));
        Main.<Integer>copy(objs, ints);
        System.out.println(objs);
    }
}
Output
[1, 2, 3.5]
[x, 1, 2]

JDK examples: Collections.copy(List<? super T> dest, List<? extends T> src), addAll(Collection<? extends E>), sort(Comparator<? super E>) — a Comparator<Object> can sort a list of strings.

Type erasure

Type erasure — generics exist only at compile time. The compiler checks types, inserts casts, then replaces each type parameter with its bound (Object if unbounded). At runtime, List<String> and List<Integer> are the same class, ArrayList.

Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<String> a = new ArrayList<>();
        List<Integer> b = new ArrayList<>();
        System.out.println(a.getClass() == b.getClass());
        System.out.println(a.getClass().getName());
        Object o = a;
        System.out.println(o instanceof List<?>);      // OK: unbounded wildcard
        // o instanceof List<String>                   // error: can't check a type argument at runtime
    }
}
Output
true
java.util.ArrayList
true
You can't…Because at runtime T is erasedWorkaround
new T()The JVM doesn't know what T isPass a Supplier<T> or Class<T>
new T[n], new List<String>[n]Arrays need their exact element type at runtimeUse a List<T>, or (T[]) new Object[n] with care
x instanceof List<String>Type arguments aren't storedinstanceof List<?>
Overload f(List<String>) and f(List<Integer>)Both erase to f(List) — same signatureUse different method names
catch (T e) / generic exception classesCatch needs a runtime type—
Use primitives as type argumentsErasure turns T into ObjectBoxed types, or specialised streams (IntStream)
Java
import java.util.*;
import java.util.function.Supplier;

public class Main {
    static <T> List<T> nCopies(int n, Supplier<T> factory) {    // instead of new T()
        List<T> out = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) out.add(factory.get());
        return out;
    }

    static <T> T create(Class<T> type) throws ReflectiveOperationException {
        return type.getDeclaredConstructor().newInstance();
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        System.out.println(nCopies(3, StringBuilder::new).size());
        System.out.println(create(ArrayList.class).isEmpty());
    }
}
Output
3
true
Bridge methods and heap pollution

When a class implements Comparable<Money>, its compareTo(Money) erases differently from the interface's compareTo(Object). The compiler generates a synthetic bridge method compareTo(Object) that casts and delegates, so polymorphism still works after erasure.

Java
import java.lang.reflect.Method;

public class Main {
    record Money(long cents) implements Comparable<Money> {
        public int compareTo(Money o) { return Long.compare(cents, o.cents); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        java.util.List<String> found = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
        for (Method m : Money.class.getDeclaredMethods())
            if (m.getName().equals("compareTo"))
                found.add("compareTo(" + m.getParameterTypes()[0].getSimpleName() + ")"
                          + (m.isBridge() ? " <- bridge" : ""));
        java.util.Collections.sort(found);
        found.forEach(System.out::println);
    }
}
Output
compareTo(Money)
compareTo(Object) <- bridge

Heap pollution happens when a variable of a parameterised type refers to an object that isn't of that type — typically through raw types, unchecked casts or generic varargs (T... creates a T[]). The compiler warns (unchecked); @SafeVarargs marks a varargs method that doesn't misuse its array.

Watch out

An unchecked warning means the compiler can no longer guarantee type safety: a ClassCastException may appear later, far from the real bug, at a cast the compiler inserted. Don't suppress it unless you can prove the cast is safe.

What is PECS?

Producer Extends, Consumer Super: use ? extends T for a parameter you read Ts from, ? super T for one you write Ts into. E.g. copy(List<? super T> dest, List<? extends T> src).

Why is List<Integer> not a subtype of List<Number>?

Generics are invariant for type safety: if it were, you could write List<Number> n = ints; n.add(2.5); and corrupt a list of integers. Use List<? extends Number> when you only need to read.

What is type erasure and what are its consequences?

The compiler removes type arguments after checking them, replacing type variables with their bounds and inserting casts. So generic type info isn't available at runtime: no new T(), no generic arrays, no instanceof List<String>, and methods differing only by type argument clash.

List<?> vs List<Object>?

List<Object> is a list you may add any object to, and only a List<Object> can be passed. List<?> accepts a list of any element type, but you can only read Objects from it and add nothing but null.

Collections Framework

Collections Framework Overview

A unified set of interfaces (List, Set, Queue, Map) and ready-made implementations — pick the interface for the behaviour you need, then the class for the performance you need.

Java Collections Framework (JCF) — the java.util interfaces, implementations and algorithms for storing groups of objects. Collections hold references to objects only — primitives are autoboxed (List<Integer>, never List<int>).

The hierarchy
Iterable<T>
→
Collection<E>
→
List / Set / Queue
  • Iterable — anything usable in a for-each loop (iterator(), forEach).
  • Collection — add, remove, contains, size, isEmpty, stream(), removeIf.
  • List — ordered, index-based, duplicates allowed: ArrayList, LinkedList, (Vector, Stack — legacy).
  • Set — no duplicates: HashSet, LinkedHashSet, TreeSet (SortedSet → NavigableSet).
  • Queue / Deque — ends-oriented: ArrayDeque, LinkedList, PriorityQueue.
  • Map<K, V> — key → value pairs. Not a Collection: HashMap, LinkedHashMap, TreeMap, (Hashtable — legacy).
Key point

Map does not extend Collection — a map is a set of entries, not of elements. You view it as collections through keySet(), values() and entrySet().

Choosing a collection
NeedUseWhy
Ordered list, fast random accessArrayListArray-backed, O(1) get(i), cache-friendly
Stack or queueArrayDequeO(1) at both ends, faster than Stack / LinkedList
Unique elements, no orderHashSetO(1) average add/contains
Unique, insertion orderLinkedHashSetHash lookup + linked list of entries
Unique, sorted / range queriesTreeSetRed-black tree, O(log n), floor/ceiling
Key → value lookupHashMapO(1) average
Keys in insertion / access order, LRULinkedHashMapPredictable iteration, removeEldestEntry
Sorted keys, nearest-key queriesTreeMapO(log n), floorKey, subMap
Always get the min / max nextPriorityQueueBinary heap: O(log n) offer/poll, O(1) peek
Thread-safe mapConcurrentHashMapFine-grained locking, no nulls
Program to the interface
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<String> names = new ArrayList<>();   // declare the interface type
        names.add("Asha");
        names.add("Ravi");
        names.add("Asha");                        // duplicates are fine in a List
        System.out.println(names + " size=" + names.size());

        Set<String> unique = new TreeSet<>(names); // copy constructor: every collection has one
        System.out.println(unique);

        Map<String, Integer> ages = new HashMap<>();
        ages.put("Asha", 30);
        ages.put("Asha", 31);                      // same key: value replaced
        System.out.println(ages);
    }
}
Output
[Asha, Ravi, Asha] size=3
[Asha, Ravi]
{Asha=31}

Declaring List<String> names (not ArrayList<String>) lets you swap the implementation later without touching the callers.

Immutable factories: List.of, Set.of, Map.of (Java 9+)
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> nums = List.of(3, 1, 2);
        Map<String, Integer> m = Map.of("a", 1);
        System.out.println(nums + " " + m);
        try {
            nums.add(4);
        } catch (UnsupportedOperationException e) {
            System.out.println("add -> UnsupportedOperationException");
        }
        try {
            List.of("x", null);
        } catch (NullPointerException e) {
            System.out.println("null element -> NullPointerException");
        }
        try {
            Set.of(1, 1);
        } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {
            System.out.println("duplicate -> " + e.getMessage());
        }
        List<Integer> copy = List.copyOf(new ArrayList<>(nums)); // immutable snapshot
        System.out.println(copy);
    }
}
Output
[3, 1, 2] {a=1}
add -> UnsupportedOperationException
null element -> NullPointerException
duplicate -> duplicate element: 1
[3, 1, 2]
List.of(...)Arrays.asList(...)Collections.unmodifiableList(l)
Add / removeThrowsThrows (fixed size)Throws
set(i, x)ThrowsAllowed (writes through to the array)Throws
null elementsRejectedAllowedAllowed
Sees later changes to the source?No sourceYes (backed by the array)Yes — it is only a read-only view
Watch out

Set.of and Map.of have a deliberately randomised iteration order that can change between JVM runs — never rely on it. Map.of accepts up to 10 pairs; use Map.ofEntries(Map.entry(k, v), …) for more.

Legacy classes
LegacyProblemModern replacement
VectorEvery method synchronized — slow even single-threadedArrayList (or CopyOnWriteArrayList)
StackExtends Vector; exposes get(i), insertElementAtArrayDeque (push/pop/peek)
HashtableGlobal lock, no null keys/valuesHashMap / ConcurrentHashMap
EnumerationNo remove, clunky namesIterator
Why doesn't Map extend Collection?

A Collection is a group of single elements with add(E). A map stores pairs and its core operation is put(K, V); forcing it into Collection would give methods with no sensible meaning. Instead a map exposes three collection views: keySet(), values(), entrySet().

Collection vs Collections?

Collection is the root interface of lists, sets and queues. Collections is a utility class of static helpers — sort, reverse, unmodifiableList, synchronizedMap, emptyList… (Similarly Array vs Arrays.)

Why can't a collection hold int?

Generics work only with reference types (type erasure turns T into Object). Primitives are autoboxed to Integer etc. — which costs memory and time. For huge numeric data use arrays or IntStream.

Collections Framework

List: ArrayList vs LinkedList

ArrayList is a resizable array and is the right default ~99% of the time; LinkedList is a doubly-linked list that only wins when you add/remove at an iterator's position.

List — an ordered collection with positional access (get(i), set(i, x), add(i, x), remove(i)), indexOf, and duplicates allowed.

How ArrayList grows
  • Backed by an Object[] elementData; size counts the slots actually used.
  • new ArrayList<>() starts with an empty shared array; the first add allocates capacity 10.
  • When full, it grows to old + old/2 (1.5×) and copies with Arrays.copyOf — O(n) once, so add is amortised O(1).
  • Pre-size with new ArrayList<>(expected) or ensureCapacity(n) to avoid repeated copying; trimToSize() releases the slack.
capacity: 10 → 15 → 22 → 33 → 49 → 73 → …
Complexity
OperationArrayListLinkedList
get(i) / set(i, x)O(1)O(n) — walks from the nearer end
add(x) at the endO(1) amortisedO(1)
add(0, x) / remove(0)O(n) — shifts elementsO(1)
Insert/remove in the middleO(n) shiftO(n) to find + O(1) to link
contains / indexOfO(n)O(n)
Memory per elementone reference (4–8 bytes)node object: item + prev + next + header (~24–40 bytes)
CPU cacheContiguous — very fast to scanPointer chasing — slow
Key point

Even the “LinkedList wins at insert-in-the-middle” case usually loses in practice: finding the position is O(n) pointer-chasing, while ArrayList shifts with a single fast System.arraycopy. For a queue or stack use ArrayDeque, not LinkedList.

Everyday operations
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<String> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of("b", "c"));
        list.add(0, "a");               // insert at index
        list.add("d");
        list.set(3, "D");               // replace
        System.out.println(list + " get(1)=" + list.get(1));
        System.out.println(list.indexOf("c") + " " + list.contains("z"));
        list.remove("a");               // by value
        System.out.println(list);

        LinkedList<Integer> linked = new LinkedList<>(List.of(2, 3));
        linked.addFirst(1);
        linked.addLast(4);
        System.out.println(linked + " first=" + linked.getFirst() + " last=" + linked.peekLast());
    }
}
Output
[a, b, c, D] get(1)=b
2 false
[b, c, D]
[1, 2, 3, 4] first=1 last=4
Trap: remove(int) vs remove(Object)
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> nums = new ArrayList<>(List.of(10, 20, 30, 1));
        nums.remove(1);                     // remove(int index) — removes 20!
        System.out.println(nums);
        nums.remove(Integer.valueOf(1));    // remove(Object) — removes the value 1
        System.out.println(nums);
    }
}
Output
[10, 30, 1]
[10, 30]
Watch out

With a List<Integer>, remove(1) picks the more specific overload remove(int index) — no boxing needed — so it deletes by position. Pass Integer.valueOf(x) (or (Integer) x) to delete by value.

subList is a view
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of(0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5));
        List<Integer> mid = list.subList(1, 4);   // [1, 4) — a live window
        mid.set(0, 99);
        System.out.println(list);
        list.subList(0, 2).clear();               // idiom: delete a range
        System.out.println(list);
        try {
            System.out.println(mid);              // parent was structurally modified
        } catch (ConcurrentModificationException e) {
            System.out.println("stale subList -> ConcurrentModificationException");
        }
    }
}
Output
[0, 99, 2, 3, 4, 5]
[2, 3, 4, 5]
stale subList -> ConcurrentModificationException
Watch out

A subList writes through to its parent and becomes invalid once the parent is structurally changed through any other path. Copy it (new ArrayList<>(list.subList(a, b))) if you need to keep it.

ArrayList vs LinkedList — when would you pick LinkedList?

Almost never. Only when you do many insertions/removals at an iterator's current position (ListIterator.add/remove) on a long list, or need the Deque methods on a list. Random access is O(n), and each node costs ~3–5× the memory. ArrayDeque beats it as a queue.

What is the default capacity of ArrayList and how does it grow?

Capacity 10 allocated lazily on the first add (the no-arg constructor starts with an empty array). When full it grows by 50% (newCapacity = old + (old >> 1)) and copies the array, giving amortised O(1) appends.

ArrayList vs Vector?

Both are resizable arrays. Vector synchronises every method (slow, and still not safe for compound actions like check-then-add) and doubles its size when growing; ArrayList is unsynchronised and grows by 1.5×. Use ArrayList, or CopyOnWriteArrayList / Collections.synchronizedList for threads.

Collections Framework

Set: HashSet, LinkedHashSet & TreeSet

A Set stores each element at most once. The three implementations differ only in ordering and speed: hash (no order), linked hash (insertion order), tree (sorted).

Set — a collection with no duplicate elements — add returns false if an equal element is already present. “Equal” means equals/hashCode for hash sets and compareTo/compare == 0 for TreeSet.

Comparison
HashSetLinkedHashSetTreeSet
Backed bya HashMap (elements are keys)a LinkedHashMapa TreeMap (red-black tree)
Iteration orderUnspecifiedInsertion orderSorted (natural or Comparator)
add / remove / containsO(1) averageO(1) averageO(log n)
null elementOne allowedOne allowedNo (NullPointerException)
Needsequals + hashCodeequals + hashCodeComparable or a Comparator
Extra API—reversed(), getFirst() (Java 21)first, floor, ceiling, headSet, subSet…
Same input, three orders
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> input = List.of(42, 7, 19, 7, 3, 42);
        System.out.println("HashSet       " + new HashSet<>(input));
        System.out.println("LinkedHashSet " + new LinkedHashSet<>(input));
        System.out.println("TreeSet       " + new TreeSet<>(input));

        Set<String> seen = new HashSet<>();
        System.out.println(seen.add("x") + " " + seen.add("x"));   // second add is rejected
    }
}
Output
HashSet       [19, 3, 7, 42]
LinkedHashSet [42, 7, 19, 3]
TreeSet       [3, 7, 19, 42]
true false
Key point

HashSet's order above comes from the bucket index (hash & (capacity − 1): 42→10, 7→7, 19→3, 3→3 with 16 buckets). It is an implementation detail that changes as the set resizes — never depend on it.

TreeSet as a NavigableSet
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        TreeSet<Integer> ts = new TreeSet<>(List.of(10, 20, 30, 40, 50));
        System.out.println(ts.first() + " " + ts.last());
        System.out.println(ts.floor(25) + " " + ts.ceiling(25));   // <= 25, >= 25
        System.out.println(ts.lower(20) + " " + ts.higher(20));    // < 20, > 20
        System.out.println(ts.floor(5));                           // none -> null
        System.out.println(ts.headSet(30) + " " + ts.tailSet(30)); // [..30) and [30..]
        System.out.println(ts.subSet(20, true, 40, true));
        System.out.println(ts.descendingSet());
        System.out.println(ts.pollFirst() + " -> " + ts);
    }
}
Output
10 50
20 30
10 30
null
[10, 20] [30, 40, 50]
[20, 30, 40]
[50, 40, 30, 20, 10]
10 -> [20, 30, 40, 50]
Trap: forgetting equals/hashCode
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Set<Point> raw = new HashSet<>();
        raw.add(new Point(1, 2));
        raw.add(new Point(1, 2));
        System.out.println("class without equals/hashCode: " + raw.size());

        Set<PointRec> rec = new HashSet<>();
        rec.add(new PointRec(1, 2));
        rec.add(new PointRec(1, 2));
        System.out.println("record: " + rec.size());
    }
}

class Point {                        // uses Object's identity equals/hashCode
    final int x, y;
    Point(int x, int y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }
}

record PointRec(int x, int y) {}     // records generate equals/hashCode from the fields
Output
class without equals/hashCode: 2
record: 1
Watch out

TreeSet ignores equals entirely and treats compare(a, b) == 0 as “duplicate”. A comparator that only compares by, say, length will silently drop "cat" when "dog" is already present.

Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Set<String> byLength = new TreeSet<>(Comparator.comparingInt(String::length));
        byLength.addAll(List.of("dog", "cat", "horse"));
        System.out.println(byLength);       // "cat" looked like a duplicate of "dog"
    }
}
Output
[dog, horse]
How does HashSet guarantee uniqueness?

It is a wrapper around a HashMap whose keys are the elements and whose values are one shared dummy PRESENT object. add(e) is map.put(e, PRESENT) == null — so uniqueness is exactly HashMap key uniqueness: same bucket via hashCode, then equals.

HashSet vs TreeSet — which and why?

HashSet for fast O(1) membership when order doesn't matter. TreeSet when you need sorted iteration or range/nearest queries (floor, ceiling, subSet) at O(log n), and it requires comparable elements and rejects null.

Can you add null to a TreeSet?

Not with natural ordering — compareTo on null throws NullPointerException. It works only with a comparator that handles nulls, e.g. Comparator.nullsFirst(Comparator.naturalOrder()).

Collections Framework

HashMap Internals

An array of buckets indexed by the key's hash: O(1) average get/put, as long as hashCode spreads keys well and keys never change while inside.

HashMap — a hash table storing Node<K,V> entries (hash, key, value, next) in an array table. Allows one null key and null values; not thread-safe; iteration order unspecified.

How put(k, v) works
1
Hash the key
h = key.hashCode(), then spread it: h ^ (h >>> 16) so the high bits influence the bucket too. A null key always hashes to 0.
2
Pick the bucket
index = (n - 1) & hash — n (the table length) is always a power of two, so this is a fast hash % n.
3
Empty bucket?
Store a new node there.
4
Collision
Walk the bucket: if a node has the same hash and (== or equals) key, replace its value and return the old one. Otherwise append a new node.
5
Treeify
If one bucket reaches 8 nodes (and the table has at least 64 buckets) the list becomes a red-black tree → worst case O(log n) instead of O(n). It turns back into a list at 6.
6
Resize
If size > capacity × loadFactor (16 × 0.75 = 12 by default), the table doubles and entries are split between index i and i + oldCap.
ParameterDefaultMeaning
Initial capacity16 (allocated lazily on first put)Always rounded up to a power of two
Load factor0.75Time/space trade-off: resize when 75% full
TREEIFY_THRESHOLD8Bucket list → tree (Java 8+)
UNTREEIFY_THRESHOLD6Tree → list after shrinking
MIN_TREEIFY_CAPACITY64Below this, resize instead of treeifying
Seeing the bucket index
Java
public class Main {
    static int spread(Object key) {
        int h;
        return (key == null) ? 0 : (h = key.hashCode()) ^ (h >>> 16);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int n = 16;
        for (Object k : new Object[]{"Aa", "BB", 17, 33, null}) {
            int h = spread(k);
            System.out.println(k + " hashCode=" + (k == null ? 0 : k.hashCode()) + " bucket=" + ((n - 1) & h));
        }
    }
}
Output
Aa hashCode=2112 bucket=0
BB hashCode=2112 bucket=0
17 hashCode=17 bucket=1
33 hashCode=33 bucket=1
null hashCode=0 bucket=0
Key point

"Aa" and "BB" have the same hashCode (2112) — a classic collision. Both still live in the map: the bucket holds a chain, and equals tells them apart.

The modern Map API
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Map<String, Integer> count = new TreeMap<>();     // TreeMap only to print in sorted order
        for (String w : "to be or not to be".split(" ")) {
            count.merge(w, 1, Integer::sum);             // insert 1, or add 1 to the old value
        }
        System.out.println(count);
        System.out.println(count.getOrDefault("xyz", 0));

        Map<String, List<String>> groups = new TreeMap<>();
        for (String w : List.of("apple", "avocado", "banana")) {
            groups.computeIfAbsent(w.substring(0, 1), k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(w);
        }
        System.out.println(groups);

        System.out.println(count.putIfAbsent("be", 100) + " " + count.putIfAbsent("new", 100));
        count.computeIfPresent("or", (k, v) -> null);   // returning null removes the entry
        System.out.println(count);

        for (Map.Entry<String, Integer> e : count.entrySet()) {
            if (e.getValue() > 1) System.out.print(e.getKey() + " ");
        }
        System.out.println();
    }
}
Output
{be=2, not=1, or=1, to=2}
0
{a=[apple, avocado], b=[banana]}
2 null
{be=2, new=100, not=1, to=2}
be new to
MethodDoes
getOrDefault(k, d)Value, or d if absent
putIfAbsent(k, v)Put only if absent; returns the existing value (or null)
computeIfAbsent(k, fn)Create the value lazily — perfect for multimaps Map<K, List<V>>
computeIfPresent(k, fn)Update an existing value; null result removes it
merge(k, v, fn)Put v, or combine with the old value — counters: merge(k, 1, Integer::sum)
entrySet()Iterate keys and values together — cheaper than keySet() + get
Trap: mutating a key
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Map<List<Integer>, String> map = new HashMap<>();
        List<Integer> key = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2));
        map.put(key, "found");
        key.add(3);                                  // hashCode of the key changes
        System.out.println(map.get(key));            // looks in the wrong bucket
        System.out.println(map.get(List.of(1, 2)));  // right bucket, but equals fails
        System.out.println(map.size());              // the entry is stuck in the map
    }
}
Output
null
null
1
Watch out

Keys must be effectively immutable while they are in a HashMap/HashSet. String, Integer and records of immutable fields are ideal keys.

equals / hashCode contract
  • If a.equals(b) then a.hashCode() == b.hashCode() — required. Break it and lookups miss.
  • Equal hash codes do not imply equals (collisions are legal, just slower).
  • Override both or neither. hashCode must be stable while the object is used as a key.
What happens on a hash collision in HashMap?

Both entries go into the same bucket: as a linked list (new node appended at the tail since Java 8), or as a red-black tree once the bucket holds 8+ nodes and capacity ≥ 64. Lookup compares hash, then ==/equals on each node in the bucket.

Why is HashMap capacity always a power of two?

So the bucket index can be computed with a cheap bitmask (n - 1) & hash instead of %, and so on resize each entry either stays at index i or moves to i + oldCap (decided by one bit). The h ^ (h >>> 16) spreading compensates for only the low bits being used.

HashMap vs Hashtable vs ConcurrentHashMap?

HashMap: unsynchronised, one null key and null values allowed. Hashtable: legacy, every method synchronised on one lock, no nulls. ConcurrentHashMap: thread-safe with CAS + per-bin locking, lock-free reads, no nulls (so get returning null unambiguously means absent).

What is the time complexity of HashMap get?

O(1) average. Worst case O(n) for a bucket that is a list (all keys colliding), improved to O(log n) since Java 8 when the bucket is treeified (keys that are Comparable get the full benefit).

Collections Framework

LinkedHashMap & TreeMap

LinkedHashMap = HashMap + a doubly-linked list for predictable order (and a two-line LRU cache). TreeMap = a red-black tree for sorted keys and nearest-key queries.

Map implementations compared
HashMapLinkedHashMapTreeMapHashtable
OrderNoneInsertion (or access)Sorted by keyNone
get / putO(1) avgO(1) avgO(log n)O(1) avg
null keyOneOneNo (natural order)No
null valuesYesYesYesNo
Thread-safeNoNoNoYes (coarse lock)
Key requirementequals/hashCodeequals/hashCodeComparable/Comparatorequals/hashCode
Use forDefault mapStable order, LRU cachesSorted output, ranges, floor/ceilingNothing new — legacy
LinkedHashMap orders
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Map<String, Integer> ins = new LinkedHashMap<>();
        ins.put("banana", 2);
        ins.put("apple", 1);
        ins.put("cherry", 3);
        ins.put("banana", 20);          // re-put does NOT move the key
        System.out.println(ins);

        // accessOrder = true: every get/put moves the entry to the end
        Map<String, Integer> acc = new LinkedHashMap<>(16, 0.75f, true);
        acc.putAll(Map.of("a", 1));
        acc.put("b", 2);
        acc.put("c", 3);
        acc.get("a");
        System.out.println(acc);
    }
}
Output
{banana=20, apple=1, cherry=3}
{b=2, c=3, a=1}
LRU cache in a few lines
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        LruCache<Integer, String> cache = new LruCache<>(2);
        cache.put(1, "one");
        cache.put(2, "two");
        cache.get(1);                 // 1 is now most recently used
        cache.put(3, "three");        // evicts 2, the least recently used
        System.out.println(cache.keySet());
    }
}

class LruCache<K, V> extends LinkedHashMap<K, V> {
    private final int capacity;

    LruCache(int capacity) {
        super(16, 0.75f, true);       // access order
        this.capacity = capacity;
    }

    @Override
    protected boolean removeEldestEntry(Map.Entry<K, V> eldest) {
        return size() > capacity;     // called after every put
    }
}
Output
[1, 3]
Key point

This is the standard answer to “design an LRU cache” in Java. The from-scratch version (a HashMap of nodes + your own doubly-linked list) has the same O(1) get/put.

TreeMap as a NavigableMap
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        TreeMap<Integer, String> grades = new TreeMap<>();
        grades.put(90, "A");
        grades.put(80, "B");
        grades.put(70, "C");
        grades.put(0, "F");

        int score = 84;
        System.out.println(score + " -> " + grades.floorEntry(score).getValue());
        System.out.println(grades.firstKey() + " " + grades.lastKey());
        System.out.println(grades.floorKey(75) + " " + grades.ceilingKey(75));
        System.out.println(grades.ceilingEntry(95));             // no key >= 95
        System.out.println(grades.headMap(80));                  // keys < 80
        System.out.println(grades.subMap(70, true, 90, false));  // [70, 90)
        System.out.println(grades.descendingMap());
    }
}
Output
84 -> B
0 90
70 80
null
{0=F, 70=C}
{70=C, 80=B}
{90=A, 80=B, 70=C, 0=F}
MethodReturns
floorKey(k) / floorEntry(k)Greatest key <= k
ceilingKey(k) / ceilingEntry(k)Least key >= k
lowerKey(k) / higherKey(k)Strictly < k / > k
firstEntry() / pollFirstEntry()Smallest (and remove it)
headMap(k) / tailMap(k) / subMap(a, b)Live range views
descendingMap()Reverse-order view
Watch out

All the floor/ceiling methods return null when no such key exists — unboxing that null into an int throws NullPointerException.

How would you implement an LRU cache in Java?

Extend LinkedHashMap with accessOrder = true and override removeEldestEntry to return size() > capacity. Access order moves each touched entry to the tail; the eldest (head) is the least recently used and is evicted after a put. For thread safety wrap it or use a library such as Caffeine.

When would you choose TreeMap over HashMap?

When you need keys in sorted order or queries like “nearest key below x” (floorKey), “all keys in a range” (subMap), min/max key — e.g. time-series lookups, interval scheduling, leaderboard ranges. The cost is O(log n) per operation instead of O(1).

What internal structure does TreeMap use?

A red-black tree — a self-balancing binary search tree whose height stays ≤ 2·log₂(n+1), guaranteeing O(log n) get, put, remove. Ordering comes from compareTo or the supplied Comparator; equals is not used.

Collections Framework

Queue, Deque & PriorityQueue

Queue = FIFO line, Deque = double-ended (queue and stack), PriorityQueue = always hands you the smallest element next. ArrayDeque and PriorityQueue cover almost every need.

Two method families
ActionThrows on failureReturns special value
Insertadd(e) — IllegalStateException if fulloffer(e) → false
Remove headremove() — NoSuchElementException if emptypoll() → null
Look at headelement() — NoSuchElementExceptionpeek() → null
Key point

Prefer offer/poll/peek — empty checks become simple null tests. (Which is also why ArrayDeque and PriorityQueue refuse null elements.)

ArrayDeque as a queue and as a stack
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Deque<Integer> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();   // FIFO: offer at tail, poll at head
        queue.offer(1);
        queue.offer(2);
        queue.offer(3);
        System.out.println(queue.poll() + " " + queue.peek() + " " + queue);

        Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();   // LIFO: push/pop at the head
        stack.push(1);
        stack.push(2);
        stack.push(3);
        System.out.println(stack.pop() + " " + stack.peek() + " " + stack);

        Deque<String> dq = new ArrayDeque<>();
        dq.offerFirst("b");
        dq.offerFirst("a");
        dq.offerLast("c");
        System.out.println(dq.pollLast() + " " + dq);
        System.out.println(new ArrayDeque<Integer>().poll());   // empty -> null
    }
}
Output
1 2 [2, 3]
3 2 [2, 1]
c [a, b]
null
Deque methodAs a queueAs a stack
offerLast / offerenqueue—
pollFirst / polldequeue—
push (= addFirst)—push
pop (= removeFirst)—pop
peekFirst / peekfronttop
Do

Use Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>() — the Javadoc of Stack itself recommends it. ArrayDeque is a circular array: O(1) amortised at both ends, no node allocation.

PriorityQueue — a binary heap
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        PriorityQueue<Integer> min = new PriorityQueue<>(List.of(5, 1, 8, 3, 2));
        System.out.println("peek=" + min.peek());
        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
        while (!min.isEmpty()) sb.append(min.poll()).append(' ');
        System.out.println("min-heap order: " + sb.toString().trim());

        PriorityQueue<Integer> max = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
        max.addAll(List.of(5, 1, 8, 3, 2));
        System.out.println("max first: " + max.poll() + " " + max.poll());

        PriorityQueue<String> byLen = new PriorityQueue<>(
            Comparator.comparingInt(String::length).thenComparing(Comparator.naturalOrder()));
        byLen.addAll(List.of("kiwi", "fig", "banana", "date"));
        System.out.println(byLen.poll() + " " + byLen.poll() + " " + byLen.poll());
    }
}
Output
peek=1
min-heap order: 1 2 3 5 8
max first: 8 5
fig date kiwi
OperationCost
offer / addO(log n) — sift up
poll / remove()O(log n) — sift down
peekO(1)
remove(Object) / containsO(n)
Build from a collectionO(n) heapify
Watch out

Printing or iterating a PriorityQueue does not give sorted order — only the head is guaranteed to be the minimum. Poll repeatedly to get sorted output.

Top-K with a size-K min-heap
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    static List<Integer> topK(int[] nums, int k) {
        PriorityQueue<Integer> heap = new PriorityQueue<>();  // min-heap of the k largest so far
        for (int x : nums) {
            heap.offer(x);
            if (heap.size() > k) heap.poll();                 // drop the smallest
        }
        List<Integer> res = new ArrayList<>(heap);
        res.sort(Comparator.reverseOrder());
        return res;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(topK(new int[]{3, 1, 9, 7, 4, 8, 2}, 3));
    }
}
Output
[9, 8, 7]

O(n log k) time, O(k) space — better than sorting everything (O(n log n)) when k ≪ n.

Why prefer ArrayDeque over Stack and LinkedList?

Stack extends the synchronised Vector (slow, and leaks list methods). LinkedList allocates a node per element and chases pointers. ArrayDeque is a resizable circular array: O(1) amortised at both ends, compact and cache-friendly. Its only restriction: no null elements.

How do you make a max-heap in Java?

new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder()), or (a, b) -> b - a only when overflow is impossible — Integer.compare(b, a) is the safe lambda form.

Is PriorityQueue thread-safe? What about blocking queues?

No. Use PriorityBlockingQueue for a thread-safe heap. For producer–consumer, the BlockingQueue implementations (ArrayBlockingQueue, LinkedBlockingQueue) add put/take, which block when full/empty.

Collections Framework

Iterators & ConcurrentModificationException

Removing from a collection while a for-each loop walks it throws ConcurrentModificationException. Use removeIf, Iterator.remove, or a concurrent collection.

Iterator — an object with hasNext(), next() and optional remove() that walks a collection. The for-each loop over any Iterable is compiled into exactly these calls.

Fail-fast iterators

ArrayList, HashMap and friends keep a counter modCount, bumped on every structural change (add/remove, not set). The iterator remembers the value when it was created and checks it on each next(); a mismatch throws.

Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> nums = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3, 4));
        try {
            for (Integer n : nums) {
                if (n % 2 == 0) nums.remove(n);   // structural change behind the iterator's back
            }
        } catch (ConcurrentModificationException e) {
            System.out.println("ConcurrentModificationException");
        }
        System.out.println(nums);
    }
}
Output
ConcurrentModificationException
[1, 3, 4]
Watch out

Despite the name, this has nothing to do with threads — one thread modifying a collection it is iterating is enough. It is also best-effort: removing the second-to-last element skips the final hasNext() check and silently exits without an exception, so never rely on it to catch bugs.

Three correct ways to remove
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> a = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6));
        a.removeIf(n -> n % 2 == 0);                  // 1. simplest (Java 8+)
        System.out.println(a);

        List<Integer> b = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6));
        for (Iterator<Integer> it = b.iterator(); it.hasNext(); ) {
            if (it.next() % 2 == 0) it.remove();      // 2. the iterator's own remove
        }
        System.out.println(b);

        List<Integer> c = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6));
        for (int i = c.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--) {     // 3. index loop, backwards
            if (c.get(i) % 2 == 0) c.remove(i);
        }
        System.out.println(c);

        Map<String, Integer> m = new HashMap<>(Map.of("a", 1, "b", 2));
        m.entrySet().removeIf(e -> e.getValue() > 1); // maps: remove through a view
        System.out.println(m);
    }
}
Output
[1, 3, 5]
[1, 3, 5]
[1, 3, 5]
{a=1}
ListIterator — both directions, edit in place
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<String> words = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a", "b", "c"));
        ListIterator<String> it = words.listIterator();
        while (it.hasNext()) {
            int i = it.nextIndex();
            String w = it.next();
            if (w.equals("b")) {
                it.set("B");          // replace the element just returned
                it.add("b2");         // insert after it
            }
            System.out.print(i + ":" + w + " ");   // "c" is now at index 3
        }
        System.out.println();
        while (it.hasPrevious()) System.out.print(it.previous() + " ");
        System.out.println();
    }
}
Output
0:a 1:b 3:c
c b2 B a
Fail-safe (snapshot / weakly consistent) iterators
Java
import java.util.*;
import java.util.concurrent.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> cow = new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3));
        for (Integer n : cow) {
            if (n == 1) cow.add(99);      // iterator walks a snapshot — no exception
            System.out.print(n + " ");
        }
        System.out.println("-> " + cow);

        Map<Integer, String> chm = new ConcurrentHashMap<>(Map.of(1, "a", 2, "b"));
        for (Integer k : chm.keySet()) chm.remove(k);   // allowed
        System.out.println(chm);
    }
}
Output
1 2 3 -> [1, 2, 3, 99]
{}
Fail-fastFail-safe / weakly consistent
ExamplesArrayList, HashMap, HashSetCopyOnWriteArrayList, ConcurrentHashMap
Modify while iteratingThrows ConcurrentModificationExceptionAllowed
Sees changes made during iteration?—COW: never (snapshot); CHM: maybe
CostNoneCOW copies the whole array on every write
What causes ConcurrentModificationException and how do you avoid it?

A fail-fast iterator notices modCount changed by something other than itself — typically list.remove(x) inside a for-each loop. Avoid it with removeIf, Iterator.remove(), collecting items to remove and calling removeAll afterwards, iterating over a copy, or using a concurrent collection.

Fail-fast vs fail-safe iterators?

Fail-fast iterators (most java.util collections) throw on concurrent structural modification. Fail-safe ones (java.util.concurrent) never throw: CopyOnWriteArrayList iterates a snapshot, ConcurrentHashMap is weakly consistent — it reflects some, maybe all, changes made after the iterator was created.

Iterator vs ListIterator?

Iterator works on any collection, forward only, with remove. ListIterator is for lists: moves both ways (previous), knows indices (nextIndex), and can set and add at the cursor.

Collections Framework

Comparable vs Comparator

Comparable gives a class its one natural order (compareTo); a Comparator is any number of external orders built fluently with comparing, thenComparing and reversed.

Comparable<T>Comparator<T>
Packagejava.langjava.util
Methodint compareTo(T other)int compare(T a, T b)
WhereInside the class being sortedSeparate object / lambda
How many ordersOne — the natural orderAs many as you like
Used byCollections.sort(list), TreeMap, Arrays.sort with no comparatorlist.sort(cmp), new TreeSet<>(cmp), stream.sorted(cmp)
Examples in the JDKString, Integer, LocalDate, enumsString.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDER, Comparator.reverseOrder()
The contract
  • Return negative if this < other, zero if equal, positive if greater — only the sign matters.
  • Antisymmetric: sgn(a.compareTo(b)) == -sgn(b.compareTo(a)); transitive; consistent across calls.
  • Strongly recommended: consistent with equals (compareTo == 0 ⇔ equals) — sorted sets and maps use compareTo for equality.
Natural order with Comparable
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Version> vs = new ArrayList<>(List.of(new Version(1, 10), new Version(1, 2), new Version(0, 9)));
        Collections.sort(vs);
        System.out.println(vs);
        System.out.println(Collections.max(vs));
    }
}

record Version(int major, int minor) implements Comparable<Version> {
    @Override
    public int compareTo(Version o) {
        int c = Integer.compare(major, o.major);
        return c != 0 ? c : Integer.compare(minor, o.minor);
    }

    @Override
    public String toString() { return major + "." + minor; }
}
Output
[0.9, 1.2, 1.10]
1.10
Building comparators fluently
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    record Emp(String name, String dept, int salary) {}

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Emp> staff = new ArrayList<>(List.of(
            new Emp("Ravi", "HR", 50), new Emp("Asha", "Eng", 90),
            new Emp("Meera", "Eng", 70), new Emp("Kiran", "HR", 50)));

        staff.sort(Comparator.comparing(Emp::dept)
                .thenComparing(Emp::salary, Comparator.reverseOrder())
                .thenComparing(Emp::name));
        staff.forEach(e -> System.out.println(e.dept() + " " + e.salary() + " " + e.name()));

        List<String> words = new ArrayList<>(List.of("pear", "Fig", "apple", "kiwi"));
        words.sort(Comparator.comparingInt(String::length).reversed());
        System.out.println(words);
        words.sort(String.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDER);
        System.out.println(words);

        List<String> withNull = new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList("b", null, "a"));
        withNull.sort(Comparator.nullsFirst(Comparator.naturalOrder()));
        System.out.println(withNull);
    }
}
Output
Eng 90 Asha
Eng 70 Meera
HR 50 Kiran
HR 50 Ravi
[apple, pear, kiwi, Fig]
[apple, Fig, kiwi, pear]
[null, a, b]
Factory / methodMeaning
Comparator.comparing(keyFn)Compare by an extracted Comparable key
comparingInt/Long/Double(fn)Same, without boxing
.thenComparing(...)Tie-breaker
.reversed()Flip the whole chain built so far
Comparator.naturalOrder() / reverseOrder()Natural order / its reverse
nullsFirst(cmp) / nullsLast(cmp)Treat null as smallest / largest
Key point

Sorting is stable for objects (List.sort, Collections.sort, Arrays.sort(Object[]) use TimSort): equal elements keep their relative order — which is why multi-key sorts can also be done as successive sorts.

Trap: comparing by subtraction
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Comparator<Integer> bad = (a, b) -> a - b;          // overflows for far-apart values
        Comparator<Integer> good = Integer::compare;
        int big = Integer.MAX_VALUE, small = -1;
        System.out.println("a - b says: " + bad.compare(big, small));
        System.out.println("compare says: " + good.compare(big, small));

        List<Integer> xs = new ArrayList<>(List.of(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1));
        xs.sort(bad);                                       // 1 - MIN_VALUE overflows to negative
        System.out.println(xs);
    }
}
Output
a - b says: -2147483648
compare says: 1
[1, -2147483648]
Watch out

a - b overflows when the operands have opposite signs and large magnitudes, flipping the sign and breaking the sort. Always use Integer.compare(a, b) or Comparator.comparingInt.

Comparable vs Comparator — when to use which?

Implement Comparable when the class has one obvious natural order (numbers, dates, versions). Use a Comparator for alternative orders, for classes you can't modify, or for multi-field sorts built with comparing(...).thenComparing(...).

What happens if compareTo is inconsistent with equals?

Sorted collections use compareTo for equality, so a TreeSet may drop elements that equals considers different (or keep ones it considers equal). Classic JDK example: new BigDecimal("1.0") and new BigDecimal("1.00") are not equals (HashSet keeps 2) but compare as 0 (TreeSet keeps 1).

How does reversed() behave in a chain?

It reverses everything built before it. comparing(A).thenComparing(B).reversed() reverses both keys; to reverse only B write thenComparing(B, Comparator.reverseOrder()) or thenComparing(comparing(B).reversed()).

Collections Framework

Collections & Arrays Utilities

java.util.Collections and java.util.Arrays are static toolboxes — sorting, searching, filling, copying, wrapping. Knowing them saves writing (and debugging) loops.

Collections algorithms
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Integer> xs = new ArrayList<>(List.of(5, 3, 9, 1, 3));
        Collections.sort(xs);
        System.out.println(xs + " binarySearch(5)=" + Collections.binarySearch(xs, 5)
                + " binarySearch(4)=" + Collections.binarySearch(xs, 4));
        Collections.reverse(xs);
        System.out.println(xs + " max=" + Collections.max(xs) + " min=" + Collections.min(xs));
        System.out.println("frequency(3)=" + Collections.frequency(xs, 3));
        Collections.swap(xs, 0, 4);
        System.out.println(xs);
        Collections.rotate(xs, 1);
        System.out.println(xs);
        Collections.shuffle(xs, new Random(42));   // seeded -> reproducible
        System.out.println(xs);
        System.out.println(Collections.nCopies(3, "ab") + " " + String.join("", Collections.nCopies(3, "-")));
        System.out.println(Collections.disjoint(List.of(1, 2), List.of(3, 4)));
    }
}
Output
[1, 3, 3, 5, 9] binarySearch(5)=3 binarySearch(4)=-4
[9, 5, 3, 3, 1] max=9 min=1
frequency(3)=2
[1, 5, 3, 3, 9]
[9, 1, 5, 3, 3]
[1, 5, 3, 3, 9]
[ab, ab, ab] ---
true
Key point

binarySearch returns -(insertionPoint) - 1 when the key is missing: 4 would be inserted at index 3, so the result is -4. The list must already be sorted in the same order.

Wrappers and empty collections
MethodReturns
Collections.unmodifiableList(l) (…Set, Map)Read-only view — still reflects changes to l
Collections.synchronizedList(l) (…Map)Every method locked on one mutex; iterate inside synchronized (list) { … }
Collections.emptyList() / emptyMap()Shared immutable empty instance
Collections.singletonList(x)Immutable one-element list
Collections.addAll(c, a, b, c)Add varargs to any collection
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<String> src = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a"));
        List<String> view = Collections.unmodifiableList(src);
        List<String> copy = List.copyOf(src);
        src.add("b");
        System.out.println(view + " " + copy);   // the view sees the change, the copy doesn't
        try {
            view.add("c");
        } catch (UnsupportedOperationException e) {
            System.out.println("view is read-only");
        }
    }
}
Output
[a, b] [a]
view is read-only
Arrays helpers
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int[] a = {5, 2, 8, 1};
        Arrays.sort(a);
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString(a) + " idx(5)=" + Arrays.binarySearch(a, 5));

        int[] filled = new int[4];
        Arrays.fill(filled, -1);
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString(filled));

        int[] bigger = Arrays.copyOf(a, 6);            // pads with 0
        int[] slice = Arrays.copyOfRange(a, 1, 3);     // [1, 3)
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString(bigger) + " " + Arrays.toString(slice));

        System.out.println(Arrays.stream(a).sum() + " " + Arrays.stream(a).max().getAsInt());
        System.out.println(Arrays.equals(a, new int[]{1, 2, 5, 8}) + " " + (a == a.clone()));

        int[][] grid = {{1, 2}, {3, 4}};
        System.out.println(Arrays.deepToString(grid));

        Integer[] boxed = {3, 1, 2};
        Arrays.sort(boxed, Collections.reverseOrder()); // comparators need object arrays
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString(boxed));
    }
}
Output
[1, 2, 5, 8] idx(5)=2
[-1, -1, -1, -1]
[1, 2, 5, 8, 0, 0] [2, 5]
16 8
true false
[[1, 2], [3, 4]]
[3, 2, 1]
Key point

Arrays.sort(int[]) uses dual-pivot quicksort (not stable, but stability is meaningless for primitives); Arrays.sort(Object[]) uses stable TimSort. Primitive arrays can't take a Comparator — box them or sort then reverse.

Trap: Arrays.asList
Java
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String[] arr = {"x", "y", "z"};
        List<String> fixed = Arrays.asList(arr);   // fixed-size view over the array
        fixed.set(0, "X");                          // allowed, writes through
        System.out.println(Arrays.toString(arr));
        try {
            fixed.add("w");
        } catch (UnsupportedOperationException e) {
            System.out.println("add -> UnsupportedOperationException");
        }

        int[] prim = {1, 2, 3};
        System.out.println(Arrays.asList(prim).size());      // List<int[]> with ONE element
        System.out.println(Arrays.asList(1, 2, 3).size());   // varargs of Integer

        List<String> growable = new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList(arr));
        growable.add("w");
        System.out.println(growable);
    }
}
Output
[X, y, z]
add -> UnsupportedOperationException
1
3
[X, y, z, w]
Watch out

Arrays.asList returns a fixed-size list backed by the array (no add/remove), and Arrays.asList(intArray) gives a List<int[]> of size 1. Wrap it in new ArrayList<>(…) for a normal list; use Arrays.stream(ints).boxed().toList() for primitives.

What does Collections.synchronizedList not protect you from?

Compound actions and iteration. Each single call is locked, but if (!list.contains(x)) list.add(x) can interleave between threads, and iterating requires manually holding synchronized (list). Prefer java.util.concurrent collections.

unmodifiableList vs List.copyOf vs List.of?

unmodifiableList is a read-only view — changes to the backing list show through. List.copyOf makes an independent immutable copy (returns the argument itself if it is already an immutable list). List.of builds an immutable list from values. Neither of the last two allows nulls.

Which sort algorithms does Java use?

Arrays.sort on primitives: dual-pivot quicksort, O(n log n) average, not stable. On objects and in Collections.sort/List.sort: TimSort — a stable merge/insertion hybrid, O(n log n) worst case and O(n) on already-sorted data.

Functional Java & Standard Library

Functional Interfaces

An interface with exactly one abstract method — the target type that every lambda and method reference is converted to.

Functional interface — an interface with a single abstract method (SAM). default, static and private methods don't count, nor do methods that Object already provides (equals, toString). A lambda is just a compact implementation of that one method.

Declaring one
Java
public class Main {
    @FunctionalInterface
    interface Calculator {
        int apply(int a, int b);                 // the single abstract method

        default Calculator twice() {             // defaults are allowed
            return (a, b) -> apply(apply(a, b), b);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Calculator add = (a, b) -> a + b;
        Calculator mul = (a, b) -> a * b;
        System.out.println(add.apply(3, 4));
        System.out.println(mul.apply(3, 4));
        System.out.println(add.twice().apply(3, 4));   // (3 + 4) + 4
    }
}
Output
7
12
11
Annotation

@FunctionalInterface is optional but recommended: the compiler then rejects the interface if someone adds a second abstract method, protecting every lambda that depends on it.

Java
@FunctionalInterface
interface Broken {
    void first();
    void second();      // error: Broken is not a functional interface
}
The java.util.function toolbox

You rarely need to declare your own — the JDK ships 43 standard shapes. Learn the six core ones; the rest are variations.

InterfaceMethodShapeTypical use
Predicate<T>boolean test(T t)T → booleanfilter, removeIf
Function<T, R>R apply(T t)T → Rmap, computeIfAbsent
Consumer<T>void accept(T t)T → nothingforEach
Supplier<T>T get()nothing → Tlazy defaults, orElseGet, factories
UnaryOperator<T>T apply(T t)T → TreplaceAll, Stream.iterate
