maximumPoolSizeUpper bound — extra threads are created only when the queue is full
keepAliveTimeHow long threads above core may stay idle
workQueueWhere waiting tasks go: bounded ArrayBlockingQueue recommended
threadFactoryName threads (order-worker-1), set daemon/priority
handlerRejection policy when both queue and max threads are full
task arrives
→
threads < core? new thread
→
else queue it
→
queue full? thread up to max
→
else reject
Java
import java.util.concurrent.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        CountDownLatch gate = new CountDownLatch(1);
        ThreadPoolExecutor pool = new ThreadPoolExecutor(
            1, 1, 0, TimeUnit.SECONDS,                 // exactly one thread
            new ArrayBlockingQueue<>(1),               // room for one waiting task
            new ThreadPoolExecutor.AbortPolicy());     // default: throw when full
        Runnable blocker = () -> {
            try { gate.await(); } catch (InterruptedException e) { Thread.currentThread().interrupt(); }
        };
        pool.execute(blocker);                         // runs on the only thread
        pool.execute(blocker);                         // waits in the queue
        try {
            pool.execute(blocker);                     // no thread, no queue space
        } catch (RejectedExecutionException e) {
            System.out.println("3rd task rejected");
        }
        System.out.println("pool size = " + pool.getPoolSize() + ", queued = " + pool.getQueue().size());
        gate.countDown();
        pool.shutdown();
        pool.awaitTermination(1, TimeUnit.SECONDS);
        System.out.println("completed = " + pool.getCompletedTaskCount());
    }
}
Output
3rd task rejected
pool size = 1, queued = 1
completed = 2
Rejection policyBehaviour
AbortPolicy (default)Throw RejectedExecutionException
CallerRunsPolicyRun the task on the submitting thread — natural back-pressure
DiscardPolicySilently drop the new task
DiscardOldestPolicyDrop the oldest queued task, retry the new one
How many threads?
threads ≈ cores × (1 + wait time / compute time)
  • CPU-bound (hashing, parsing, math): about Runtime.getRuntime().availableProcessors() threads — more only adds context switching.
  • I/O-bound (HTTP calls, DB queries): many more, since threads mostly wait. On Java 21 prefer virtual threads here.
  • Use separate pools for separate kinds of work, so a slow dependency can't starve everything (bulkheads).
submit() vs execute()?

execute(Runnable) comes from Executor, returns nothing, and an exception goes to the thread's uncaught-exception handler. submit() accepts Runnable or Callable, returns a Future, and captures any exception inside it (rethrown as ExecutionException from get()).

Why is Executors.newFixedThreadPool considered risky in production?

Its LinkedBlockingQueue is unbounded: if producers outpace workers, tasks pile up until OutOfMemoryError. Construct a ThreadPoolExecutor with a bounded queue, named threads and an explicit rejection policy instead.

When does a ThreadPoolExecutor create threads beyond corePoolSize?

Only when the work queue is full. With an unbounded queue, maximumPoolSize is never reached — a common surprise.

shutdown() vs shutdownNow()?

shutdown() is graceful: no new tasks, queued and running tasks complete. shutdownNow() interrupts running tasks, drains the queue and returns the unstarted tasks. Follow either with awaitTermination to actually wait.

Concurrency

CompletableFuture

CompletableFuture composes asynchronous steps into a pipeline — transform, chain, combine and recover — without blocking a thread on each get().

CompletableFuture<T> — a Future you can complete explicitly and a CompletionStage: attach callbacks (thenApply, thenCompose, …) that run when the value is ready. Async methods run on ForkJoinPool.commonPool() unless you pass an Executor.

Transform and combine
Java
import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        CompletableFuture<Integer> price = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 200);
        CompletableFuture<Double> discount = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 0.9);

        double total = price
            .thenCombine(discount, (p, d) -> p * d)   // wait for BOTH, merge
            .thenApply(x -> x + 15)                   // transform the value (shipping)
            .join();                                  // block for the final result
        System.out.println("total = " + total);

        CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "order-42")
            .thenApply(String::toUpperCase)
            .thenAccept(id -> System.out.println("placed " + id))   // consume, returns CF<Void>
            .thenRun(() -> System.out.println("email queued"))      // no input, no output
            .join();
    }
}
Output
total = 195.0
placed ORDER-42
email queued
thenApply vs thenCompose

thenApply is map: the function returns a plain value. thenCompose is flatMap: the function itself returns a CompletableFuture, and you want the inner result — not a nested CompletableFuture<CompletableFuture<T>>.

Java
import java.util.List;
import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;

public class Main {
    static CompletableFuture<String> findUser(int id) {
        return CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> "user" + id);
    }

    static CompletableFuture<List<String>> findOrders(String user) {
        return CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> List.of(user + ":book", user + ":pen"));
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<String> orders = findUser(7)
            .thenCompose(Main::findOrders)        // async step depending on the previous one
            .join();
        System.out.println(orders);
    }
}
Output
[user7:book, user7:pen]
MethodFunction shapeAnalogy
thenApply(f)T -> Umap
thenCompose(f)T -> CompletionStage<U>flatMap — sequential async calls
thenCombine(other, f)(T, U) -> Vzip two independent futures
thenAccept(c) / thenRun(r)T -> void / () -> voidside effect at the end
allOf(cfs...)→ CompletableFuture<Void>wait for all
anyOf(cfs...)→ CompletableFuture<Object>first to finish wins
...Async variantssame, but callback runs on the pooldon't hog the completing thread
Waiting for many: allOf
Java
import java.util.List;
import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<CompletableFuture<Integer>> calls = List.of("alpha", "beta", "gamma").stream()
            .map(s -> CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> s.length()))
            .toList();

        CompletableFuture.allOf(calls.toArray(new CompletableFuture[0])).join();
        List<Integer> lengths = calls.stream().map(CompletableFuture::join).toList();  // all done: no waiting
        System.out.println(lengths + " sum=" + lengths.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).sum());
    }
}
Output
[5, 4, 5] sum=14
Handling errors
Java
import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;
import java.util.concurrent.CompletionException;
import java.util.concurrent.ExecutionException;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        CompletableFuture<Integer> failing = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> Integer.parseInt("x"));

        int recovered = failing.exceptionally(ex -> -1).join();          // fallback value
        System.out.println("exceptionally -> " + recovered);

        String handled = failing.handle((value, ex) ->                   // sees success OR failure
            ex == null ? "ok " + value : "failed: " + ex.getCause().getClass().getSimpleName()).join();
        System.out.println("handle -> " + handled);

        try {
            failing.join();                                              // unchecked wrapper
        } catch (CompletionException e) {
            System.out.println("join threw CompletionException, cause " + e.getCause().getClass().getSimpleName());
        }
        try {
            failing.get();                                               // checked wrapper
        } catch (ExecutionException e) {
            System.out.println("get threw ExecutionException, cause " + e.getCause().getClass().getSimpleName());
        }
    }
}
Output
exceptionally -> -1
handle -> failed: NumberFormatException
join threw CompletionException, cause NumberFormatException
get threw ExecutionException, cause NumberFormatException
MethodCalled onCan change result?
exceptionally(fn)failure onlyYes — supply a fallback
handle((v, ex) -> ...)success and failureYes — map either to a new value
whenComplete((v, ex) -> ...)success and failureNo — side effects (logging); original result passes through
orTimeout(t, unit) / completeOnTimeout(v, t, unit)Java 9+Fail / default after a timeout
Use your own executor
Java
import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;
import java.util.concurrent.ExecutorService;
import java.util.concurrent.Executors;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        try (ExecutorService io = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(2, r -> new Thread(r, "io-worker"))) {
            String where = CompletableFuture
                .supplyAsync(() -> Thread.currentThread().getName(), io)   // runs on our pool
                .join();
            System.out.println("ran on " + where);
        }
    }
}
Output
ran on io-worker
Watch out

The common ForkJoinPool is sized for CPU work (cores − 1 threads) and shared by the whole JVM, including parallel streams. Blocking I/O in supplyAsync(...) without an executor can starve everything else — pass a dedicated executor (or virtual threads).

join() vs get()?

Both block for the result. get() throws checked InterruptedException and ExecutionException; join() throws unchecked CompletionException, which makes it usable inside lambdas and stream pipelines.

thenApply vs thenCompose?

thenApply maps the result with a function returning a value. thenCompose is for a function that returns another CompletionStage — it flattens, so dependent async calls chain as CF<U> instead of CF<CF<U>>.

Future vs CompletableFuture?

A plain Future only lets you poll or block with get(). CompletableFuture can be completed manually (complete, completeExceptionally), supports non-blocking callbacks, composition (thenCompose, thenCombine, allOf) and error recovery (exceptionally, handle).

Concurrency

ConcurrentHashMap, Atomics & Locks

java.util.concurrent replaces most hand-written synchronized/wait code with tested building blocks: concurrent collections, atomic variables, explicit locks and synchronizers.

Thread-safe maps compared
HashtableCollections.synchronizedMapConcurrentHashMap
LockingEvery method locks the whole tableEvery method locks one mutexCAS + per-bin locks; reads are lock-free
Concurrent reads/writesNoNoYes
null keys/valuesNot allowedAllowed (if the backing map allows)Not allowed
Iteration while modifyingFail-fastMust lock manually; else CMEWeakly consistent, never throws CME
Atomic compound opsNoNomerge, compute, putIfAbsent…
StatusLegacySimple wrapperDefault choice
Java
import java.util.List;
import java.util.TreeMap;
import java.util.concurrent.ConcurrentHashMap;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        ConcurrentHashMap<String, Integer> counts = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
        List<String> words = List.of("a", "b", "a", "c", "a", "b");

        Runnable countAll = () -> { for (String w : words) counts.merge(w, 1, Integer::sum); };  // atomic
        Thread[] ts = new Thread[4];
        for (int i = 0; i < ts.length; i++) (ts[i] = new Thread(countAll)).start();
        for (Thread t : ts) t.join();

        System.out.println(new TreeMap<>(counts));     // sorted for display
        counts.computeIfAbsent("d", k -> 0);
        counts.compute("d", (k, v) -> v + 100);
        System.out.println("d = " + counts.get("d") + ", size = " + counts.size());
    }
}
Output
{a=12, b=8, c=4}
d = 100, size = 4
Watch out

if (!map.containsKey(k)) map.put(k, v); is a check-then-act race even on a ConcurrentHashMap — each call is atomic, the pair is not. Use putIfAbsent, computeIfAbsent or merge.

Atomic variables and CAS

CAS (compare-and-swap) — a single CPU instruction: if the value is still expected, set it to new. Atomic classes retry CAS in a loop, giving lock-free thread safety for single variables.

Java
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicInteger;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.LongAdder;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        AtomicInteger a = new AtomicInteger(5);
        System.out.println(a.compareAndSet(5, 10) + " " + a.get());   // expected 5: swap
        System.out.println(a.compareAndSet(5, 20) + " " + a.get());   // stale expectation: no swap
        System.out.println(a.updateAndGet(x -> x * 3));
        System.out.println(a.getAndIncrement() + " then " + a.get());

        AtomicInteger hits = new AtomicInteger();
        LongAdder adder = new LongAdder();
        Runnable work = () -> {
            for (int i = 0; i < 10_000; i++) { hits.incrementAndGet(); adder.increment(); }
        };
        Thread[] ts = new Thread[4];
        for (int i = 0; i < ts.length; i++) (ts[i] = new Thread(work)).start();
        for (Thread t : ts) t.join();
        System.out.println("AtomicInteger = " + hits.get() + ", LongAdder = " + adder.sum());
    }
}
Output
true 10
false 10
30
30 then 31
AtomicInteger = 40000, LongAdder = 40000
ClassUse
AtomicInteger / AtomicLongCounters, sequence numbers, compareAndSet state machines
AtomicBooleanRun-once flags (if (started.compareAndSet(false, true)))
AtomicReference<V>Swap an immutable object atomically
LongAdderHot counters under heavy contention — cells per thread, summed on read
AtomicIntegerArrayPer-index atomic updates
Key point

ABA problem: CAS only checks the value, so A → B → A looks unchanged. When it matters (lock-free stacks), use AtomicStampedReference, which pairs the reference with a version stamp.

ReentrantLock and Condition

An explicit Lock does what synchronized does plus: try without blocking, time out, be interrupted while waiting, optional fairness, and several wait-sets (Conditions) per lock.

Java
import java.util.concurrent.locks.ReentrantLock;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        ReentrantLock lock = new ReentrantLock();
        lock.lock();                                   // main holds it
        try {
            Thread t = new Thread(() -> System.out.println("worker tryLock: " + lock.tryLock()));
            t.start();
            t.join();
        } finally {
            lock.unlock();                             // ALWAYS unlock in finally
        }
        Thread t2 = new Thread(() -> {
            if (lock.tryLock()) {
                try { System.out.println("worker tryLock after unlock: true"); }
                finally { lock.unlock(); }
            }
        });
        t2.start();
        t2.join();
        System.out.println("held now? " + lock.isLocked());
    }
}
Output
worker tryLock: false
worker tryLock after unlock: true
held now? false
Java — Condition (compile-checked)
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.Deque;
import java.util.concurrent.locks.Condition;
import java.util.concurrent.locks.ReentrantLock;

class LockBuffer<T> {
    private final Deque<T> items = new ArrayDeque<>();
    private final int capacity;
    private final ReentrantLock lock = new ReentrantLock();
    private final Condition notFull = lock.newCondition();    // two separate wait-sets
    private final Condition notEmpty = lock.newCondition();

    LockBuffer(int capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; }

    void put(T x) throws InterruptedException {
        lock.lock();
        try {
            while (items.size() == capacity) notFull.await();
            items.addLast(x);
            notEmpty.signal();              // wake only a consumer
        } finally {
            lock.unlock();
        }
    }

    T take() throws InterruptedException {
        lock.lock();
        try {
            while (items.isEmpty()) notEmpty.await();
            T x = items.removeFirst();
            notFull.signal();               // wake only a producer
            return x;
        } finally {
            lock.unlock();
        }
    }
}
synchronizedReentrantLock
ReleaseAutomatic at block endManual unlock() in finally
Try / timeoutNotryLock(), tryLock(t, unit)
Interruptible wait for lockNolockInterruptibly()
FairnessNonew ReentrantLock(true) (FIFO-ish, slower)
ConditionsOne wait-set (wait/notify)Many (newCondition())
ReentrantYesYes

ReentrantReadWriteLock lets many readers in at once but writers alone — good for read-mostly data. StampedLock (Java 8) adds optimistic reads for even cheaper read paths.

Synchronizers
ToolWhat it doesReusable?Typical use
CountDownLatch(n)await() blocks until countDown() was called n timesNoWait for N services to start / N tasks to finish
CyclicBarrier(n, action)n threads wait for each other, then all continueYesPhased parallel algorithms
Semaphore(permits)acquire()/release() permitsYesLimit concurrency (max 10 DB connections)
PhaserFlexible barrier with dynamic partiesYesMulti-phase work with join/leave
ExchangerTwo threads swap objectsYesPipeline buffers
Java
import java.util.List;
import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicInteger;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        // CountDownLatch: wait for 3 workers
        CountDownLatch done = new CountDownLatch(3);
        List<String> results = new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>();
        for (String svc : List.of("db", "cache", "queue")) {
            new Thread(() -> { results.add(svc + " ready"); done.countDown(); }).start();
        }
        done.await();
        System.out.println(results.stream().sorted().toList());

        // Semaphore: at most 2 tasks inside at once
        Semaphore permits = new Semaphore(2);
        AtomicInteger inside = new AtomicInteger(), maxInside = new AtomicInteger();
        try (ExecutorService pool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(6)) {
            for (int i = 0; i < 6; i++) {
                pool.submit(() -> {
                    permits.acquire();
                    try {
                        maxInside.accumulateAndGet(inside.incrementAndGet(), Math::max);
                        Thread.sleep(20);
                        inside.decrementAndGet();
                    } finally {
                        permits.release();
                    }
                    return null;
                });
            }
        }
        System.out.println("max concurrent <= 2: " + (maxInside.get() <= 2));
    }
}
Output
[cache ready, db ready, queue ready]
max concurrent <= 2: true
Key point

Other concurrent collections: CopyOnWriteArrayList (copy on every write — ideal for rarely-changed listener lists), ConcurrentLinkedQueue (lock-free queue), ConcurrentSkipListMap/Set (sorted, concurrent TreeMap/TreeSet).

How does ConcurrentHashMap achieve thread safety (Java 8+)?

The table is an array of bins. Inserting into an empty bin uses CAS; otherwise the thread locks only that bin's first node. Reads are lock-free via volatile reads. Long bins turn into red-black trees. Resizing is done cooperatively by several threads. (Java 7 used 16 lock segments instead.)

Why doesn't ConcurrentHashMap allow null?

Ambiguity: get(k) returning null could mean "absent" or "mapped to null", and unlike a single-threaded map you can't follow up with containsKey atomically — another thread may change the map in between.

AtomicInteger vs LongAdder?

AtomicInteger is one CAS'd variable — exact reads, but many threads hammering it retry a lot. LongAdder spreads increments over per-thread cells and sums on sum(), scaling far better for write-heavy counters, at the cost of a non-atomic snapshot read.

CountDownLatch vs CyclicBarrier?

A latch is one-shot: some threads count down, others await; it cannot be reset. A barrier makes a fixed group of threads wait for each other, can run a barrier action, and resets automatically for the next round.

Concurrency

Virtual Threads (Java 21)

Virtual threads (JEP 444, final in Java 21) are lightweight threads managed by the JVM — you can have millions, so plain blocking code scales like async code.

Virtual thread — a java.lang.Thread not tied to an OS thread. The JVM mounts it on a small pool of carrier (platform) threads; when it blocks on I/O, sleep or a lock, it unmounts and the carrier runs another virtual thread.

Platform vs virtual threads
Platform threadVirtual thread
Backed byOne OS thread for its whole lifeAny carrier thread, only while running
StackFixed, ~1 MB reservedHeap-allocated, grows as needed (KBs)
How manyThousandsMillions
CreationExpensive — pool themCheap — create one per task, never pool
SchedulerOSJVM (ForkJoinPool of carriers, ~one per core)
Always daemon?ConfigurableYes; priority fixed
Best forCPU-bound work, long-lived threadsHigh-concurrency blocking I/O (servers, clients)
Creating virtual threads
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        Thread v = Thread.ofVirtual().name("v-worker").start(() ->
            System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName()
                + " virtual=" + Thread.currentThread().isVirtual()
                + " daemon=" + Thread.currentThread().isDaemon()));
        v.join();

        Thread quick = Thread.startVirtualThread(() -> System.out.println("started with startVirtualThread"));
        quick.join();

        Thread unstarted = Thread.ofVirtual().unstarted(() -> { });
        System.out.println("state = " + unstarted.getState());

        Thread p = Thread.ofPlatform().name("p-worker").start(() ->
            System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " virtual=" + Thread.currentThread().isVirtual()));
        p.join();
    }
}
Output
v-worker virtual=true daemon=true
started with startVirtualThread
state = NEW
p-worker virtual=false
One virtual thread per task

10 000 tasks that each block for 10 ms. A pool of 100 platform threads would need ~1 s; with a thread per task the sleeps all overlap and the whole batch finishes in roughly the time of one.

Java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.concurrent.ExecutorService;
import java.util.concurrent.Executors;
import java.util.concurrent.Future;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        List<Future<Integer>> results = new ArrayList<>();
        try (ExecutorService exec = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) {
            for (int i = 0; i < 10_000; i++) {
                int n = i;
                results.add(exec.submit(() -> {
                    Thread.sleep(10);          // blocking call: the virtual thread unmounts
                    return n;
                }));
            }
        }                                      // close() waits for all 10 000
        long sum = 0;
        for (Future<Integer> f : results) sum += f.resultNow();
        System.out.println(results.size() + " tasks, sum = " + sum);
    }
}
Output
10000 tasks, sum = 49995000
When they help — and when they don't

Great for

Thread-per-request servers, calling many HTTP/DB services, anything that spends most of its time waiting. Write simple blocking code; get async-level throughput.

No gain for

CPU-bound work — there are still only as many carriers as cores. Virtual threads increase throughput for waiting tasks, not the speed of a single task.

Pitfalls
PitfallWhyInstead
Pooling virtual threadsThey are cheap; pooling just limits concurrencyOne per task; limit access to a scarce resource with a Semaphore
Pinning (Java 21)Blocking inside synchronized (or a native call) keeps the carrier occupiedUse ReentrantLock around blocking I/O; diagnose with -Djdk.tracePinnedThreads=full (fixed for synchronized in JDK 24, JEP 491)
Heavy ThreadLocal cachesA million threads × a cached object each = memory blow-upPass context explicitly; scoped values (preview)
Long CPU loopsNo time-slicing preemption between virtual threadsKeep CPU-heavy work on a platform pool
Watch out

Virtual threads are always daemon threads: if main returns without joining them (or closing their executor), the JVM exits and their work is lost.

Related previews

Structured concurrency (StructuredTaskScope, preview in Java 21, needs --enable-preview) treats a group of subtasks as one unit: fork them, join() the scope, and if one fails the others are cancelled — no leaked threads. Scoped values (also preview) are the virtual-thread-friendly replacement for ThreadLocal.

Do virtual threads make code faster?

Not individual tasks — a CPU-bound computation runs at the same speed. They raise throughput for I/O-bound workloads by letting you have one cheap thread per concurrent task, so waiting no longer ties up an expensive OS thread.

What is a carrier thread? What is pinning?

A carrier is the platform thread (from a ForkJoinPool) a virtual thread is mounted on while it runs. Pinning happens when a virtual thread blocks but can't unmount — in Java 21, while inside a synchronized block or a native method — so the carrier is blocked too, reducing scalability.

Should you use a thread pool of virtual threads?

No. Pools exist to amortise expensive thread creation and cap resource use; virtual threads are cheap, so create one per task (newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor). To cap access to a limited resource, use a Semaphore instead.

JVM Internals & Memory

JVM Memory: Heap, Stack & Metaspace

The JVM splits memory into areas with different lifetimes: objects on the heap, method calls on per-thread stacks, class metadata in metaspace.

Runtime data areas — the regions of memory the JVM specification defines. Some are shared by all threads (heap, metaspace), others are private to each thread (stack, PC register).

The runtime data areas
AreaShared?HoldsError when full
Heap — young gen (Eden + 2 Survivor spaces)All threadsNewly created objects and arraysOutOfMemoryError: Java heap space
Heap — old (tenured) genAll threadsObjects that survived several young GCsOutOfMemoryError: Java heap space
JVM stack (one per thread)Per threadOne frame per method call: local variables, operand stack, return addressStackOverflowError
Metaspace (native memory, Java 8+)All threadsClass metadata: method bytecode, field layout, runtime constant poolOutOfMemoryError: Metaspace
PC registerPer threadAddress of the bytecode instruction being executed—
Native method stackPer threadFrames of native (C/C++) methods called through JNIStackOverflowError
Code cacheAll threadsMachine code produced by the JIT compilerJIT stops compiling (warning)
History

The string pool lives on the heap (moved out of PermGen in Java 7). PermGen itself was removed in Java 8 and replaced by metaspace, which grows in native memory instead of having a fixed size.

What lives where

Primitive locals

int x = 5; inside a method — the value sits directly in the stack frame. Gone when the method returns.

References

Box b is a local of reference type — the reference (a pointer-sized value) is on the stack…

Objects

…and the new Box() it points to is on the heap, shared by every reference to it. Instance fields (primitive or not) live inside the object.

Static fields

Belong to the class. Since Java 8 they are stored with the Class object on the heap; the class metadata is in metaspace.

Java
public class Main {
    static class Box { int value; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int a = 10;           // primitive local → in main's stack frame
        int b = a;            // copies the value
        b++;

        Box x = new Box();    // reference x on the stack, Box object on the heap
        Box y = x;            // copies the reference, not the object
        y.value = 42;

        System.out.println(a + " " + b);
        System.out.println(x.value + " " + (x == y));
    }
}
Output
10 11
42 true
Stack frames and StackOverflowError
1
Call
Calling a method pushes a new frame onto the current thread's stack.
2
Execute
The frame holds the method's parameters and locals, plus an operand stack the bytecode works on.
3
Return
The frame is popped — its locals disappear instantly. No GC is needed for stack memory.
Java
public class Main {
    static int depth = 0;

    static void recurse() {
        depth++;
        recurse();              // no base case → a new frame on every call
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        try {
            recurse();
        } catch (StackOverflowError e) {
            System.out.println("StackOverflowError after more than 1000 frames: " + (depth > 1000));
        }
    }
}
Output
StackOverflowError after more than 1000 frames: true
OutOfMemoryError

Run with a deliberately tiny heap (-Xmx64m) and ask for a 400 MB array:

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        try {
            int[] huge = new int[100_000_000];      // ~400 MB
            System.out.println(huge.length);
        } catch (OutOfMemoryError e) {
            System.out.println("OutOfMemoryError: " + e.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
Output
OutOfMemoryError: Java heap space
StackOverflowErrorOutOfMemoryError
AreaA thread's stackHeap, metaspace, direct buffers, native threads…
Typical causeUnbounded / too deep recursionLeak (objects stay reachable), or heap simply too small
FixBase case, iteration, or bigger -XssFix the leak (heap dump), then size -Xmx
TypeBoth extend Error (via VirtualMachineError) — not meant to be caught in normal code
Sizing flags
FlagMeaningExample
-XmsInitial heap size-Xms512m
-XmxMaximum heap size (default: 1/4 of physical RAM)-Xmx2g

$ java -Xms256m -Xmx1g -Xss512k -XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError App.java
$ java -XX:+PrintFlagsFinal -version | grep -E "MaxHeapSize|ThreadStackSize"
Escape analysis

The JIT checks whether an object escapes the method that creates it (returned, stored in a field, passed to another thread). If it doesn't, the JIT may scalar-replace it — keep its fields in registers/stack slots and never allocate it on the heap at all.

Java
public class Main {
    record Point(int x, int y) {}

    static int distanceSquared(int x, int y) {
        Point p = new Point(x, y);      // never escapes → candidate for scalar replacement
        return p.x() * p.x() + p.y() * p.y();
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        long sum = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < 1_000; i++) sum += distanceSquared(i, i);
        System.out.println(sum);
    }
}
Output
665667000
Key point

"All objects live on the heap" is the language model. Escape analysis is an invisible optimisation — your program behaves exactly the same either way.

Where are local variables, objects and static variables stored?

Local primitives and local references are in the thread's stack frame. Objects (including their instance fields) are on the heap. Static fields are stored with the class's Class object on the heap (Java 8+); the class metadata itself is in metaspace.

What replaced PermGen and why?

Metaspace (Java 8). PermGen had a fixed maximum size and caused OutOfMemoryError: PermGen space in apps that load many classes (app servers, dynamic proxies). Metaspace lives in native memory and grows on demand; cap it with -XX:MaxMetaspaceSize.

Is the stack or the heap thread-safe?

Each thread has its own stack, so locals are never shared — they are inherently thread-confined. The heap is shared by all threads, so objects reachable from more than one thread need synchronization.

JVM Internals & Memory

Garbage Collection

The garbage collector frees heap objects that are no longer reachable — you never call free, but you still decide what stays reachable.

Garbage — an object that cannot be reached by following references from any GC root. Reference counting is not used, so cycles of objects pointing at each other are still collected.

GC roots and reachability
  • Local variables and parameters in every live thread's stack frames.
  • Static fields of loaded classes.
  • Active threads themselves.
  • JNI references held by native code, and objects used as monitors (synchronized).
Java
public class Main {
    static class Node { Node next; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Node a = new Node();
        Node b = new Node();
        a.next = b;
        b.next = a;          // a cycle: a → b → a

        a = null;
        b = null;            // no root reaches the cycle any more → both are garbage
        System.out.println("the cycle is unreachable and will be collected");
    }
}
Output
the cycle is unreachable and will be collected
Generational collection

Weak generational hypothesis: most objects die young (temporary strings, iterators, DTOs). So the heap is split and the young part is collected often and cheaply.

1
Allocate in Eden
new bumps a pointer in a thread-local allocation buffer — allocation is extremely cheap.
2
Minor GC
When Eden fills, live objects are copied to a Survivor space; everything left in Eden is reclaimed at once.
3
Age and promote
Each survived minor GC increments an object's age; past a threshold it is promoted to the old generation.
4
Major / full GC
When the old generation fills, it is collected too — more expensive, historically the source of long pauses.
Phase / termMeaning
MarkTrace from the roots and mark every reachable object.
SweepReclaim the memory of unmarked objects.
CompactSlide live objects together to remove fragmentation.
CopyingCopy live objects to an empty space (used for the young gen: cost ∝ live objects, not heap size).
Stop-the-world (STW)All application threads are paused while the GC works.
ConcurrentGC work done while application threads keep running.
The collectors
CollectorFlagBest forNotes
Serial-XX:+UseSerialGCTiny heaps, single-core containersOne thread, stop-the-world
Parallel-XX:+UseParallelGCBatch jobs — maximum throughputMany threads, still STW
G1 (default since 9)-XX:+UseG1GCGeneral purpose, heaps of GBsRegion-based; targets a pause goal (-XX:MaxGCPauseMillis=200)
ZGC-XX:+UseZGC (generational in 21: -XX:+ZGenerational)Huge heaps, low latencyPauses under a millisecond, mostly concurrent
Shenandoah-XX:+UseShenandoahGCLow latencyConcurrent compaction (OpenJDK builds)
CMS——Deprecated in 9, removed in 14
Terminal
$ java -XX:+UseZGC -Xmx4g -Xlog:gc App.java      # choose a collector, log each GC
$ java -Xlog:gc*:file=gc.log App.java            # detailed GC log to a file
$ jcmd <pid> GC.heap_info                        # inspect a running JVM
System.gc(), finalize() and cleanup
Watch out

System.gc() is only a hint — the JVM may ignore it (-XX:+DisableExplicitGC), and when honoured it usually triggers an expensive full GC. Never call it to “free memory”.

finalize()

finalize() is deprecated for removal (Java 9/18): it runs at an unpredictable time or never, slows GC, and can resurrect objects. Release resources with try-with-resources; for a safety net use java.lang.ref.Cleaner.

Java
import java.lang.ref.Cleaner;

public class Main {
    static final Cleaner CLEANER = Cleaner.create();

    static class Resource implements AutoCloseable {
        private final Cleaner.Cleanable cleanable;

        Resource(String name) {
            // the cleanup action must NOT capture `this`, or the object never becomes unreachable
            cleanable = CLEANER.register(this, () -> System.out.println("released " + name));
        }

        @Override
        public void close() { cleanable.clean(); }   // runs the action at most once
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        try (Resource r = new Resource("db-connection")) {
            System.out.println("using the resource");
        }
        System.out.println("after try");
    }
}
Output
using the resource
released db-connection
after try
Tuning in practice
  1. Measure first: GC logs (-Xlog:gc), JDK Flight Recorder (-XX:StartFlightRecording), jstat -gc <pid>.
  2. Size the heap: set -Xms = -Xmx for servers to avoid resizing.
  3. Pick the collector by goal: throughput → Parallel, balanced → G1, latency → ZGC.
  4. Reduce garbage: reuse builders, avoid needless boxing, stream large files instead of loading them.
Can an object be garbage collected while it still references other objects?

Yes. Reachability is about incoming references from roots. An object that points to others — even a whole cycle — is garbage as soon as no root can reach it.

What is a stop-the-world pause and how do modern collectors reduce it?

A pause during which all application threads are stopped so the GC can work on a consistent heap. G1 limits pause length by collecting a subset of regions per cycle; ZGC and Shenandoah do marking and compaction concurrently, keeping pauses below ~1 ms regardless of heap size.

Minor GC vs major GC?

A minor GC collects only the young generation — frequent and fast because most young objects are dead. A major (old-gen) or full GC collects the old generation or the whole heap — rarer and much more expensive.

Does calling System.gc() guarantee collection?

No. It is a request the JVM may ignore. Code that depends on it for correctness is broken.

JVM Internals & Memory

References & Memory Leaks

Java has garbage collection, not leak immunity: anything you keep reachable by accident is a leak.

Memory leak (Java) — objects that are no longer needed but are still reachable from a GC root, so the collector can never reclaim them. The heap slowly fills until OutOfMemoryError.

Four reference strengths
TypeCollected when…get() after collectionTypical use
Strong (Obj o = …)Never, while reachable—Normal code
Soft — SoftReferenceOnly when memory is low (before OOM)nullMemory-sensitive caches
Weak — WeakReferenceAt the next GC once only weakly reachablenullWeakHashMap, canonicalising maps, listener registries
Phantom — PhantomReferenceAfter finalisation; enqueued for cleanupalways nullPost-mortem cleanup (Cleaner is built on it)
Java
import java.lang.ref.WeakReference;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        StringBuilder data = new StringBuilder("payload");
        WeakReference<StringBuilder> weak = new WeakReference<>(data);

        System.out.println(weak.get());          // still strongly reachable via `data`
        data = null;                             // drop the only strong reference
        System.gc();                             // a hint — usually honoured in practice
        System.out.println(weak.get());
    }
}
Typical output
payload
null
Key point

Weak/soft clearing depends on when the GC actually runs, so never write logic that needs a reference to be cleared at a particular moment.

WeakHashMap

Keys are held weakly: when a key has no strong references elsewhere, its entry disappears on a later GC. Useful for attaching metadata to objects you don't own.

Watch out

A WeakHashMap with String literal keys or boxed small integers never clears — those keys are strongly held by the string pool / Integer cache. And a value that refers back to its own key keeps the key alive.

The usual leak suspects
LeakWhy it's reachableFix
Static collection used as a cacheStatic fields are GC roots — the map lives as long as the classBounded cache (LRU via LinkedHashMap.removeEldestEntry, Caffeine), or eviction
Listeners / callbacks never removedThe subject's listener list points at every subscriberUnregister in a close(); or weak listeners
Non-static inner class / lambda capturesAn inner instance holds Outer.this; lambdas hold captured variablesUse static nested classes; capture only what you need
ThreadLocal in thread poolsPool threads never die, so their thread-local values never go awaytry { … } finally { tl.remove(); }
Unclosed resourcesStreams, connections, native buffers hold memory and file handlestry-with-resources
Wrong equals/hashCode as map keyDuplicate keys pile up; mutated keys become unreachable-yet-presentImmutable keys with a correct contract
Java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class Main {
    static final List<Runnable> LISTENERS = new ArrayList<>();   // static → a GC root

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        for (int i = 0; i < 1_000; i++) {
            byte[] buffer = new byte[10_000];
            LISTENERS.add(() -> System.out.print(buffer.length));   // lambda captures buffer
        }
        // ~10 MB can never be freed: every buffer is reachable through LISTENERS
        System.out.println("listeners: " + LISTENERS.size());

        LISTENERS.clear();                 // unsubscribe → buffers become garbage
        System.out.println("listeners: " + LISTENERS.size());
    }
}
Output
listeners: 1000
listeners: 0
Java
public class Main {
    private static final ThreadLocal<StringBuilder> BUFFER =
            ThreadLocal.withInitial(StringBuilder::new);

    static String handle(String request) {
        try {
            StringBuilder sb = BUFFER.get();
            sb.setLength(0);
            return sb.append("handled ").append(request).toString();
        } finally {
            BUFFER.remove();          // pooled threads live forever — always clean up
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(handle("GET /"));
    }
}
Output
handled GET /
Finding a leak
1
Confirm
Heap usage after each full GC keeps climbing (GC logs, JFR, VisualVM / JConsole graphs).
2
Dump the heap
jcmd <pid> GC.heap_dump heap.hprof (or jmap -dump:live,format=b,file=heap.hprof <pid>, or -XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError).
3
Analyse
Open it in Eclipse MAT or VisualVM: look at the dominator tree and the path to GC roots of the biggest retained sets.
4
Fix & verify
Remove the unintended reference, then re-run and compare dumps.
Terminal
$ jcmd                                   # list running JVMs
$ jcmd <pid> GC.class_histogram | head   # which classes use the most memory
$ jcmd <pid> GC.heap_dump /tmp/heap.hprof
$ jstat -gcutil <pid> 1000               # GC utilisation every second
How can a garbage-collected language leak memory?

The GC only frees unreachable objects. If a static map, a listener list, a thread-local in a pooled thread or a long-lived object keeps a reference to data you no longer need, that data is reachable and stays forever.

SoftReference vs WeakReference?

A weakly reachable object is cleared at the next GC. A softly reachable object is kept until the JVM is short of memory, which makes soft references suitable for simple memory-sensitive caches (though a proper bounded cache is usually better).

Why can a non-static inner class cause a leak?

Every instance of a non-static inner class holds a hidden reference to its enclosing instance (Outer.this). If the inner object outlives the outer one (e.g. registered as a callback), the whole outer object is retained. Make it a static nested class.

JVM Internals & Memory

String & Memory Interview Traps

A collection of classic “what does this print?” questions about strings, the pool, boxing and constant folding — each verified.

== on strings
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String a = "java";
        String b = "java";                 // same pooled literal
        String c = new String("java");     // a new heap object
        String d = "ja" + "va";            // compile-time constant → folded to "java"
        String part = "ja";
        String e = part + "va";            // runtime concatenation → new object
        final String fpart = "ja";
        String f = fpart + "va";           // final + literal is a constant → folded

        System.out.println(a == b);
        System.out.println(a == c);
        System.out.println(a == d);
        System.out.println(a == e);
        System.out.println(a == f);
        System.out.println(a == e.intern());
        System.out.println(a.equals(c));
    }
}
Output
true
false
true
false
true
true
true
ExpressionPooled?Why
"java"YesEvery string literal is interned in the pool
new String("java")Nonew always creates a fresh heap object
"ja" + "va"YesConstant expression, folded by javac
part + "va" (non-final part)NoComputed at run time (StringConcatFactory / StringBuilder)
fpart + "va" (final with constant initialiser)Yesfpart is a constant variable, so the whole expression is constant
s.intern()YesReturns the pooled instance, adding it if absent
Key point

Rule: compare strings with equals (or Objects.equals for null-safety). == only tells you whether two references point at the same object.

How many objects does new String("hello") create?

Up to two: the literal "hello" is placed in the string pool when the class is loaded (if it isn't already there), and new creates one more String object on the heap. If the literal was already pooled, only one new object is created at run time.

Integer cache and boxing
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Integer a = 127, b = 127;
        Integer c = 128, d = 128;
        System.out.println(a == b);          // cached: -128..127
        System.out.println(c == d);          // two different objects
        System.out.println(c.equals(d));

        Long big = 127L;
        System.out.println(big.equals(127));  // Integer vs Long → false
        System.out.println(big == 127);       // unboxed numeric comparison → true

        Integer missing = null;
        try {
            int n = missing;                  // auto-unboxing null
            System.out.println(n);
        } catch (NullPointerException ex) {
            System.out.println("NPE on unboxing");
        }

        boolean flag = true;
        try {
            int r = flag ? missing : 0;       // ternary unboxes `missing`
            System.out.println(r);
        } catch (NullPointerException ex) {
            System.out.println("NPE in ternary");
        }
    }
}
Output
true
false
true
false
true
NPE on unboxing
NPE in ternary
Watch out

The cache covers Integer, Short, Long, Byte in −128…127, Character up to 127 and both Boolean values. The upper bound for Integer can be raised with -XX:AutoBoxCacheMax, so == on boxed numbers is never reliable.

Concatenation and char arithmetic
Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("a" + 1 + 2);      // left to right: ("a" + 1) + 2
        System.out.println(1 + 2 + "a");      // (1 + 2) + "a"
        System.out.println('a' + 'b');        // char + char → int
        System.out.println("" + 'a' + 'b');
        System.out.println((char) ('a' + 1));
        System.out.println(Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE));   // overflow: no positive counterpart
        System.out.println(0.1 + 0.2);
    }
}
Output
a12
3a
195
ab
b
-2147483648
0.30000000000000004
String + in a loop

Each s += x creates a brand-new string and copies everything so far — O(n²) for n appends. Inside loops use a StringBuilder (single-expression concatenations are fine: the compiler optimises them).

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
        for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
            sb.append(i).append(',');
        }
        sb.setLength(sb.length() - 1);            // drop the trailing comma
        System.out.println(sb);

        System.out.println(String.join(",", "a", "b", "c"));   // often simplest
    }
}
Output
0,1,2,3,4
a,b,c
Other traps

substring copies

Before Java 7u6, substring shared the parent's char[] — a tiny substring could pin a huge string in memory. Now it copies, so no leak (but O(k) cost).

Passwords in char[]

A String is immutable and may be pooled; it lingers in memory until GC and can show up in heap dumps. A char[] can be wiped with Arrays.fill(pw, '\0') right after use — that's why Console.readPassword() returns char[].

Compact strings (9+)

Strings that contain only Latin-1 characters store one byte per char instead of two (byte[] + a coder flag).

Strings are immutable

s.toUpperCase() returns a new string; calling it without assigning the result does nothing.

Java
public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String s = "hello";
        s.toUpperCase();                   // result discarded
        System.out.println(s);
        s = s.toUpperCase();
        System.out.println(s);
    }
}
Output
hello
HELLO
switch on strings and null

A switch on a String compiles to a switch on hashCode() followed by equals(). A null selector throws NullPointerException — unless (Java 21) you add case null.

Java
public class Main {
    static String classic(String cmd) {
        switch (cmd) {
            case "start": return "starting";
            case "stop":  return "stopping";
            default:      return "unknown";
        }
    }

    static String modern(String cmd) {
        return switch (cmd) {
            case null -> "no command";            // Java 21
            case "start" -> "starting";
            default -> "unknown";
        };
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(classic("stop"));
        try {
            classic(null);
        } catch (NullPointerException e) {
            System.out.println("NPE from classic switch");
        }
        System.out.println(modern(null));
    }
}
Output
stopping
NPE from classic switch
no command
Why is String immutable in Java?

(1) The string pool is only safe if pooled strings can't change. (2) Security: class names, file paths and URLs passed to APIs can't be altered after a check. (3) Thread safety for free. (4) The hash code can be cached, which makes strings fast HashMap keys.

What does intern() do, and should you use it?

It returns the canonical pooled copy of the string, adding it to the pool if needed, so equal interned strings are ==. It can save memory for many duplicate strings, but the pool lookup has a cost — modern G1 also offers -XX:+UseStringDeduplication, which deduplicates backing arrays automatically.

Design, Modern Java & Interview Prep

Design Patterns: Singleton, Builder & Factory

Creational patterns control how objects get made: exactly one (Singleton), step by step (Builder), or without the caller naming the concrete class (Factory).

Singleton

Singleton — a class with exactly one instance and a global access point to it. Private constructor + a static way to get the instance.

VariantLazy?Thread-safe?Notes
Eager static final fieldNo — created at class initYes (class init is thread-safe)Simplest; fine when creation is cheap
synchronized getInstance()YesYesLock on every call — slow
Double-checked lockingYesYes only with volatileVerbose; easy to get wrong
Holder idiom (static nested class)YesYesJVM class-loading guarantees; no locks
enum with one constantAt enum initYesRecommended (Effective Java): also safe against reflection and serialization
Java
public class Main {
    // 1. Eager
    static final class Eager {
        private static final Eager INSTANCE = new Eager();
        private Eager() {}
        static Eager getInstance() { return INSTANCE; }
    }

    // 2. Double-checked locking — volatile prevents seeing a half-constructed object
    static final class Dcl {
        private static volatile Dcl instance;
        private Dcl() {}
        static Dcl getInstance() {
            Dcl local = instance;
            if (local == null) {                       // 1st check, no lock
                synchronized (Dcl.class) {
                    local = instance;
                    if (local == null) {               // 2nd check, with lock
                        instance = local = new Dcl();
                    }
                }
            }
            return local;
        }
    }

    // 3. Holder idiom — Holder is initialised on first getInstance() call
    static final class Lazy {
        private Lazy() { System.out.println("Lazy created"); }
        private static final class Holder { static final Lazy INSTANCE = new Lazy(); }
        static Lazy getInstance() { return Holder.INSTANCE; }
    }

    // 4. Enum
    enum Config {
        INSTANCE;
        private int timeout = 30;
        int timeout() { return timeout; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(Eager.getInstance() == Eager.getInstance());
        System.out.println(Dcl.getInstance() == Dcl.getInstance());
        System.out.println("before first use");
        System.out.println(Lazy.getInstance() == Lazy.getInstance());
        System.out.println(Config.INSTANCE.timeout());
    }
}
Output
true
true
before first use
Lazy created
true
30
Double-checked locking

Without volatile, double-checked locking is broken: the write of the reference can be reordered before the constructor's field writes, so another thread may see a non-null but not fully initialised object.

Breaking a singleton — and the enum defence
Java
import java.lang.reflect.Constructor;

public class Main {
    static final class Classic {
        static final Classic INSTANCE = new Classic();
        private Classic() {}
    }

    enum Single { INSTANCE }

    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        Constructor<Classic> c = Classic.class.getDeclaredConstructor();
        c.setAccessible(true);                       // bypass `private`
        Classic second = c.newInstance();
        System.out.println("classic still single? " + (second == Classic.INSTANCE));

        Constructor<Single> e = Single.class.getDeclaredConstructor(String.class, int.class);
        e.setAccessible(true);
        try {
            e.newInstance("INSTANCE", 0);
        } catch (IllegalArgumentException ex) {
            System.out.println(ex.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
Output
classic still single? false
Cannot reflectively create enum objects
Java
import java.io.*;

public class Main {
    static final class Settings implements Serializable {
        private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;
        static final Settings INSTANCE = new Settings();
        private Settings() {}

        private Object readResolve() {       // deserialization returns this instead
            return INSTANCE;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        ByteArrayOutputStream bytes = new ByteArrayOutputStream();
        try (ObjectOutputStream out = new ObjectOutputStream(bytes)) {
            out.writeObject(Settings.INSTANCE);
        }
        try (ObjectInputStream in = new ObjectInputStream(new ByteArrayInputStream(bytes.toByteArray()))) {
            Object copy = in.readObject();
            System.out.println(copy == Settings.INSTANCE);   // false without readResolve
        }
    }
}
Output
true
Key point

Singletons are global state: they hide dependencies and make unit tests hard. In application code prefer one instance created at startup and passed in (dependency injection — what Spring beans are by default).

Builder

For classes with many parameters, especially optional ones. Replaces telescoping constructors and avoids the half-built objects of the JavaBeans setter style.

Java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class Main {
    static final class Pizza {
        private final String size;              // required
        private final boolean extraCheese;      // optional
        private final List<String> toppings;    // optional

        private Pizza(Builder b) {
            this.size = b.size;
            this.extraCheese = b.extraCheese;
            this.toppings = List.copyOf(b.toppings);
        }

        static Builder builder(String size) { return new Builder(size); }

        @Override
        public String toString() {
            return size + " pizza, cheese=" + extraCheese + ", toppings=" + toppings;
        }

        static final class Builder {
            private final String size;
            private boolean extraCheese;
            private final List<String> toppings = new ArrayList<>();

            private Builder(String size) { this.size = size; }

            Builder extraCheese() { this.extraCheese = true; return this; }
            Builder topping(String t) { toppings.add(t); return this; }

            Pizza build() {
                if (toppings.size() > 5) throw new IllegalStateException("too many toppings");
                return new Pizza(this);
            }
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Pizza p = Pizza.builder("large")
                .topping("olives")
                .topping("basil")
                .extraCheese()
                .build();
        System.out.println(p);
    }
}
Output
large pizza, cheese=true, toppings=[olives, basil]
StringBuilderHttpRequest.newBuilder()Stream.builder()Locale.BuilderLombok @Builder
Factories

Static factory method

A static method that returns an instance: List.of, Integer.valueOf, Optional.empty. Has a name, can cache (valueOf), can return a subtype.

Simple factory

One method picks the concrete class from a parameter — callers depend only on the interface.

Factory Method (GoF)

A superclass defines an abstract createX() and subclasses decide which class to instantiate.

Abstract Factory

An interface for creating families of related objects (e.g. a UI kit: createButton, createCheckbox).

Java
public class Main {
    sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Square {
        double area();

        static Shape of(String kind, double size) {        // simple / static factory
            return switch (kind) {
                case "circle" -> new Circle(size);
                case "square" -> new Square(size);
                default -> throw new IllegalArgumentException("unknown shape: " + kind);
            };
        }
    }

    record Circle(double r) implements Shape {
        public double area() { return Math.PI * r * r; }
    }

    record Square(double side) implements Shape {
        public double area() { return side * side; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Shape s = Shape.of("square", 3);
        System.out.println(s + " area=" + s.area());
        System.out.printf("%.2f%n", Shape.of("circle", 1).area());
        System.out.println(Integer.valueOf(100) == Integer.valueOf(100));   // valueOf caches
    }
}
Output
Square[side=3.0] area=9.0
3.14
true
Which singleton implementation would you use and why?

An enum with a single constant: it is thread-safe by class initialisation, serialization returns the same constant automatically, and reflection cannot create a second instance. If laziness matters and the class must extend something, use the holder idiom.

Why must the instance field be volatile in double-checked locking?

instance = new X() is allocate → initialise → publish; without volatile the publish can be reordered before initialisation, so a thread that skips the lock could see a non-null reference to an object whose fields are still defaults. volatile adds the happens-before edge that forbids this.

When would you choose a builder over constructors?

When there are more than a handful of parameters, several are optional, or several have the same type (easy to swap by mistake). The builder gives named, readable calls and still produces an immutable object validated in one place (build()).

Design, Modern Java & Interview Prep

Design Patterns: Strategy, Observer & More

Behavioural and structural patterns in modern Java — most of them are a lambda or a small interface away.

Strategy

Strategy — define a family of interchangeable algorithms behind one interface and choose one at run time. With functional interfaces, each strategy is just a lambda.

Java
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.function.DoubleUnaryOperator;

public class Main {
    static final Map<String, DoubleUnaryOperator> DISCOUNTS = Map.of(
            "none", price -> price,
            "festive", price -> price * 0.8,
            "flat50", price -> Math.max(0, price - 50));

    static double checkout(double price, String code) {
        return DISCOUNTS.getOrDefault(code, DISCOUNTS.get("none")).applyAsDouble(price);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(checkout(200, "festive"));
        System.out.println(checkout(200, "flat50"));
        System.out.println(checkout(200, "bogus"));
    }
}
Output
160.0
150.0
200.0

Comparator is the JDK's best-known strategy: list.sort(Comparator.comparing(Person::age)) plugs a comparison algorithm into a sort.

Observer

Observer — a subject keeps a list of listeners and notifies them of events; listeners don't know about each other. The basis of event buses, GUI listeners and reactive streams.

Java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.Consumer;

public class Main {
    static final class StockTicker {
        private final List<Consumer<Double>> listeners = new ArrayList<>();

        Runnable subscribe(Consumer<Double> l) {
            listeners.add(l);
            return () -> listeners.remove(l);          // handle to unsubscribe
        }

        void publish(double price) {
            listeners.forEach(l -> l.accept(price));
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        StockTicker ticker = new StockTicker();
        ticker.subscribe(p -> System.out.println("display: " + p));
        Runnable cancel = ticker.subscribe(p -> {
            if (p > 100) System.out.println("alert: above 100");
        });

        ticker.publish(99.5);
        ticker.publish(101.0);
        cancel.run();                                  // stop the alerts
        ticker.publish(120.0);
    }
}
Output
display: 99.5
display: 101.0
alert: above 100
display: 120.0
Watch out

Forgotten subscriptions are a classic memory leak — always give listeners a way to unsubscribe. java.util.Observable is deprecated (Java 9); use your own listener list, PropertyChangeSupport or java.util.concurrent.Flow.

Decorator

Wrap an object in another object with the same interface to add behaviour. java.io is built this way: each stream adds one feature (buffering, decoding, line reading).

Java
import java.io.BufferedReader;
import java.io.IOException;
import java.io.StringReader;

public class Main {
    interface Coffee { double cost(); String describe(); }

    record Espresso() implements Coffee {
        public double cost() { return 2.0; }
        public String describe() { return "espresso"; }
    }

    record WithMilk(Coffee inner) implements Coffee {
        public double cost() { return inner.cost() + 0.5; }
        public String describe() { return inner.describe() + " + milk"; }
    }

    record WithSyrup(Coffee inner) implements Coffee {
        public double cost() { return inner.cost() + 0.75; }
        public String describe() { return inner.describe() + " + syrup"; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {
        Coffee order = new WithSyrup(new WithMilk(new Espresso()));
        System.out.println(order.describe() + " = " + order.cost());

        // the JDK version: a Reader decorated with buffering and readLine()
        try (BufferedReader r = new BufferedReader(new StringReader("line 1\nline 2"))) {
            System.out.println(r.readLine());
        }
    }
}
Output
espresso + milk + syrup = 3.25
line 1
Template method

An abstract class fixes the skeleton of an algorithm in a final method and lets subclasses fill in the steps.

Java
public class Main {
    static abstract class Report {
        final String render() {                      // the template — not overridable
            return header() + "\n" + body() + "\n-- end --";
        }
        String header() { return "REPORT"; }         // hook with a default
        abstract String body();                      // step subclasses must supply
    }

    static final class SalesReport extends Report {
        @Override String body() { return "sales: 42 units"; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(new SalesReport().render());
    }
}
Output
REPORT
sales: 42 units
-- end --
Adapter

Convert one interface into another that a client expects. Arrays.asList adapts an array to List; InputStreamReader adapts a byte stream to a character Reader.

Java
import java.util.function.Supplier;

public class Main {
    static final class LegacyThermometer {                  // can't change this class
        double readFahrenheit() { return 98.6; }
    }

    interface CelsiusSensor { double celsius(); }

    static CelsiusSensor adapt(LegacyThermometer t) {     // the adapter
        return () -> (t.readFahrenheit() - 32) * 5 / 9;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        CelsiusSensor s = adapt(new LegacyThermometer());
        Supplier<String> fmt = () -> String.format("%.1f C", s.celsius());
        System.out.println(fmt.get());
    }
}
Output
37.0 C
Where the JDK uses them
PatternKindIn the JDK
StrategyBehaviouralComparator, ThreadPoolExecutor's RejectedExecutionHandler
ObserverBehaviouralPropertyChangeListener, Swing listeners, Flow.Subscriber
IteratorBehaviouralIterator / Iterable behind every for-each loop
Template methodBehaviouralAbstractList (get + size → everything else), InputStream.read(byte[])
CommandBehaviouralRunnable, Callable submitted to executors
DecoratorStructuralBufferedInputStream, Collections.unmodifiableList, synchronizedMap
AdapterStructuralArrays.asList, InputStreamReader, OutputStreamWriter
ProxyStructuraljava.lang.reflect.Proxy (Spring AOP, mocks)
Factory / static factoryCreationalList.of, Calendar.getInstance, NumberFormat.getInstance
BuilderCreationalStringBuilder, HttpClient.newBuilder()
SingletonCreationalRuntime.getRuntime()
Strategy vs template method?

Both vary part of an algorithm. Template method uses inheritance — subclasses override steps of a fixed skeleton. Strategy uses composition — the varying algorithm is an object (often a lambda) passed in, so it can change at run time and needs no subclass.

Decorator vs inheritance for adding behaviour?

Inheritance fixes the combination at compile time and explodes into subclasses for every combination (MilkSyrupCoffee…). Decorators compose at run time: wrap in any order and any number, each class adding one responsibility.

Design, Modern Java & Interview Prep

Modern Java: 8 → 21 Feature Tour

What changed from Java 8 to Java 21 — the features interviewers expect you to know and modern codebases actually use.

LTS

Long-term-support releases: 8, 11, 17, 21 (and 25). Since Java 10 a feature release ships every six months; preview features need --enable-preview.

Feature timeline
VersionHeadline features
8 (2014, LTS)Lambdas, method references, Streams, Optional, java.time, default & static interface methods, CompletableFuture
9Module system (JPMS), List.of / Set.of / Map.of, JShell, private interface methods, Stream.takeWhile, compact strings
10var for local variables, List.copyOf, Collectors.toUnmodifiableList
11 (LTS)String.isBlank/strip/repeat/lines, Files.readString/writeString, standard HttpClient, single-file launch java App.java, var in lambda params
12–13Collectors.teeing, String.indent/transform; switch expressions & text blocks in preview
14Switch expressions (->, yield), helpful NullPointerException messages
15Text blocks """, String.formatted, ZGC production-ready
16Records, pattern matching for instanceof, Stream.toList()
17 (LTS)Sealed classes, strong encapsulation of JDK internals, RandomGenerator
18–20UTF-8 by default, simple web server jwebserver; previews of virtual threads, record patterns
21 (LTS)Virtual threads, pattern matching for switch, record patterns, sequenced collections (getFirst, reversed), generational ZGC
Many features, one program
Java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class Main {
    sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Rect {}             // 17: sealed
    record Circle(double r) implements Shape {}                 // 16: records
    record Rect(double w, double h) implements Shape {}

    static String describe(Object o) {
        return switch (o) {                                     // 21: pattern switch
            case Circle(double r) when r > 10 -> "big circle";  // 21: record pattern + guard
            case Circle c -> "circle r=" + c.r();
            case Rect(double w, double h) -> "rect " + (w * h);
            case String s -> "text of length " + s.length();
            case null -> "null!";
            default -> "something else";
        };
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var shapes = List.of(new Circle(2), new Rect(3, 4), new Circle(20));   // 10: var, 9: List.of
        shapes.forEach(s -> System.out.println(describe(s)));                 // 8: lambdas
        System.out.println(describe("hi") + ", " + describe(null));

        Object o = shapes.get(1);
        if (o instanceof Rect r && r.w() < r.h()) {                             // 16: instanceof pattern
            System.out.println("tall " + r);
        }

        String json = """
                {"name": "%s", "ok": %b}""".formatted("java", true);          // 15: text block + formatted
        System.out.println(json);

        System.out.println("  hi  ".strip() + "|" + "ab".repeat(3) + "|" + "   ".isBlank());   // 11

        var names = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a", "b", "c"));
        System.out.println(names.getFirst() + names.getLast() + " " + names.reversed());    // 21: sequenced

        List<String> kinds = shapes.stream().map(s -> s.getClass().getSimpleName()).toList();   // 16: toList()
        System.out.println(kinds);
    }
}
Output
circle r=2.0
rect 12.0
big circle
text of length 2, null!
tall Rect[w=3.0, h=4.0]
{"name": "java", "ok": true}
hi|ababab|true
ac [c, b, a]
[Circle, Rect, Circle]
Helpful NullPointerExceptions (14+)

public class Main {
    record Address(String city) {}
    record User(Address address) {}

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        User u = new User(null);
        try {
            System.out.println(u.address().city().length());
        } catch (NullPointerException e) {
            System.out.println(e.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
